### [75] [SIVA-RL: Sensitivity-Invariance Visual Alignment for Multimodal Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13931)
*Cheng Tang,Junzhi Ning,Min Cen,Wei Li,Xinyi Zeng,Pinxian Zeng,Rongbin Li,Qiming Zhu,Yuqiang Li,Junjun He,Yirong Chen,Ming Hu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SIVA-RL replaces intervention-type-based supervision with outcome-conditioned supervision for RLVR in multimodal reasoning, using token-aligned PatchSwap and a frozen audit policy to route samples to sensitivity or invariance alignment based on observed reward drop, achieving consistent gains across 9 benchmarks on 3B/7B models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing visual-intervention methods assign supervision by the type of intervention (e.g., operator identity) rather than its observed effect, which fails because identical operators produce heterogeneous outcomes across samples.

Method: SIVA-RL constructs localized interventions via token-aligned, distance-constrained within-image PatchSwap. A frozen audit policy scores each clean-intervention pair; observed reward drops serve as soft routing weights: large-drop pairs drive sensitivity alignment, low-drop pairs drive clean-anchored invariance alignment, ambiguous pairs are down-weighted. Compatible with GRPO and DAPO backbones.

Result: Across 9 multimodal reasoning benchmarks, SIVA-RL improves 3B and 7B models over matched RL baselines in every setting: 8.79 percentage-point gain on vision-dependent reasoning, up to 14.9% relative overall improvement across all four GRPO- and DAPO-based configurations.

Conclusion: Decoupling intervention construction from supervision assignment via outcome-conditioned routing is effective; SIVA-RL consistently outperforms operator-conditioned regularization across diverse multimodal reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) drives multimodal reasoning, but answer-level correctness does not guarantee that a vision-language model grounds its predictions in visual evidence. Existing visual-intervention methods contrast policy behavior on original and modified images, yet assign supervision by the type of intervention rather than its observed effect. This assumption fails: identical operators produce heterogeneous outcomes across samples. We propose SIVA-RL, a Sensitivity-Invariance Visual Alignment framework that replaces operator-conditioned regularization with sample-wise, outcome-conditioned supervision. SIVA-RL constructs localized interventions through token-aligned, distance-constrained within-image PatchSwap. A frozen audit policy then scores each clean-intervention pair, and the observed reward drop becomes soft routing weights. Large-drop pairs drive sensitivity alignment, low-drop pairs drive clean-anchored invariance alignment, and ambiguous pairs are down-weighted. This design decouples intervention construction from supervision assignment and is compatible with both GRPO and DAPO backbones. Across nine multimodal reasoning benchmarks spanning mathematical, logical, and vision-dependent tasks, SIVA-RL improves 3B and 7B models over matched RL baselines in every setting. It yields an 8.79 percentage-point gain on vision-dependent reasoning and up to 14.9% relative overall improvement across all four GRPO- and DAPO-based configurations.

</details>


### [76] [A novel unsupervised machine learning strategy to handle multimodal cardiac PET/MRI data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13936)
*Brunnhilde Ponsi,Thomas Carlier,Lara Marteau,Aurélien Monnet,Thomas Eugène,Jean-Michel Serfaty,Nicolas Piriou,Hatem Necib*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study develops a PET/MRI multiparametric clustering approach (z-scoring, supervoxels, spectral clustering) to generate automated health reports for arrhythmogenic left ventricular cardiomyopathy, achieving balanced accuracy of 0.76 on 99 patients and ≥0.8 on 167 phantoms against cardiac physician assessments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Arrhythmogenic left ventricular cardiomyopathy lacks gold standard diagnostic criteria; simultaneous PET/MRI with multiparametric analysis could help identify phenotype and progression profiles.

Method: Two-step clustering applied to T1/T2 maps, LGE, and 18F-FDG-PET from 99 patients; each image z-scored and summed into a single volume, clustered into supervoxels, then 32 inter-patient groups obtained via spectral clustering. An "abnormality" score per cluster/modality enabled automated textual and bullseye reports, validated via nested cross-validation and 167 numerical phantoms.

Result: Balanced accuracy = 0.76 ± 0.04 in repeated nested cross-validation on patients; BA ≥ 0.8 on phantoms. Abnormal clusters matched visual observations for fibrosis/inflammation.

Conclusion: The clustering-based approach enables systematic handling of multimodal PET/MRI data to characterize myocardial heterogeneity in arrhythmogenic left ventricular cardiomyopathy.

Abstract: Arrhythmogenic left ventricular cardiomyopathy is a genetic myocardial disease difficult to diagnose due to the lack of gold standard criteria. Simultaneous PET/MR imaging, combined with multiparametric quantitative analysis, could facilitate the identification of different profiles related to the phenotype and progression of cardiomyopathy. This preliminary study focuses on a methodological strategy for dealing with PET/MRI data, including inter-patient data linkage and regional analysis. Two-step clustering was applied to T1 and T2 maps, LGE, and 18F-FDG-PET images of 99 patients genetically diagnosed with arrhythmogenic left ventricular cardiomyopathy. Each patient's images were independently z-scored and summed into a single volume, which was clustered into supervoxels. Thirty-two inter-patient groups of supervoxels were obtained by spectral clustering. An "abnormality" score was assigned to each cluster and modality, and used to visualise abnormal regions likely associated with disease. They enabled the generation of automated textual and bullseye health reports for each patient, which were compared with cardiac imager assessments using balanced accuracy in repeated nested cross-validation. This approach was further validated on a larger cohort of 167 numerical phantoms. The reports generated by clustering accurately identified most of the cardiac physicians' observations (BA = 0.76 $\pm$ 0.04 in repeated nested cross-validation on patients, and BA $\ge$ 0.8 on phantoms). Furthermore, the identified abnormal clusters closely matched their visual observations, facilitating the identification of varying degrees of fibrosis or inflammation on the images. This approach enables a more systematic handling of multimodal PET/MRI data to characterise myocardial heterogeneity in arrhythmogenic left ventricular cardiomyopathy patients.

</details>


### [77] [Peak-End-Net: A Peak-End Rule Inspired Framework for Generalizable Video Aesthetic Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13941)
*Geng Li,Haiwen Li,Rui Chen,Jing Tang,Lei Sun,Xiangxiang Chu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Peak-End-Net is a lightweight, interpretable VAA framework inspired by the peak-end rule from psychology. It uses pretrained IAA knowledge for frame-wise aesthetic priors, an aesthetic rhythm encoder for temporal progression, and a dynamic gated fusion mechanism for robust assessment. It achieves SOTA on VADB and DIVIDE-3K with minimal trainable parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VAA is underexplored due to lack of large-scale benchmarks and the inherent subjectivity of aesthetic judgment. The paper aims to address these by grounding VAA in psychological principles (peak-end rule) to better model human aesthetic perception.

Method: 1) Transfers knowledge from IAA via a pretrained IAA head to produce frame-wise aesthetic priors. 2) Uses peak-end rule-based temporal aggregation to identify salient moments and ending. 3) Designs an aesthetic rhythm encoder to model temporal evolution. 4) Employs a dynamic gated fusion mechanism for robustness under distribution shift. Built on frozen ViT with few trainable parameters.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on VADB (in-domain) and DIVIDE-3K (cross-domain) benchmarks.

Conclusion: Psychologically grounded modeling (peak-end rule) is effective for VAA, and the proposed method is both parameter-efficient and scalable.

Abstract: Video aesthetic assessment (VAA) aims to predict how aesthetically pleasing a video is, yet remains far less explored than other visual assessment tasks. Its progress is hindered not only by the scarcity of large-scale benchmarks, but also by the intrinsic subjectivity of aesthetic judgment, which is shaped by human perception. In this paper, we revisit VAA from a psychological perspective and propose \textit{Peak-End-Net}, a lightweight and interpretable framework inspired by the \textit{peak-end rule}, which suggests that people tend to judge a temporal experience mainly according to its salient moments and the ending. Building on this intuition, we first transfer knowledge from image aesthetic assessment (IAA) to VAA by introducing a pretrained IAA head to produce frame-wise aesthetic priors, which serve as surrogate signals for identifying aesthetically salient moments and guiding \textit{peak-end rule}-based temporal aggregation. To further capture how a video evolves aesthetically over time, we design an aesthetic rhythm encoder that models temporal progression beyond isolated moments. Additionally, we refine the overall assessment through a dynamic gated fusion mechanism to improve robustness under distribution shift. Our method is built on a frozen vision transformer (ViT) and requires only a small number of trainable parameters, making it scalable and parameter-efficient. Extensive experiments on two existing VAA benchmarks, including in-domain evaluation on VADB and cross-domain testing on DIVIDE-3K, demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance, affirming the value of psychologically grounded modeling for VAA. Our code and models are available at https://github.com/AMAP-ML/Peak-End-Net.

</details>


### [78] [PlumeQuant: Uncertainty-aware consistency assessment of methane plume masks and emission-rate estimates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13945)
*Parisa Masnadi Khiabani,Wolfgang Jentner,Alireza Rangrazjeddi,Michael C. Wimberly,Binbin Weng,David Ebert,Charles Nicholson*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that published methane plume products (mask, IME, plume length, emission rate) do not uniquely constrain plume boundaries, revealing equifinality via genetic-algorithm ensembles. It introduces PlumeQuant to recompute diagnostics and a Carbon Mapper-like mask generator that achieves high consistency with reference products, identifying weak/ambiguous plumes for expert review.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To expose the equifinality (different plausible masks yielding same published scalar quantities) in methane plume products from imaging spectrometers, and to provide diagnostic tools for consistency assessment and expert review flagging.

Method: Analysis of 63 EMIT-derived Carbon Mapper plume records (27 scenes) using genetic-algorithm (GA) ensembles conditioned on published IME and plume length to explore alternative plausible masks. Introduction of PlumeQuant, which recomputes IME, plume length, emission rate, and five-term uncertainty from distributed product components under stated conventions, evaluating four mask representations: reference mask, CM-like mask (generated without reference or published quantities, fixed on 44-plume development split), GA ensemble, and expert edits.

Result: Published scalar quantities do not uniquely constrain plume boundaries; the high-confidence core selected by target-consistent masks covers median 13% of plausible footprint envelope, with ambiguity largest for weak, low-overlap plumes. CM-like mask reproduced published IME with +0.72% median diff, emission rate with +0.16% (6.98% mean absolute), median IoU 0.843 against reference, uncertainty scale ratio 1.01. Holdout MAE: 7.6% (IME), 9.5% (length), 6.1% (rate).

Conclusion: The equifinality in methane plume products is significant and should be explicitly acknowledged. PlumeQuant and the CM-like mask generator provide product-level consistency diagnostics that effectively flag weak, offset, or ambiguous plumes for expert review, but do not constitute independent validation.

Abstract: Imaging spectrometers increasingly distribute source-resolved methane plume products in which the plume mask, integrated mass enhancement (IME), plume length, emission rate, and uncertainty are physically and algorithmically linked. Using 63 EMIT-derived Carbon Mapper plume records from 27 scenes, we show that these published scalar quantities do not uniquely constrain the plume boundary: substantially different yet plausible masks reproduce the same IME, plume length, and emission rate. Genetic-algorithm (GA) ensembles conditioned on the published IME and plume length make this equifinality explicit: the high-confidence core selected by nearly all target-consistent masks covers a median of 13% of the plausible footprint envelope, and ambiguity is largest for weak, low-overlap plumes. The diagnostics come from PlumeQuant, which recomputes IME, plume length, emission rate, and five-term uncertainty from distributed product components under stated conventions and evaluates four mask representations: the distributed reference mask, a transparent Carbon Mapper-informed analogue (CM-like), the GA ensemble, and optional expert edits. The CM-like mask is generated per plume without access to the reference mask or published quantities, with settings fixed once on a scene-disjoint 44-plume development split. It reproduced published IME with +0.72% median difference and emission rate with +0.16% (6.98% mean absolute), reached 0.843 median intersection-over-union against the reference masks, and matched the published uncertainty scale (median ratio 1.01). Holdout mean absolute errors were 7.6% (IME), 9.5% (length), and 6.1% (rate). These are product-level consistency diagnostics, not independent validation. They flag weak, offset, or ambiguous plumes for expert review.

</details>


### [79] [Music-to-Dance Generation via Atomic Movements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13978)
*Xinhao Cai,Yixuan Sun,Minghang Zheng,Qingchao Chen,Xin Jin,Song-chun Zhu,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A structure-aware framework for music-driven dance generation that models choreography as sequences of atomic movements, using LLMs to construct a semantic movement vocabulary, followed by a two-stage generation pipeline (planning + completion) that produces structurally coherent, rhythmically aligned, and controllable dances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural dance generation methods treat motion as a continuous signal, ignoring its compositional nature, which leads to structurally incoherent dances that are difficult to control or edit.

Method: (1) Build atomic movement vocabulary by segmenting large-scale dance data and clustering into atomic groups, then use LLMs to semantically relabel and refine clusters. (2) Two-stage generation: first, an atomic movement planning stage predicts type/duration/timing conditioned on music as symbolic dance allocation; second, a transition-aware generator synthesizes smooth motion conditioned on the planned structure.

Result: The method produces dances with significantly improved structural coherence, rhythmic alignment, and perceptual naturalness compared to existing baselines, while offering enhanced interpretability and controllable editing through explicit structural representation.

Conclusion: Modeling dance as compositional atomic movements with explicit structural planning enables more controllable, interpretable, and structurally coherent music-driven dance generation.

Abstract: Music-driven dance generation aims to produce human motion that is both rhythmically synchronized and semantically consistent with music. While recent neural approaches have achieved impressive visual realism, they typically model motion as a continuous signal and neglect its compositional nature, making generated dances structurally incoherent and difficult to control. In this work, we introduce a structure-aware framework that models choreography as a sequence of atomic movements-semantically interpretable motion events that serve as the building blocks of dance. To construct this atomic movement vocabulary, we first segment large-scale dance data and cluster them into atomic movement groups. We then employ a large language model to semantically relabel and refine the clusters, yielding a set of interpretable and reusable atomic movements. Based on these atomic movement annotations, we design a two-stage generation framework that mirrors the human choreography process. In the atomic movement planning stage, the model predicts the type, duration, and timing of atomic movements conditioned on the input music, forming a symbolic dance allocation. In the completion stage, a transition-aware generator synthesizes smooth and stylistically coherent motion conditioned on the planned structure. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method produces dances with significantly improved structural coherence, rhythmic alignment, and perceptual naturalness compared to existing baselines, while providing enhanced interpretability and controllable editing through explicit structural representation.

</details>


### [80] [CF-Net: Conflict Fusion with Speaker Normalisation and Certainty Weighting for Ambivalence/Hesitancy Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13976)
*Tung Hung Bui,Hong Hai Nguyen,Van Thong Huynh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CF-Net is a deep multimodal network for detecting ambivalence and hesitancy in video by explicitly modeling cross-modal incongruence, achieving state-of-the-art results on the BAH benchmark (Macro F1 0.7364 on private test set).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ambivalence and hesitancy (AH) detection in unconstrained video is challenging because the signal is inherently ambiguous and expressed through subtle cross-modal incongruence rather than prototypical affect, requiring specialized modeling beyond standard emotion recognition.

Method: CF-Net encodes visual (SigLIP2), audio (HuBERT), and transcript (DistilBERT) streams with frozen backbones, normalizes features per speaker to reduce identity leakage, fuses modalities via a ConflictFusion module that explicitly computes pairwise cross-modal incongruence, and trains with certainty-weighted focal loss, manifold mixup, modality dropout, and an auxiliary certainty-regression head.

Result: Macro F1 of 0.7155 on BAH validation set and 0.7364 (AP = 0.7492) on the private challenge test set.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling cross-modal incongruence via ConflictFusion, combined with regularization strategies, effectively captures ambivalence and hesitancy from multimodal video data, achieving competitive performance on the ABAW 2026 BAH benchmark.

Abstract: Detecting ambivalence and hesitancy (AH) in unconstrained video is challenging because the target signal is inherently ambiguous and expressed through subtle cross-modal incongruence rather than prototypical affect. We present CF-Net, a deep multimodal network submitted to the 3rd Edition of the AH Video Recognition Challenge (ABAW 11th, ECCV 2026), targeting the BAH dataset. CF-Net encodes visual, audio, and transcript streams with frozen SigLIP2, HuBERT, and DistilBERT backbones, normalises backbone features per speaker to reduce identity leakage, and fuses them via a ConflictFusion module that explicitly computes pairwise cross-modal incongruence. Training combines certainty-weighted focal loss, manifold mixup, and modality dropout; an auxiliary certainty-regression head leverages ambiguity annotations to stabilise learning on genuinely borderline samples. CF-Net achieves a Macro F1 of 0.7155 on the BAH validation set and 0.7364 (AP = 0.7492) on the private challenge test set.

</details>


### [81] [Screening Is Effective for Visual Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13983)
*Shunya Shimomura,Kazuhiro Hotta*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VisionScreen extends the Screening mechanism (explicitly excluding low-relevance tokens via thresholding) from language modeling to visual recognition, enabling each image patch to selectively aggregate only content-wise and spatially relevant patches without softmax-based competition, outperforming conventional ViT on image classification benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard ViT's self-attention uses softmax-normalized relative weights, making it unable to explicitly reject irrelevant/background patches, which introduces unnecessary information into feature aggregation.

Method: Proposes VisionScreen, which extends Screening mechanism to 2D spatial domain by treating image patches as grid-arranged tokens and estimating absolute relevance based on query-key similarity with thresholding to exclude low-relevance patches.

Result: Outperforms conventional ViT on image classification benchmarks.

Conclusion: Screening can be effective for visual recognition, offering an alternative to relative feature aggregation based on softmax attention.

Abstract: Vision Transformer (ViT) has been widely used as a powerful framework for modeling global dependencies among image patches. However, its core component, self-attention assigns softmax-normalized relative weights to all patches, making it difficult to evaluate the relevance between patches independently. In visual recognition, images often contain many background or redundant patches, yet self-attention cannot explicitly reject such irrelevant patches, which may introduce unnecessary information into feature aggregation. To address this limitation, Screening has been proposed in the field of language modeling, where the relevance of each token is independently evaluated based on query-key similarity and low-relevance tokens are explicitly excluded through thresholding. In this work, we propose VisionScreen, a new vision model that extends Screening mechanism to visual recognition. VisionScreen treats image patches as tokens arranged on a two-dimensional grid and extends absolute relevance estimation based on query-key similarity to the two-dimensional spatial domain. This allows each patch to selectively aggregate only content-wise and spatially relevant patches without relying on competition among patches. Experiments on image classification benchmarks demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms conventional ViT. These results suggest that Screening can be effective for visual recognition, offering an alternative to relative feature aggregation based on softmax attention.

</details>


### [82] [Task-Specific Feature Fusion Method for Multi-Task Affective Behavior Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13986)
*Jiajun Sun,Zhe Gao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a task-adaptive feature fusion approach for multi-task affective behavior analysis (ABAW11), using two frozen pretrained backbones (DINOv2 ViT-L and DINOv3 ConvNeXt-base) with task-specific prediction heads, fusion, and calibration strategies, achieving a validation score of 1.6341 (EXPR macro-F1: 0.4222, AU macro-F1: 0.5402, mean VA CCC: 0.6717).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although valence-arousal, categorical expressions, and facial action units are naturally related, they benefit from different visual features, temporal processing, fusion mechanisms, and calibration procedures, motivating the need for task-adaptive rather than shared architectures.

Method: Two pretrained backbones (DINOv2 ViT-L and DINOv3 ConvNeXt-base) are adapted on an external expression-oriented facial dataset and frozen to extract complementary frame-level features. Various heads (frame-level, temporal CNN, post-hoc smoothing, LightGBM), fusion strategies (concatenation, gated, residual, late logit), calibration (threshold calibration), and shared MTL structures are systematically compared, with the final system selecting task-specific combinations.

Result: Validation set results: EXPR macro-F1 = 0.4222, AU macro-F1 = 0.5402, mean VA CCC = 0.6717, overall validation score = 1.6341.

Conclusion: Task-adaptive fusion of frozen visual features is a simple and effective strategy for ABAW-style multi-task affective behavior analysis.

Abstract: The 11th Affective Behavior Analysis in-the-wild (ABAW11) Multi-Task Learning Challenge requires a unified system to predict valence-arousal, categorical expressions, and facial action units from the official s-Aff-Wild2 images. Although these tasks are naturally related through facial behavior, our validation experiments show that they benefit from different visual features, temporal processing strategies, fusion mechanisms, and calibration procedures. In this paper, we study task-adaptive feature fusion for ABAW11 multi-task affective behavior analysis. We first adapt two pretrained visual backbones, DINOv2 ViT-L and DINOv3 ConvNeXt-base, on an external expression-oriented facial image set and then freeze them to extract complementary frame-level features from the official ABAW11 data. On top of these frozen features, we systematically compare frame-level prediction heads, temporal convolutional heads, post-hoc temporal smoothing, LightGBM models, feature concatenation, gated fusion, residual fusion, late logit fusion, threshold calibration, and shared MTL structures. The final system selects task-specific fusion and prediction strategies rather than forcing all tasks to share a single architecture. On the ABAW11 validation set, the selected system achieves an EXPR macro-F1 of 0.4222, an AU macro-F1 of 0.5402, and a mean VA CCC of 0.6717, resulting in an overall validation score of 1.6341. The results suggest that task-adaptive fusion of frozen visual features is a simple and effective strategy for ABAW-style multi-task affective behavior analysis.

</details>


### [83] [Multi-Expert Routing for Multi-Domain Low-Resource OCR: A Manchu Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14041)
*Zhan Chen,Jiqiao Ma,Chih-wen Kuo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A multi-expert OCR system for Historical Manchu uses a lightweight page-level classifier to route documents to appropriate domain specialists (reused from iterative fine-tuning), achieving high accuracy across diverse scripts with minimal additional training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Historical Manchu OCR must handle diverse visual writing styles (regular script, running script, chancery hand in palace memorials) with limited labeled data, motivating a multi-expert routing approach to avoid training separate models for each domain.

Method: A multi-expert system reuses checkpoints from iterative fine-tuning as domain specialists and uses a lightweight page-level image classifier to dispatch pages by visual style. If the checkpoint pool lacks a suitable specialist, an additional expert is trained for that domain.

Result: Routed system matches selected specialist precision at two decimals: 0.30% CER (regular script), 1.57% (memorials), 4.83% (running script). Router achieves 99.3% page-level domain accuracy and matches domain-label oracle precision.

Conclusion: The routing approach is effective and reproducible, with two of three specialists not originally trained for their final domain, demonstrating domain reuse from iterative fine-tuning.

Abstract: Historical Manchu OCR must accommodate various visually distinct writing styles, including regular script, running script, and the semi-cursive chancery hand used in palace memorials, despite limited labeled data. We study a multi-expert system that reuses checkpoints from an iterative fine-tuning process as domain specialists and uses a lightweight page-level image classifier to dispatch pages by visual style. When the checkpoint pool lacks a suitable specialist, we train an additional expert for that domain. On three frozen test sets, the routed system matches the selected specialist for each style at two-decimal precision: 0.30 percent CER on regular script, 1.57 percent on memorials, and 4.83 percent on running script. The router achieves 99.3 percent page-level domain accuracy and matches the domain-label oracle at the same precision. Two of the three selected specialists were not trained specifically for their final domain; only the running-script expert was trained with that domain as its target. We report the evaluation protocol, router design, and per-page predictions to make the comparison reproducible.

</details>


### [84] [M$^\text{4}$World: A Multi-view Multimodal Driving World Model for Interactive Object Manipulation and Minute-long Streaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14005)
*Ke Cheng,Hanqiao Ye,Lei Shi,Yahui Liu,Yunhan Shen,Jingtao Dong,Zhenke Wang,Wenxuan Ao,Weixiang Xu,Kaining Huang,Shuhan Shen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: M⁴World is a generative driving world model that produces multi-view surround videos and synchronized LiDAR scans with interactive object manipulation and stable minute-long streaming, using a flexible conditioning interface and multi-stage training for efficient long-horizon generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Driving-world generation is essential for scalable autonomous-driving simulation, but existing methods lack object-level controllability and long-horizon stability.

Method: M⁴World uses (1) a flexible conditioning interface for controlling spatial layout and visual appearance of objects, (2) a multi-stage training framework enabling online causal generation in only four denoising steps for stable minute-long streaming, (3) few-clip post-training and visual reference-conditioned models for long-tail customization, and (4) an automated VLM-based judging pipeline for evaluating controllability.

Result: M⁴World achieves high generation quality, precise controllability, and stable minute-long streaming, validated by comprehensive experiments and downstream applications in long-tail augmentation and scene editing.

Conclusion: M⁴World demonstrates strong potential for controllable, scalable driving simulation through its combination of interactivity, long-horizon stability, and rare-case customization.

Abstract: Driving-world generation has emerged as a core capability for scalable autonomous-driving simulation, yet existing methods remain limited in object-level controllability and long-horizon stability. We present M$^\text{4}$World, a Multi-view and Multimodal generative driving world model that synthesizes future surround-view video streams and synchronized LiDAR scans while supporting interactive object Manipulation and stable Minute-long streaming. Fine-grained object manipulation is realized through a flexible conditioning interface that supports explicit control over both the spatial layout and visual appearance of individual objects. Stable minute-long streaming, on the other hand, is achieved through a multi-stage training framework that enables online causal generation in only four denoising steps while maintaining coherent world dynamics throughout extended rollouts. Building on these components, we introduce an efficient few-clip post-training as well as a suite of visual reference-conditioned generation models, preserving general generation ability while allowing rare-case customization for long-tail controllability. To assess controllability beyond realism, we further introduce an automated VLM-based judging pipeline that evaluates scene-level condition adherence, view-wise object controllability, and cross-view object consistency. Comprehensive experiments show that M$^\text{4}$World consistently delivers high generation quality, precise controllability, and stable minute-long streaming. Together with downstream long-tail augmentation and scene editing, these results demonstrate the potential of M$^\text{4}$World for controllable, scalable driving simulation.

</details>


### [85] [From Pixels to States: Rethinking Interactive World Models as Game Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14076)
*Zhen Li,Zian Meng,Shuwei Shi,Mingliang Zhai,Jiaming Tan,Chuanhao Li,Kaipeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper examines interactive game world modeling through a recurrent action-state-observation loop framework, analyzing four key dimensions (player action control, game state dynamics, state-observation persistence, real-time generation) and introduces a 90+ hour gameplay dataset from Black Myth: Wukong with aligned actions, states, and observations for state-aware modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building interactive worlds that respond coherently to player actions is a shared goal of graphics, games, and AI; recent video generative models are seen as potential next-gen game engines but lack rule-following interaction outcomes, long-horizon persistence, and real-time operation that conventional game engines provide.

Method: The paper takes the conventional action-state-observation game loop as an organizing lens and examines interactive world modeling along four dimensions: player action control, game state dynamics, state-observation persistence, and real-time interactive generation. For each dimension, required capabilities are identified, approaches are grouped into families, and trade-offs are discussed. A data engine for Black Myth: Wukong collects 90+ hours of gameplay with frame-aligned actions, ground-truth states, and visual observations plus structured/semantic annotations.

Result: Taxonomy of approaches across four dimensions with identified trade-offs, plus a large-scale dataset (90+ hours, frame-aligned) from Black Myth: Wukong for state-aware game world modeling research.

Conclusion: Provides a structured picture of where the field stands in interactive game world modeling, offering a framework for understanding capabilities and trade-offs, and contributes a valuable resource (dataset) to drive progress toward genuinely interactive game worlds.

Abstract: Building interactive worlds that respond coherently to player actions has long been a shared goal of computer graphics, games, and artificial intelligence. Recent video generative models provide a data-driven route toward this goal by predicting future observations conditioned on user actions, and are increasingly regarded as potential next-generation game engines. Realizing a genuinely interactive game world, however, requires interaction outcomes that follow rules over evolving game conditions, consequences that persist over long horizons, and a generation loop that operates in real time. Conventional game engines realize these properties through a recurrent action-state-observation loop, in which player actions update an explicit game state according to predefined rules and observations are rendered from the resulting state. Taking this loop as an organizing lens, this paper examines interactive game world modeling along four dimensions: player action control, game state dynamics, state-observation persistence, and real-time interactive generation. For each dimension, we start from the capabilities required by an interactive game world, group existing approaches into representative families, and discuss the strengths and trade-offs of each family. Complementing this analysis, we present a scalable data engine for Black Myth: Wukong that collects over 90 hours of gameplay with frame-aligned player actions, ground-truth game states, and visual observations, together with structured and semantic annotations, as a resource for state-aware game world modeling. We hope this paper offers a clear picture of where the field stands and fosters progress toward interactive game worlds.

</details>


### [86] [VideoRAE: Taming Video Foundation Models for Generative Modeling via Representation Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14088)
*Zhihao Xie,Junfeng Wu,Xinting Hu,Junchao Huang,Li Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VideoRAE is a representation autoencoder that leverages frozen Video Foundation Model features to produce compact, reconstruction-capable video latents (continuous or discrete), achieving state-of-the-art reconstruction and 5x faster convergence in video generation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional 3D-VAEs are optimized for pixel-level reconstruction, limiting semantic and spatio-temporal structure in latents. The authors explore whether frozen Video Foundation Models (VFMs) can be repurposed to produce compact, generation-friendly video latents.

Method: VideoRAE uses a frozen video foundation encoder (V-JEPA 2/VideoMAEv2) to extract multi-scale hierarchical features, compresses them via a lightweight 1D self-attention projector, supports continuous latents (DiT) and discrete tokens (AR models) via multi-codebook high-dimensional quantization, and employs local-and-global representation alignment with the frozen VFM teacher without KL regularization.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art class-to-video gFVDs of 40 (AR) and 93 (DiT) on UCF-101, converges ~5x faster than competing autoencoder baselines, and in a 2B-scale text-to-video study, replacing LTX-VAE with VideoRAE leads to faster convergence.

Conclusion: Frozen VFM representations are validated as versatile, reconstruction-capable, and generation-friendly video latents, offering a promising alternative to conventional 3D-VAEs.

Abstract: Video generative models commonly rely on latent spaces learned by 3D Variational Autoencoders (3D-VAEs). However, conventional 3D-VAEs are mainly optimized for pixel-level reconstruction, which can limit the semantic and spatio-temporal structure captured by their latents. Meanwhile, Video Foundation Models (VFMs) such as V-JEPA 2 and VideoMAEv2 show strong video understanding capabilities, yet whether their frozen representations can be transformed into compact, reconstruction-capable, and generation-friendly video latents remains largely unexplored. We answer this question with VideoRAE, a representation autoencoder that leverages multi-scale hierarchical features from a frozen video foundation encoder and compresses them with a lightweight 1D self-attention projector. VideoRAE supports both continuous latents for Diffusion Transformers and discrete tokens for autoregressive models via multi-codebook high-dimensional quantization. During decoding, a local-and-global representation alignment objective with the frozen VFM teacher improves semantic preservation and enables training without KL regularization. Experiments show that VideoRAE achieves strong reconstruction in both continuous and discrete regimes. On UCF-101, it obtains state-of-the-art class-to-video gFVDs of 40 and 93 with AR and DiT generators, respectively, while converging approximately 5x faster than competing autoencoder baselines. In a controlled 2B-scale text-to-video study, replacing LTX-VAE with VideoRAE leads to faster convergence under comparable settings. These results validate frozen VFM representations as versatile and generation-friendly video latents. The model and code will be released on https://zhxie0117.github.io/VideoRAE.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [87] [OriginBlame: Record- and Token-Level Data Provenance for AI Training Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13037)
*Haolin Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ob is a record- and token-level data provenance system that precisely resolves data revocation requests into forget sets, reducing over-deletion from 101x to 1.3x with low overhead and improving unlearning by 42%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When data contributors request removal, no existing tool can accurately locate which training records belong to a given author. Current provenance systems operate at file or dataset level, forcing catastrophic over-deletion.

Method: ob propagates author identity through data processing pipelines and resolves revocation requests into precise forget sets via deterministic queries at record and token granularity.

Result: On 219,555 Wikipedia pages: record-level provenance reduced over-deletion from 101x to 1.3x; throughput overhead was 1.3-4.0% (HuggingFace) and 2.1-19.0% (Datatrove); on a 1.7B model, provenance-based forget sets improved unlearning by 42% over random baselines.

Conclusion: Record- and token-level data provenance effectively bridges the gap between data revocation requests and machine unlearning, enabling precise forget sets with minimal overhead and significantly better unlearning performance.

Abstract: When a data contributor requests removal, model trainers face a practical gap: unlearning algorithms require a forget set, yet no tool can locate which training records belong to a given author. Existing provenance systems operate at file or dataset level, forcing catastrophic over-deletion. We present ob, a record- and token-level data provenance system that propagates author identity through data processing pipelines and resolves revocation requests into precise forget sets via deterministic queries. Evaluation on 219,555 Wikipedia pages demonstrates that record-level provenance eliminates dataset-level over-deletion (from 101x to 1.3x), while integration adds 1.3-4.0% throughput overhead (HuggingFace) and 2.1-19.0% (Datatrove) on wiki data. On a 1.7B model, provenance-based forget sets improve unlearning by 42% over random baselines.

</details>


### [88] [SPINE: Bridging the Cyber-Physical Gap with Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13049)
*Minkyu Ham,Dongho Kim,Chan Lee,Jiayi Wang,Min Jun Kim,Yixi Zhang,Guo Ye,Jihai Zhao,Soyeon Park,Han Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPINE is an agentic framework that uses multi-agent workflows to automate the debugging and deployment of bimanual robots, reducing the need for expert calibration and improving operationalization success across different robotic platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The deployment gap (the robot's "spinal cord") — the expert-driven calibration required to deploy AI decision-making into physical robot platforms — remains a major bottleneck for scalable Embodied AI, preventing foundation models from being practically deployed.

Method: SPINE (Scalable Physical Integration with ageNtic Expertise) employs an agentic harness with two orchestrated multi-agent workflows: (1) a profile builder that creates robot-specific context, and (2) a debugger that iterates through diagnosis, repair, and validation cycles until teleoperation works.

Result: On DOBOT X-Trainer, a novice using SPINE outperformed experts using Claude Code (no SPINE), improving operationalization success from 75% to 100% and reducing mean time-to-teleoperation from 16m45s to 13m47s. On AgileX PiPER, SPINE resolved all 10 implanted bugs versus 9/10 for experts, in comparable time.

Conclusion: SPINE transfers across bimanual platforms, reduces dependence on expert calibration, and moves Embodied AI closer to scalable real-world deployment.

Abstract: Foundation models have given robots a sophisticated brain for complex decision-making, yet deploying that intelligence into a physical platform still demands tedious, expert-driven calibration. This deployment gap, the robot's spinal cord, remains a primary bottleneck to scalable Embodied AI. Hence, we propose SPINE (Scalable Physical Integration with ageNtic Expertise): an agentic framework for systematically debugging and deploying bimanual robots with minimal robotics expertise. SPINE's harness comprises two orchestrated multi-agent workflows: a profile builder that creates robot-specific context, and a debugger that cycles through diagnosis, repair, and validation until teleoperation works. Across seven DOBOT X-Trainer debugging scenarios, a robotics novice using SPINE outperformed human operators using Claude Code with the same reference materials, but without SPINE's structured workflow, improving operationalization success from 75% to 100% and reducing mean time-to-teleoperation from 16 min 45 s to 13 min 47 s. On AgileX PiPER, a distinct ROS/CAN bimanual arm, SPINE resolved all 10 implanted bugs, versus 9 out of 10 for the expert baseline, in nearly the same amount of time. Together, these results show that SPINE can transfer across bimanual platforms, reduce dependence on expert calibration, and move embodied AI closer to scalable real-world deployment.

</details>


### [89] [Interventional Grounding Audits: Black-Box Premise-Dependency Tests for LLM Chain-of-Thought via Predicate Substitution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13069)
*Hironao Nakamura*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces interventional grounding audits, a black-box method to test whether LLM chain-of-thought reasoning genuinely depends on its stated premises, and demonstrates on ProntoQA that it significantly outperforms baselines in detecting premise dependencies while also revealing "right answer, wrong reasoning" cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models produce chain-of-thought reasoning that appears logically sound but may not genuinely depend on its stated premises, motivating the need for a rigorous, step-level test of premise dependency.

Method: Interventional grounding audits: intervene on a single premise by substituting its target predicate with a fresh symbol, re-run the model, and check whether each reasoning step's normalized conclusion changes. Evaluated on 50 ProntoQA problems with GPT-4o.

Result: Achieves F1=0.806 on detecting proof-tree dependencies (F1=0.885 on predicate-determining dependencies; Recall=100%), significantly outperforming a self-consistency baseline (F1=0.343). Identifies that 66% of correctly-solved problems contain at least one "right answer, wrong reasoning" step.

Conclusion: The method reveals hidden reasoning flaws invisible to passive methods and has scope limits beyond formal, parsable benchmarks.

Abstract: Large language models produce chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning that appears logically sound yet may not genuinely depend on its stated premises. We introduce interventional grounding audits, a black-box, step-level test of premise dependency: we intervene on a single premise by substituting its target predicate with a fresh symbol, re-run the model, and check whether each reasoning step's normalized conclusion (canonical predicate form) changes. We evaluate on ProntoQA, a synthetic multi-hop deductive reasoning benchmark with gold proof trees, where step-level premise dependencies are known. Applied to 50 ProntoQA problems with GPT-4o, our method achieves F1 = 0.806 on detecting proof-tree dependencies (F1 = 0.885 on predicate-determining dependencies; Recall = 100%), significantly outperforming a self-consistency baseline (F1 = 0.343; 95% bootstrap CIs non-overlapping). We further identify that 66% of correctly-solved problems contain at least one aligned step insensitive to a direct proof-tree dependency under consistent substitution -- all involving entity-introduction premises, a documented blind spot of the consistent-substitution evaluator -- a "right answer, wrong reasoning" signal invisible to passive methods. All audit certificates, raw outputs, and reproduction scripts are available in a public GitHub repository, and we discuss scope limits beyond formal, parsable benchmarks.

</details>


### [90] [Probabilistic Extension of Neuro-Symbolic AGI Robots based on Belnap's Typed Intensional FOL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13073)
*Zoran Majkic*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper enhances the neuro-symbolic AI framework $IFOL_B$ by integrating probabilistic reasoning (based on Nilsson's probability structure) and Shannon's maximum entropy principle, using neural networks to compute probability density functions. It introduces global and local symmetry transformations to handle knowledge preservation and real-time decision-making, aiming to improve the cognitive power of the system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of purely neural systems (lack of interpretability and logical structure) by combining neural learning with symbolic reasoning, and to expand the cognitive capabilities of $IFOL_B$ with probabilistic reasoning for unknown sentences.

Method: Extends $IFOL_B$ with Nilsson's probability structure for handling unknown sentences; introduces global symmetry transformation (preserves knowledge base and logical deduction) and local symmetry transformation (enables real-time decisions on subsets of predicates); computes probability density function using Shannon's maximum information entropy via neural networks.

Result: A probabilistic neuro-symbolic AGI framework capable of both global knowledge consistency and local real-time probabilistic reasoning, with neural networks providing entropy-maximizing probability density estimates.

Conclusion: The proposed probabilistic extension of $IFOL_B$ enhances cognitive power by integrating uncertainty quantification and symmetry-preserving reasoning, advancing toward more robust neuro-symbolic AGI.

Abstract: Neuro-symbolic AI based on $IFOL_B$ is a way to combine neural learning and symbolic reasoning to overcome limitations of purely neural systems (like lack of interpretability and logical structure) with formal logical machinery for self-reference. In this paper we expand the cognitive power of $IFOL_B$ by using the probability computation for the currently unknown sentences, based on Nilsson's probability structure for the $IFOL_B$. We introduce the global symmetry transformation that preserves the current knowledge database and logical deduction, and the local one used for real-time decisions about concrete (sub)problems that involve only a very strict subset of $IFOL_B$ predicates. The computation of probability density function $KI$ in both cases, based on the Shannon's maximum information entropy, is provided by neural networks of this probabilistic neuro-symbolic AGI.

</details>


### [91] [Self-Improvements in Modern Agentic Systems: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13104)
*Zhe Ren,Yimeng Chen,Dandan Guo,Guowei Rong,Tonghui Li,R. B. Xiong,Qingfeng Lan,Wenyi Wang,Li Nanbo,Yibo Yang,Mingchen Zhuge,Jürgen Schmidhuber*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey provides a system-level framework for modeling self-improving autonomous agents as configurations coupling foundation models with operational scaffolds (prompts, memory, tools, control logic), formalizing self-improvement as self-induced update operators, and organizing prior work by update target and driving signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-improving autonomous agents are transitioning from research prototypes to deployed systems, but there is a lack of a unified framework to understand and compare how these agents achieve controllable evolution (adaptation) from experience with minimal human input.

Method: The authors propose a system-level framework representing an agent as a configuration coupling a foundation model with an operational scaffold (prompts, memory, tools, control logic), formalize self-improvement as a self-induced update operator that obtains and commits updates to either model parameters or scaffold components, and organize prior work by update target and driving signals.

Result: A structured taxonomy of self-improvement approaches organized by update target (model parameters vs. scaffold components) and the signals that drive change, along with a review of applications and evaluation methods.

Conclusion: The paper closes with open problems and future directions for self-improving agents, and provides a curated resource list on GitHub for tracking technical updates.

Abstract: Self-improving autonomous agents are moving from research prototypes to deployed systems. The primary goal is controllable evolution, or adaptation, from experience with minimal or even no human input. This survey frames modern self-improving agents as adaptive systems that convert experience into accumulated capability gains. We offer a system-level framework that represents a modern agent as a configuration coupling a foundation model with an operational scaffold of prompts, memory, tools, and control logic. Within this framework, self-improvement is formalized as a self-induced update operator that obtains and commits updates to model parameters or scaffold components. We organize prior work by update target and by the signals that drive change, then review applications and discuss evaluation, before closing with open problems and future directions. For convenience, we track technical updates on https://github.com/selfimproving-agent/awesome-Self-Improving-Agents.

</details>


### [92] [Improving Molecular Property Prediction in Small Language Models Using Graph-based Tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13115)
*Konstantinos Bougiatiotis,Dimitrios Kelesis,Georgios Paliouras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Small language models for molecular property prediction from SMILES suffer from structural blindness. The authors propose a Context-Augmented Prompting framework that uses GNN expert models to provide predictive hints, confidence scores, and explanatory subgraphs at inference time. This approach yields accuracy gains of up to 74% on Tox21, though a gap remains compared to specialized GNNs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small language models struggle with zero-shot molecular property prediction from SMILES strings because sequential representations fail to capture key graph-topological cues (structural blindness).

Method: A modular Context-Augmented Prompting framework that enables agentic tool use: a trained GNN expert provides predictive hints with confidence, and a GNN extracts instance-specific explanatory subgraphs (subgraph SMILES + explanatory paragraph) to augment prompts. Evaluated on MUTAG and Tox21 with 5 prompting configurations using 3 SLMs.

Result: Substantial accuracy gains across datasets (up to 74% on Tox21, >25% relative improvement). Validated functional relevance of motifs via edge-drop intervention. However, a persistent gap remains compared to specialized GNN models.

Conclusion: Graph-derived context significantly improves SLM molecular property prediction, but text-conditioned reasoning still cannot match specialized GNNs, highlighting both the value and limits of this approach.

Abstract: Small language models (SLMs) have shown promise for zero-shot molecular property prediction from SMILES strings, yet they often suffer from structural blindness because sequence representations under-specify key graph-topological cues. We propose a modular Context-Augmented Prompting framework that enables agentic tool use at inference time: a trained GNN expert model provides a predictive hint with confidence, and a GNN extracts an instance-specific explanatory subgraph (e.g., a subgraph SMILES and an accompanying explanatory paragraph). We evaluate three commonly used SLMs on MUTAG and Tox21 under five prompting configurations ranging from SMILES-only to using all available tools at hand. Across two datasets, enriching prompts with graph-derived context yields substantial accuracy gains, often exceeding 25% relative improvement and up to 74% on Tox21. We further validate the functional relevance of the extracted motifs via a necessity-based edge-drop intervention. Despite the observed gains, a persistent gap remains to specialized GNN models, highlighting both the value and limits of text-conditioned reasoning for molecular structure.

</details>


### [93] [Oracle Agent Memory as an Enterprise Memory Substrate for Long-Horizon AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13157)
*Richmond Alake,Cesare Bernardis,Paul Cayet,Luca Engel,Damien Hilloulin,Sungpack Hong,Allen Hosler,Nickolas Kavantzas,Ingo Kossyk,Son Le,Rhicheek Patra,Kartik Talamadupula,Valentin Venzin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This report introduces Oracle Agent Memory, a database-native memory substrate for long-horizon agents, achieving 93.8% accuracy on LongMemEval while using 10.7x fewer tokens compared to flat-history baselines, organized around memory lifecycle, layered architecture, and evaluation methodology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon agents require persistent memory for task state retention across extended conversations, user-specific fact recovery, and procedural knowledge accumulation, which go beyond simple document retrieval and necessitate a structured memory substrate.

Method: The paper proposes Oracle Agent Memory built on Oracle Database, featuring a lifecycle model (ingestion, extraction, consolidation, retrieval, summarization, revision/removal), a layered architecture separating active memory core from passive memory-store with scope control (users, agents, threads), and a dual evaluation methodology combining downstream task accuracy with memory-centric metrics (evidence retrieval, recall, latency, token use).

Result: Achieved 93.8% accuracy on LongMemEval, used approximately 10.7x fewer tokens than flat-history baselines, with comparisons against published external baselines where available.

Conclusion: Oracle Agent Memory provides an effective database-native solution for agent memory management, demonstrating strong accuracy with significant token efficiency, and the report includes implementation-oriented appendix material for practical deployment.

Abstract: Agent memory is a systems problem for long-horizon agents. Practical deployments require retention of task state across extended conversations, recovery of user-specific facts and preferences across sessions, and accumulation of procedural knowledge from prior outcomes. These requirements extend beyond document retrieval: a memory layer must determine which interactions become durable state, how that state is scoped, how it is retrieved under latency constraints, and how it is revised or removed over time. This report studies Oracle Agent Memory as a database-native memory substrate built on Oracle Database. Three themes organize the discussion: memory as a lifecycle spanning ingestion, extraction, consolidation, retrieval, summarization, and revision or removal; a layered architecture that separates an active memory core from a passive memory-store interface with explicit scope control across users, agents, and threads; and evaluation methodology in which downstream task accuracy is complemented by memory-centric measures such as evidence retrieval, recall, latency, and estimated token use. The report summarizes LongMemEval results, reaching 93.8% accuracy, compares Oracle Agent Memory against flat-history baselines, using about 10.7x fewer tokens, and published or reported external baselines where available, and closes with implementation-oriented appendix material covering setup, thread lifecycle, and search semantics.

</details>


### [94] [Learning Safe Agent Behaviour from Human Preferences and Justifications via World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13172)
*Ilias Kazantzidis,Timothy J. Norman,Yali Du,Christopher T. Freeman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DROPJ is a human-centered method for safe reinforcement learning that uses a learned world model, human-generated simulated trajectories, justified preference feedback, and model predictive control to train and deploy safe policies without needing real environment rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional reinforcement learning is impractical in safety-critical environments due to unknown dynamics and lack of suitable reward functions, necessitating a human-centered approach for safe training and deployment.

Method: DROPJ learns a world model from prior real-world trajectories, has a human play in this simulator to extract informative trajectories, collects pairwise preference judgments with justifications from humans, trains a reward model from these justified preferences, and deploys the agent using model predictive control with the world model and reward model.

Result: Generating informative simulated trajectories significantly reduces computational cost during training. Using preferences over other feedback types substantially improves deployment performance. Safety justifications accompanying preferences can enhance safety or prioritize user-prescribed safety aspects during deployment.

Conclusion: DROPJ effectively enables safe training and deployment in unknown, reward-free environments by leveraging human input through justified preferences in a learned simulator, achieving both computational efficiency and improved safety during deployment.

Abstract: We address the problem of safely training an agent policy and deploying a good and safe policy, in settings where the environment dynamics are unknown and no suitable reward function is available. In the context of safety-critical environments, we consider traditional reinforcement learning impractical and resort to the resource of human input. We introduce DROPJ, a human-centred method for both safe training and deployment. We first learn a world model (a learned simulator) from a dataset of prior real-world trajectories. A human then plays the game in this learned simulator to extract several informative simulated trajectories. From these, we sample pairs of simulated trajectory segments and elicit from a human their preference over these segments, as well as a reason (justification) for their choice. We then train a reward model from these justified preferences and use it, together with the world model, to directly deploy the agent using model predictive control. Running real-user experiments, we find that generating informative simulated trajectories from a user significantly reduces the computational cost during training compared to other strategies, and can also improve the performance during deployment. In the context of training within a learned simulator, we show that the use of preferences rather than other types of feedback substantially improves the performance during deployment. We further demonstrate that safety justifications accompanying preferences can significantly enhance safety or prioritise user-prescribed aspects of safety associated with them during deployment.

</details>


### [95] [CayleyR: Solving the TopSpin puzzle via cycle intersection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13219)
*Yuri Baramykov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: cayleyR is an R package that solves permutation puzzles (specifically TopSpin(n,k)) using iterative bidirectional search on Cayley graphs, detecting cycle intersections to find connecting paths, with C++ and optional GPU acceleration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide an efficient, accessible software tool for solving permutation puzzles (like TopSpin) by exploiting the algebraic structure of Cayley graphs of the symmetric group.

Method: Iterative bidirectional search: random operation sequences generate cycles in the Cayley graph of Sn; cycle intersections yield a connecting path between initial and target states. When no intersection is found, distance-guided bridge selection narrows the gap. Implementation uses C++ hash-indexed state store with optional Vulkan GPU acceleration.

Result: A publicly available R package (on CRAN) that can solve TopSpin(n,k) puzzles by finding paths in their Cayley graph state space.

Conclusion: cayleyR provides a practical, GPU-accelerated algebraic approach to solving permutation puzzles through cycle intersection detection in Cayley graphs.

Abstract: We present cayleyR, an R package for solving permutation puzzles by detecting cycle intersections in Cayley graphs. The core algorithm performs an iterative bidirectional search: from both the initial and target permutation states, random operation sequences generate cycles in the Cayley graph of the symmetric group Sn; their intersection yields a connecting path. When no direct intersection is found, a distance-guided bridge selection narrows the gap, and the process repeats. The package targets the TopSpin(n,k) puzzle, whose state space is a Cayley graph of Sn generated by a cyclic shift and a prefix reversal. We describe the mathematical framework, the algorithm, and its implementation, which combines a C++ hash-indexed state store with optional Vulkan GPU acceleration. The software is publicly available on CRAN.

</details>


### [96] [Networked Intelligence: Active Shared Context Graphs for Human-AI Team Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13220)
*Sutanay Choudhury,Jeffrey J. Czajka,Lummy M. O. Monteiro,Erin Bredeweg,Jason McDermott,Katherine Wolf,Alex Beliaev,Josh Elmore,Paul Piehowski,Kylee Tate,Yuqian Gao,Aivett Bilbao,Kelly Stratton,Scott Baker,Jaydeep P. Bardhan,Kristin Burnum Johnson,Chris Oehmen,Robert Rallo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Mycelium is an active shared workspace that connects multiple researchers and AI agents as a multi-user co-scientist, enabling networked intelligence by automatically capturing, relating, and routing observations and hypotheses across team members. Tested in a biological multi-omics campaign, it successfully routed shared context from a local analytical finding into a cross-expert mechanistic constraint and experimental design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Challenging scientific problems are solved by teams with diverse expertise, not single reasoners. Current AI-for-science systems focus on scaling individual reasoning (better models, larger contexts, long-horizon execution) but neglect scaling connections between humans and AI to enable networked intelligence where results produced in one context reach others who can act on them.

Method: Mycelium is an active shared workspace that (1) captures important observations and hypotheses as human users and agents work, (2) tracks how they relate to the team's evolving model, and (3) routes relevant context to the person or agent whose next decision it can inform. The paper also provides a computational account framing networked intelligence as sparse conditional computation over distributed scientific contexts.

Result: In a biological multi-omics campaign, routed shared context transformed a local analytical finding into a cross-expert mechanistic constraint and ultimately into an experimental design. The computational analysis distinguishes when a scaled standalone agent matches the network versus when independent expertise and non-mergeable contexts make the network irreducible.

Conclusion: Networked intelligence—scaling connections between humans and AI systems rather than just scaling individual models—is essential for solving complex scientific problems. Mycelium provides a working instantiation, and the sparse conditional computation framework offers a theoretical basis for understanding when networked approaches are necessary over standalone agents.

Abstract: Most AI-for-science systems focus on scaling a single reasoning process through better models, larger context windows, long-horizon agentic execution, or digital co-scientists working with one principal user. However, challenging scientific problems are rarely solved by one reasoner alone. They are solved by teams whose members bring different priors, experimental backgrounds, tacit knowledge, and domain-trained intuitions. The open problem is therefore not only how to scale models, but how to cultivate networked intelligence: scaling the connections between humans and AI systems so that a result or hypothesis produced in one context reaches another person, agent, instrument, or robot that can act on it. We introduce Mycelium, an active shared workspace that automatically connects researchers and AI agents as a multi-user co-scientist. As human users and agents work, the system captures important observations and hypotheses, tracks how they relate to the team's evolving model, and routes them to the person or agent whose next decision they can inform. We evaluate Mycelium in its first empirical test, a biological multi-omics campaign in which routed shared context turned a local analytical finding into a cross-expert mechanistic constraint and ultimately into an experimental design. We also give networked intelligence a computational account as sparse conditional computation over distributed scientific contexts. This account distinguishes when a scaled standalone agent can match the network from when independent expertise and non-mergeable contexts make the network irreducible.

</details>


### [97] [AI-Native Insurance for Agentic AI: Pricing, Underwriting, and End-to-End Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13230)
*Quanyan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops an AI-native mathematical framework for underwriting, pricing, and contract design for agentic AI deployments, mapping risk states (autonomy, authority, exposure, governance, dependency) to insurance parameters and optimizing contracts under constraints. It establishes insurability properties and demonstrates the framework through a healthcare case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems introduce novel insurance challenges due to autonomous decision-making, tool invocation, environment modification, and third-party interactions, which traditional insurance frameworks cannot adequately address.

Method: An AI-native mathematical framework that represents AI deployments as risk states (autonomy level, operational authority, permission exposure, governance maturity, dependency concentration), maps these to insurance parameters (event probabilities, loss severities, governance costs, premiums, deductibles, coverage allocation, policy covenants), and formulates an optimization problem under participation, profitability, and incentive compatibility constraints.

Result: Structural properties of insurability including an insurability region, monotone deterioration of feasibility with increasing exposure, and governance certification thresholds. Insurance is interpreted as both an operational cost and regulatory mechanism. A healthcare case study demonstrates contract optimization, sensitivity analysis, and automated claims processing.

Conclusion: The framework provides a rigorous mathematical foundation for insuring agentic AI deployments, establishing that insurance can serve dual roles as operational cost and regulatory mechanism, with governance maturity playing a critical role in determining insurability.

Abstract: Agentic AI introduces new insurance challenges because autonomous AI systems can make decisions, invoke tools, modify external environments, and interact with third-party services. This paper develops an AI-native mathematical framework for underwriting, pricing, and contract design for agentic AI deployments. A deployment is represented by a risk state that captures autonomy level, operational authority, permission exposure, governance maturity, and dependency concentration. The framework maps the risk state to event probabilities, loss severities, governance costs, premiums, deductibles, coverage allocation, and policy covenants, and formulates an optimization problem for insurance contract design under participation, profitability, and incentive compatibility constraints. The paper establishes structural properties of insurability, including characterization of an insurability region, monotone deterioration of feasibility with increasing exposure, and governance certification thresholds. Insurance is further interpreted as both an operational cost and a regulatory mechanism for AI deployment. A healthcare case study illustrates contract optimization, sensitivity analysis, and automated claims processing for agentic AI systems.

</details>


### [98] [Cost-Optimal Foundation Model Deployment Portfolio for Transportation Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13239)
*Xi Cheng,Ke Liu,Siyuan Feng,Jane Lin,H. Oliver Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Foundation Model Deployment Portfolio (FMDP) problem, a mixed-integer program for selecting the optimal combination of foundation models (LLMs, VLMs) and deployment modes (open-source APIs, closed APIs, on-premise GPUs) across multiple TMC functions to minimize total cost of ownership under quality, latency, safety, and shared GPU constraints. The problem is proven NP-hard, and a greedy heuristic is proposed. A case study with 5 TMC functions and 19 candidates shows a mixed portfolio costing $34/month (97% lower than the cheapest all-closed-API baseline), with break-even analysis indicating on-premise GPU is economical only above ~309 vision queries/hour or if API prices double.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying multiple foundation models across various TMC functions (anomaly detection, incident reporting, traveler information) raises a complex portfolio optimization question: which model should serve which function, in which deployment mode (open-source API, closed API, on-premise), and under what shared hardware budget?

Method: The FMDP problem is formulated as a mixed-integer program minimizing TCO while satisfying per-function quality, latency, and safety constraints over shared GPU capacity. NP-hardness is proved via reduction from 0-1 knapsack, and a polynomial-time greedy heuristic is proposed.

Result: In a case study with 5 TMC functions and 19 candidate (model, mode) pairs, FMDP yields a mixed portfolio costing $34/month, which is 97% below the cheapest all-closed-API baseline. Four functions are routed to open-source APIs; one requiring higher quality uses a closed API. On-premise GPU investment breaks even only above ~309 vision queries/hour or if API prices double.

Conclusion: Mixed deployment portfolios combining open-source and closed APIs can dramatically reduce TCO for multi-function TMC deployments, while on-premise GPU deployment is only cost-effective at high query volumes or with significant API price increases.

Abstract: Foundation models, including large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs), are increasingly used for transportation management center (TMC) tasks such as anomaly detection, incident reporting, and traveler information. Deploying multiple such models across TMC functions raises a portfolio question: which model should serve each function, in which deployment mode, and under what shared hardware budget? We formulate this as the Foundation Model Deployment Portfolio (FMDP) problem, a mixed-integer program minimizing total cost of ownership (TCO) subject to per-function quality, latency, and safety constraints over shared GPU capacity. We prove the problem NP-hard by reduction from the 0-1 knapsack problem and propose a polynomial-time greedy heuristic. In an illustrative case study with five TMC functions and 19 candidate (model, mode) pairs, FMDP identifies a mixed portfolio costing $34/mo (97% below the cheapest feasible all-closed-API baseline) by routing four functions to open-source APIs and the one function whose quality floor no open-source model meets to a closed API. Break-even analysis shows that on-premise GPU investment becomes reasonable only above approximately 309 vision queries/hour or if API prices double.

</details>


### [99] [Harness Handbook: Making Evolving Agent Harnesses Readable,Navigable, and Editable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13285)
*Ruhan Wang,Yucheng Shi,Zongxia Li,Zhongzhi Li,Yue Yu,Junyao Yang,Kishan Panaganti,Haitao Mi,Dongruo Zhou,Leoweiliang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Harness Handbook, a behavior-centric representation automatically synthesized from AI agent harness codebases via static analysis and LLM-assisted structuring. It also proposes Behavior-Guided Progressive Disclosure (BGPD) to help agents locate code implementing specific behaviors. This approach improves behavior localization and edit-plan quality for evolving complex agentic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modifying production AI agent harnesses is difficult because they are large, tightly coupled, and behaviorally distributed. Developers must manually map behavior descriptions to code locations (behavior-to-code mapping), which is a central bottleneck in harness evolution.

Method: The authors propose (1) Harness Handbook: a behavior-centric representation synthesized automatically via static analysis and LLM-assisted structuring that links each behavior to its source code, and (2) Behavior-Guided Progressive Disclosure (BGPD): a technique that guides agents from high-level behaviors to relevant implementation details and verifies candidate locations against the current source.

Result: On diverse modification requests from two open-source harnesses, Handbook-Assisted planning improves behavior localization and edit-plan quality while using fewer planner tokens. The largest gains are observed on scattered sites, rarely executed paths, and cross-module interactions.

Conclusion: Evolving complex agentic systems depends not only on generating edits but also on determining where those edits should be made. Behavior localization is a critical bottleneck that can be addressed through behavior-centric representations like the Harness Handbook.

Abstract: The capability of a modern AI agent depends not only on its foundation model but also on its harness, which constructs prompts, manages state, invokes tools, and coordinates execution. As models, APIs, environments, and requirements evolve, the harness must be continually modified. Before such a change can be made, a developer or coding agent must identify all code locations that implement the target behavior. This is difficult because production harnesses are large, tightly coupled, and behaviorally distributed, while modification requests describe what the system should do and repositories are organized by files and modules. Code search, repository indexing, and long-context processing ease inspection, but still leave this behavior-to-code mapping to be recovered by hand. Behavior localization is therefore a central bottleneck in harness evolution. We introduce the Harness Handbook, a behavior-centric representation synthesized automatically from a harness codebase via static analysis and LLM-assisted structuring, linking each behavior to its corresponding source. We also introduce Behavior-Guided Progressive Disclosure (BGPD), which guides agents from high-level behaviors to relevant implementation details and verifies candidate locations against the current source. On diverse modification requests from two open-source harnesses, Handbook-Assisted planning improves behavior localization and edit-plan quality while using fewer planner tokens, with the largest gains on scattered sites, rarely executed paths, and cross-module interactions. Evolving complex agentic systems thus depends not only on generating edits, but also on determining where those edits should be made.

</details>


### [100] [Theory-Level Autoformalization: From Isolated Statements to Unified Formal Knowledge Bases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13292)
*Marcus J. Min,Mike He,Zhaoyu Li,Zixuan Yi,Sharad Malik,Aarti Gupta,Xujie Si,Osbert Bastani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This position paper argues for shifting autoformalization from individual statements to complete theories (including all axioms, definitions, lemmas, and dependencies), identifies challenges, and proposes three future research directions.


<details>
