  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autoformalization work focuses on individual statements, but real formalization requires entire theory-level structures with interconnected axioms, definitions, and lemmas. The paper advocates for a paradigm shift to theory-level autoformalization.

Method: The paper presents a position argument by examining the significance of theory-level autoformalization, addressing alternative views, identifying open challenges, and proposing three promising research directions.

Result: Three promising paths forward are proposed for advancing theory-level autoformalization, though specific results are not detailed as this is a position paper.

Conclusion: Autoformalization should evolve from statement-level to theory-level formalization, requiring structured libraries of interdependent formal components.

Abstract: Autoformalization translates informal natural language into formal, machine-verifiable languages. While most work focuses on individual statements, real formalization efforts are inherently theory-level: they require an entire web of axioms, definitions, and lemmas before target theorems can even be stated. In this position paper, we argue for theory-level autoformalization: formalizing complete theories, including all their inter-dependencies, as structured libraries. We examine the significance of this shift, address alternative views, identify open challenges, and propose three promising paths forward. Our survey of autoformalization is available at https://github.com/marcusm117/Awesome-Autoformalization.

</details>


### [101] [EZSMT Version 3, Matured](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13344)
*Yuliya Lierler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents EZSMTV3, an extensible SMT-based CASP framework that advances the translational approach to CASP solving, offering a more expressive input language, weak constraint optimization, and benchmarking results against peers like CLINGCON, CLINGO[DL], and CLINGO[LP].


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To advance the translational approach to CASP solving by developing an extensible SMT-based framework with more expressive input language, optimization support, and streamlined integration of new constraint types, building upon the EZSMT+ system.

Method: EZSMTV3 leverages state-of-the-art SMT solvers (CVC5, YICES, Z3) rather than implementing custom search procedures, introduces a more expressive input language, supports optimization via weak constraints, and provides foundations for streamlined integration of new constraint types.

Result: Benchmarking results comparing EZSMTV3 with CASP peers (CLINGCON, CLINGO[DL], CLINGO[LP]) demonstrate its ability to handle mixed-domain constraints involving both integers and reals.

Conclusion: EZSMTV3 provides a robust platform for future extensions and theoretical exploration within the CASP domain.

Abstract: Constraint Answer Set Programming (CASP) is a hybrid reasoning paradigm that combines Answer Set Programming (ASP) with Constraint Processing and Satisfiability Modulo Theories (SMT), enabling powerful declarative encodings of complex combinatorial search problems. This paper presents the design and implementation of EZSMTV3, an extensible SMT-based CASP framework that advances the translational approach to CASP solving. Building upon the foundation of the EZSMT+ system, EZSMTV3 introduces a more expressive input language, supports optimization via weak constraints, and offers foundations for streamlined integration of new constraint types. Rather than implementing custom search procedures, EZSMTV3 leverages state-of-the-art SMT solvers, such as CVC5, YICES, and Z3 to perform reasoning. The paper provides benchmarking results comparing EZSMTV3 with its CASP peers such as CLINGCON, CLINGO[DL], and CLINGO[LP], while showcasing its ability to handle mixed-domain constraints involving both integers and reals. The system provides a robust platform for future extensions and theoretical exploration within the CASP domain.

</details>


### [102] [Set-shifting Behavioral Test for Harnessed Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13396)
*Ziwei Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies how LLM agents adapt when tool reliability silently shifts within sessions, finding they settle into routine behaviors and exhibit distinct failure modes. Set framing (tools presented as competing vs. complementary) influences adaptation dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how LLM agents handle hidden reliability changes in tools during ongoing sessions, borrowing the set-shifting paradigm from cognitive psychology to systematically evaluate adaptation capabilities.

Method: The authors (1) construct tool-skill libraries with redundant tools that differ in hidden reliability, (2) design a branched evaluation schedule that shifts reliable tool groups at hidden boundaries with paired no-shift controls, (3) measure set-shifting accuracy as joint probability of routing to the correct tool group post-shift, and (4) test open-weight LLMs in an open-source agentic harness while varying set framing (competing vs. complementary tool presentation).

Result: Agents default to small recurring routines within a few turns of each boundary, with call shares concentrating on discrete values. Open-weight LLMs show qualitatively distinct failure modes across routines. Set framing significantly shifts routing dynamics.

Conclusion: LLM agents struggle to adapt to silent reliability shifts, exhibiting rigid routine behaviors. The presentation of tool alternatives (set framing) modulates adaptation, suggesting that interface design can impact agent robustness to reliability changes.

Abstract: What happens to an LLM agent's tool choice when the reliable tool silently changes within an ongoing session? We borrow set-shifting from cognitive psychology to study how well agents adapt to hidden reliability shifts. Our benchmark mounts tool-skill libraries with redundancies, where many tools solve the same task but differ in hidden reliability. In our evaluation framework, a branched schedule shifts the reliable tool group at hidden boundaries and pairs every shift with a no-shift control. We find that agents, by default, settle on a small recurring routine within a few turns of each boundary, with call shares concentrating on a few discrete values after each reliability shift. We score the set-shifting accuracy for each agent trajectory: the joint probability of routing to the target tool group in every post-shift window. We test open-weight LLMs in an open-source agentic harness and find qualitatively distinct failure modes across the same set of routines. We also find that set framing, how the toolset presents the alternatives as competing or complementary, shifts the routing dynamics.

</details>


### [103] [LAPO: Leave-One-Turn Attribution for Self-Generated Process Rewards in Multi-Turn Search Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13501)
*Qiang Zhu,Jiajun Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LAPO is a self-generated process-supervision method for multi-turn search reasoning that uses backward leave-one-turn attribution with answer-likelihood gain to assign process rewards, outperforming baselines across seven QA datasets without needing extra models or verifiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL for multi-turn search reasoning uses only terminal outcome rewards, which cannot distinguish between useful, redundant, and harmful intermediate interactions, leading to suboptimal credit assignment.

Method: LAPO replaces each search turn and its retrieval observation with a [DELETE] placeholder, measures the change in the policy's mean log-likelihood of the gold answer (Answer-Likelihood Gain), applies sign-consistency gating to retain only normalized process advantages whose directions agree with raw attribution scores.

Result: Across seven knowledge-intensive QA datasets with local retrieval, LAPO achieves an average exact-match score of 0.326, outperforming the strongest step-reward baseline IGPO by 0.053.

Conclusion: Policy-derived retrospective attribution can provide effective process supervision for multi-turn search agents, with complementary benefits from backward attribution and sign-consistency gating, and the method requires no additional reward model, teacher, verifier, or LLM-as-a-Judge.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for multi-turn search reasoning typically relies on terminal outcome rewards, which cannot distinguish useful, redundant, and harmful intermediate interactions. We propose LAPO, a self-generated process-supervision method based on backward leave-one-turn attribution. For each search turn, LAPO replaces the turn and its retrieval observation with a fixed [DELETE] placeholder and measures the resulting change in the current policy's mean log-likelihood of the gold answer. This Answer-Likelihood Gain estimates the turn's contribution while preserving all downstream interactions, allowing early evidence to be evaluated in the complete reasoning context. LAPO further applies sign-consistency gating, retaining only normalized process advantages whose directions agree with their raw attribution scores. The method requires no additional reward model, teacher, verifier, or LLM-as-a-Judge. Across seven knowledge-intensive question-answering datasets with local retrieval, LAPO achieves an average exact-match score of 0.326, outperforming the strongest step-reward baseline, IGPO, by 0.053. Ablations show complementary benefits from backward attribution and sign-consistency gating, demonstrating that policy-derived retrospective attribution can provide effective process supervision for multi-turn search agents.

</details>


### [104] [How Far Can Root Cause Analysis Go on Real-World Telemetry Data?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13548)
*Athira Gopal,Ashwanth Krishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses root cause analysis (RCA) in microservice failures using multimodal telemetry. Classical causal discovery and existing LLM-based multi-agent systems perform poorly on the OpenRCA benchmark. The authors propose a Structured Multi-Agent RCA pipeline that outperforms baselines, and introduce a reverse reasoning agent to classify failures as Reasoning Gap (evidence present but unused) or Data Ambiguity (evidence absent). They find the bottleneck is reasoning capability, not data access, and introduce automated rule mining to reduce manual knowledge curation. Model-level improvements, not better scaffolding, are needed to close the performance gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Root cause identification in production microservice failures is challenging due to large-scale, multimodal telemetry (metrics, logs, traces). Existing classical and LLM-based methods fail to achieve reliable accuracy on complex benchmarks like OpenRCA, motivating the need for a more effective approach and a diagnostic framework to understand where failures originate.

Method: The authors propose a Structured Multi-Agent RCA pipeline with two stages: (1) evidence extraction from multimodal telemetry, and (2) causal reasoning over extracted evidence. They introduce a reverse reasoning agent that, given the correct answer, traces back to identify which signals in the extracted anomalies support it and whether Stage 1 accessed those signals, classifying failures into Reasoning Gap or Data Ambiguity. They also develop an automated rule mining pipeline that extracts discrimination rules from reverse reasoning reports to reduce manual knowledge curation.

Result: The proposed Structured Multi-Agent RCA pipeline substantially outperforms both classical causal discovery methods and existing LLM-based multi-agent systems on the OpenRCA benchmark. The reverse reasoning analysis reveals that the required evidence is present in the vast majority of failures, indicating the bottleneck is reasoning capability rather than data access. Stronger models embed more domain expertise, and explicit knowledge injection partially compensates. Reasoning performance remains practically bounded even when evidence extraction is perfect.

Conclusion: Model reasoning capability and domain knowledge are the primary constraints for effective RCA in microservice failures. Scaffold engineering and better data pipelines alone cannot close the performance gap; progress requires improvements at the model level.

Abstract: Identifying root causes in production microservice failures requires reasoning over large-scale, multimodal telemetry spanning metrics, logs, and traces, a problem that has proved resistant to both classical and LLM-based approaches. The OpenRCA dataset exemplifies these challenges: it is large-scale, multimodal, and lacks detailed domain knowledge, and yields consistently low accuracy across all existing methods. We show that classical causal discovery methods and existing LLM-based multi-agent systems fail to reliably identify root causes on this benchmark, and present a Structured Multi-Agent RCA pipeline that substantially outperforms existing LLM-based and classical baselines, supporting both domain-knowledge and knowledge-free operating modes. To diagnose where failures originate, we introduce a reverse reasoning agent that, given the correct answer, identifies which signals in the extracted anomalies support it and determines whether Stage~1 had access to those signals, classifying each failure as Reasoning Gap (evidence present but unused) or Data Ambiguity (evidence genuinely absent). This analysis reveals that the required evidence is present in the vast majority of failures: the bottleneck is not data access but the agent's ability to reason over it correctly. We further introduce an automated rule mining pipeline that systematically extracts discrimination rules from reverse reasoning reports, reducing reliance on manual knowledge curation. Across all configurations, model reasoning capability and domain knowledge are the primary constraints: stronger models embed more domain expertise, and explicit knowledge injection partially compensates for this gap. Reasoning performance remains practically bounded even when evidence extraction is perfect: scaffold engineering and better data pipelines alone cannot close this gap; progress requires improvements at the model level.

</details>


### [105] [Multi-Agent Collaborative Reasoning with Tool-Augmented Evidence for Urban Region Profiling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13558)
*Xixuan Hao,Yutian Jiang,Jiabo Liu,Yihang Yang,Guangyin Jin,Song Gao,Yuxuan Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UrbanAgent is a multi-agent reasoning framework for urban region profiling that replaces correlation-driven multimodal representation learning with structured multi-agent collaborative reasoning, active evidence acquisition, and reinforcement learning-optimized tool use, achieving 8.1% average R² improvement over baselines on global datasets for carbon emissions, GDP, and population estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing urban region profiling methods rely on correlation-driven multimodal representation learning that assumes cross-modal consistency and uses static pipelines, which limits robustness in heterogeneous or unseen urban regions.

Method: UrbanAgent instantiates an independent agent per data modality, performs structured multi-agent collaborative reasoning to explicitly address cross-modal inconsistencies, and extends indicator prediction as a closed-loop process of active evidence acquisition with tool-augmented retrieval optimized via reinforcement learning.

Result: UrbanAgent consistently outperforms existing baselines, achieving an average improvement of 8.1% in R², and exhibits strong generalization performance in unseen-city settings on global urban datasets for Carbon emissions, GDP, and Population estimation.

Conclusion: Reframing urban region profiling from correlation-driven representation learning to reasoning-driven inference with multi-agent collaboration and active evidence acquisition yields more robust and generalizable predictions.

Abstract: Urban region profiling constitutes a core problem in urban computing, supporting applications such as population estimation, economic assessment, and environmental monitoring. Existing methods typically formulate this task as multimodal representation learning, fusing heterogeneous urban data, e.g., satellite imagery, points of interest, textual descriptions, and 3D building information, into latent embeddings for prediction. However, these approaches are largely correlation-driven, assume cross-modal consistency, and rely on static pipelines, which limit their robustness in heterogeneous or unseen urban regions. We propose UrbanAgent, an agentic framework that reframes urban region profiling as a reasoning-driven inference problem. UrbanAgent instantiates an independent agent for each data modality and performs structured multi-agent collaborative reasoning to explicitly address cross-modal inconsistencies rather than absorbing them into a single representation. In addition, UrbanAgent extends indicator prediction as a closed-loop process of active evidence acquisition and iterative reasoning, enabling agents to verify uncertain inferences through tool-augmented retrieval of external knowledge optimized via reinforcement learning. Extensive experiments on global urban datasets for Carbon emissions, GDP, and Population estimation show that UrbanAgent consistently outperforms existing baselines, achieving an average improvement of 8.1% in R2, and exhibiting strong generalization performance in unseen-city settings.

</details>


### [106] [AI advice suppresses people's willingness to say "I don't know", even when the advice is wrong and accuracy is incentivized](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13562)
*Chiara Marcoccia,Walter Quattrociocchi,Valerio Capraro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI assistance reduces people's willingness to say "I don't know," leading to more answers but far lower accuracy and inflated confidence, even when AI advice is wrong.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate how AI availability affects human judgment, specifically the metacognitive decision to suspend judgment when uncertain.

Method: Five experiments (N=3,132) where participants answered difficult questions with access to AI advice that was engineered to be wrong, separating AI use from accuracy.

Result: AI access nearly eliminated judgment suspension; participants answered more questions but were correct about a third as often, yet confidence nearly doubled. Incentives improved but did not fully restore suspension behavior.

Conclusion: AI suggestions may not only affect answer accuracy but also lower the metacognitive threshold for deciding whether to answer, reducing appropriate uncertainty expression.

Abstract: Knowing when to say "I don't know" is fundamental to human judgment, yet AI assistants offer a fluent answer to almost any question. In five experiments (N = 3,132; four preregistered, one direct replication), participants answered difficult questions and could always decline to respond. We engineered the questions so that AI advice was wrong, separating AI use from its accuracy. Merely having access to AI nearly eliminated participants' willingness to suspend judgment, and this held whether the advice was actively requested or simply displayed. Consequently, participants answered more questions but were correct about a third as often as when AI was unavailable-yet their confidence nearly doubled. Incentivizing accuracy and penalizing inaccuracy led participants to seek and follow AI advice less, answer more accurately, and suspend judgment more often, though still far less than when AI was unavailable. As AI suggestions grow ubiquitous and unsolicited, they may not simply affect answer accuracy; they may even alter the metacognitive threshold at which people decide whether they know enough to answer.

</details>


### [107] [SAFETY SENTRY: Context-Aware Human Intervention via EXECUTE-ASK-REFUSE Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13594)
*Tianyu Chen,Chujia Hu,Wenjie Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Safety Sentry, a lightweight guard model that reframes LLM agent safety from binary classification to three-way routing (EXECUTE/ASK/REFUSE), using a single decoding-time threshold for flexible deployment across risk tolerances without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Binary safe/unsafe classification conflates harmfulness and contextual appropriateness, operates at action-category granularity rather than per-instance, and causes routine interruptions that erode user autonomy and alert fatigue.

Method: Per-instance three-way routing (EXECUTE, ASK, REFUSE) instantiated via Safety Sentry, a lightweight guard model whose inference reduces to a single decoding call, with a single decoding-time threshold enabling repositioning across deployments without retraining.

Result: Safety Sentry outperforms open-weight and frontier closed-source baselines on overall accuracy and safety-related recall while controlling both directional error rates simultaneously.

Conclusion: Instance-level three-way routing with a tunable threshold is a more effective and flexible safety paradigm for LLM agents than binary classification at action-category granularity.

Abstract: LLM agents act on real-world environments through tool calls, and a single misjudged action can cause irreversible harm. The standard safeguard is a guard model that labels each proposed action as safe or unsafe, but this binary view conflates two distinct decisions: whether the action is harmful in itself, and whether it is appropriate given the user's context. It also operates at the granularity of action categories rather than individual instances, producing routine interruptions that erode autonomy and train users to wave through the most consequential alerts. We reframe the problem as a per-instance three-way routing decision over {EXECUTE, ASK, REFUSE} and instantiate it with Safety Sentry, a lightweight guard model whose inference reduces to a single decoding call. A single decoding-time threshold lets one fixed checkpoint be re-positioned across deployments of differing risk tolerance without retraining. Safety Sentry outperforms a broad set of open-weight and frontier closed-source baselines on overall accuracy and safety-related recall, while controlling both directional error rates simultaneously.

</details>


### [108] [Automatic Ordinary Differential Equations Discovery For Biological Systems Using Large Language Model Powered Agentic System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13608)
*David Krongauz,Arad Zulti,Eran Segal,Teddy Lazebnik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MEDA is an LLM- and symbolic-regression-powered agentic framework that discovers ODE models of biological and biologically inspired dynamical systems by retrieving knowledge, defining variables, generating constraints, proposing candidates, and fitting/evaluating them, achieving strong structural recovery and biologically plausible models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic scientific discovery for biological dynamical systems remains underexplored, with existing approaches focused on narrow equation-discovery benchmarks or broad end-to-end pipelines that do not specialize in biology.

Method: MEDA integrates LLM-based agents with symbolic regression to retrieve background knowledge, define admissible variables, generate mechanistic constraints, propose candidate ODEs, and fit/evaluate them.

Result: MEDA recovered correct state variables, achieved strong structural recovery in model retrieval and extrapolation tasks, and produced biologically plausible discovery-oriented models; ablation showed knowledge-guided formalization and mechanistic constraints are essential.

Conclusion: Knowledge-guided formalization and mechanistic constraints are critical components; numerical fitting alone can produce trajectory-compatible but biologically incorrect equations, highlighting the need for structured priors in biological model discovery.

Abstract: Automatic scientific discovery has long been a goal of computational scholars - a machine that can discover nature's secrets on its own, moving computational systems beyond data-fitting tools toward the generation and refinement of mechanistic models of the universe. Recent advances in symbolic regression (SR) and large-language-model (LLM)-based agents suggest that such systems can recover equations from data, incorporate domain priors, and automate parts of the research workflow. However, most existing approaches either focus on narrow equation-discovery benchmarks or broad end-to-end automation pipelines, while biological systems remain comparatively underexplored. Here, we introduce the MEDA system, an LLM- and SR-powered agentic framework for discovering ordinary-differential-equation (ODE) models of biological and biologically inspired dynamical systems. MEDA retrieves background knowledge, defines admissible variables, generates mechanistic constraints, proposes candidate ODEs, and fits and evaluates them. We evaluate it across canonical model retrieval, reasoning-based extrapolation to unseen variants, and open-ended discovery, with and without experimental data. Across these settings, MEDA recovered the correct state variables, achieved strong structural recovery in retrieval and extrapolation tasks, and produced biologically plausible discovery-oriented models. Ablation and robustness analyses show that knowledge-guided formalization and mechanistic constraints are load-bearing components, whereas numerical fitting alone can preserve trajectory-compatible but biologically incorrect equations.

</details>


### [109] [AgentCompass: A Unified Evaluation Infrastructure for Agent Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13705)
*Zichen Ding,Jiaye Ge,Shufan Jiang,Kai Chen,Mo Li,Qingqiu Li,Zehao Li,Zonglin Li,Tiaohao Liang,Shudong Liu,Zerun Ma,Zixing Shang,Wenhui Tian,Zun Wang,Liwei Wu,Zhenyu Wu,Jun Xu,Bowen Yang,Dingbo Yuan,Qi Zhang,Songyang Zhang,Peiheng Zhou,Dongsheng Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentCompass is an open-source, lightweight, and extensible evaluation infrastructure for LLM-based agents that decouples evaluation into three independent components (Benchmark, Harness, Environment), supports 20+ benchmarks across five capability dimensions, and provides fault-tolerant runtime and trajectory analysis tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation pipelines for LLM-based agents are fragmented and tightly coupled, hindering reproducibility and causing redundant engineering effort.

Method: AgentCompass organizes evaluation around three independent components (Benchmark, Harness, Environment) for flexible configuration; includes a fault-tolerant asynchronous runtime and comprehensive trajectory analysis tools for diagnosing failure modes like reward-hacking.

Result: Natively supports over 20 benchmarks across five capability dimensions, providing scalable and reproducible evaluation infrastructure.

Conclusion: AgentCompass offers the community a unified, reproducible, and extensible infrastructure to advance LLM agent research.

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) evolve into autonomous agents, the need for unified evaluation infrastructure becomes critical. However, current evaluation pipelines remain highly fragmented and tightly coupled, hindering reproducibility and causing redundant engineering. To address this, we introduce AgentCompass, an open-source, lightweight, and extensible infrastructure for evaluating LLM-based agents. AgentCompass organizes the evaluation process around three independent components, namely Benchmark, Harness, and Environment, thereby enabling flexible configurations without requiring the reimplementation of complex execution logic. Furthermore, it features a fault-tolerant asynchronous runtime and comprehensive trajectory analysis tools to transparently diagnose nuanced failure modes like reward-hacking. Natively supporting over 20 benchmarks across five capability dimensions, AgentCompass provides the community with a scalable and reproducible infrastructure for advancing agent research.

</details>


### [110] [STOCKTAKE: Measuring the Gap Between Perception and Action in LLM Agents with a Fair Oracle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13618)
*Sagar Deb,Ashwanth Krishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STOCKTAKE is a 26-week supply-chain benchmark that separates state-estimation failures (not knowing the hidden state) from control failures (knowing but failing to act) in LLM agents, revealing that even agents detecting 84-88% of hidden failures can score below a symptom-blind baseline due to both under-response and costly over-response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLM agents on multi-week decision tasks cannot distinguish whether an agent failed due to misreading the world (state estimation error) or reading it correctly but failing to act (the knowing-doing gap), because reference policies either rely on privileged information or are missing entirely.

Method: STOCKTAKE is a factored partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP) with six hidden factor processes, designed so that an exact Bayes filter per factor drives a rollout policy on the identical observation stream as the agent, serving as a fair oracle reference. Skill scores (0 = symptom-blind baseline, 1 = oracle) and written-rationale grading (stated-belief detection lag, knowing-doing rate) separate state estimation from control.

Result: Claude Sonnet 5, GPT-5.4, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, and Grok 4.5 detect 84-88% of hidden failures within a week of onset, but skill scores range from 0.62 to -0.23. Two models score below the symptom-blind floor despite naming factors slightly faster than the two that beat it. Under sustained stress, 34-43% of correctly diagnosed stress weeks still end in stockout for every model.

Conclusion: The knowing-doing gap has two faces: under-response (stocking out on diagnosed stress weeks) and over-response (costly actions that exceed what they protect). The two models scoring below the floor stock out least on diagnosed weeks but over-respond; STOCKTAKE measures both directions of failure.

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly evaluated on multi-week decision tasks in which the state that drives cost is never directly observed. On such tasks the final cost cannot say why an agent failed: it may have misread the world, or read it correctly and still failed to act (the knowing-doing gap). Existing evaluations cannot separate these two failures; their reference policies either read privileged information the agent never sees, or are missing altogether. We introduce STOCKTAKE, a 26-week supply-chain replenishment benchmark built as a factored partially observable Markov decision process with six hidden factor processes, designed so that a fair reference policy is computable: an exact Bayes filter per factor drives a rollout policy on the identical observation stream the agent receives. Scoring each run between a symptom-blind base-stock floor (0) and this oracle (1) yields a skill score, and grading each week's written rationale yields a stated-belief detection lag and a knowing-doing rate, so state estimation and control are measured separately. On fifty seeds with curated stress profiles, Claude Sonnet 5, GPT-5.4, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, and Grok 4.5 detect 84-88% of hidden failures, typically within a week of onset, yet span skill scores from 0.62 to -0.23: two of the four end below the symptom-blind floor while naming factors slightly faster than the two that beat it. The failure has two faces. Where stress persists, 34-43% of correctly diagnosed stress weeks still end in stockout for every model, a rate that partly reflects the severity of the weeks models notice. That rate also runs opposite to skill: the two models under the floor stock out least on diagnosed weeks, so under-response is only one face of the gap, and their traces point to the other, responses whose cost exceeds what they protect. STOCKTAKE measures both directions of that failure.

</details>


### [111] [UESF-Bench: Benchmarking and Probing for Unified Embodied Seeking and Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13621)
*Kun Yu,Jianhua Yang,Yixiang Chen,Changwei Wang,Hongyuan Yu,Yan Huang,Fushuo Huo,Ya Jing,Zhumin Chen,Keji He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces UESF-Bench, a unified benchmark for embodied agents that must both find and follow a language-described person, and proposes SeekFollow-VLA, a vision-language-action framework that achieves significant improvements over baselines in this task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for language-guided human following assume the target is visible at the start, overlooking realistic scenarios where agents must first search for a language-described target and then follow it in dynamic environments. Current work treats searching and following as separate tasks with no unified evaluation.

Method: The authors propose UESF-Bench (a large-scale diverse benchmark for embodied human seeking and following) and SeekFollow-VLA (a vision-language-action framework with a task-driven routing mechanism for latent phase inference and transition modeling between seeking and following).

Result: SeekFollow-VLA achieves clear improvements over both single-head and dual-head baselines across single-person and multi-person environments.

Conclusion: The work establishes a baseline for unified embodied seek-and-follow, addressing limitations of fragmented prior work by providing a systematic evaluation benchmark and effective framework for the combined task.

Abstract: Language-guided human following is an important capability for embodied agents, but existing benchmarks typically assume that the target person is visible at the start of an episode. This setting simplifies the problem and overlooks a more realistic requirement: an agent often needs to first find a language-described target and then persistently follow that target in a dynamic environment. While recent work has started to study human search, existing settings are typically evaluated in task-specific scenarios and often rely on stronger prior knowledge of the environment. Moreover, they usually treat searching and following as separate tasks and still lack a unified benchmark for systematic evaluation. To address these limitations, we introduce the Unified Embodied Seeking and Following Benchmark (UESF-Bench), a large-scale and diverse benchmark for embodied human seeking and following. The benchmark requires agents to handle semantic-guided exploration, reliable behavior switching and recovery, and delayed identity grounding. To this end, we propose SeekFollow-VLA, a vision-language-action framework with a task-driven routing mechanism for latent phase inference and transition modeling between seeking and following. Experimental results show that SeekFollow-VLA achieves clear improvements over both single-head and dual-head baselines across single-person and multi-person environments, establishing a baseline for unified embodied seek-and-follow.

</details>


### [112] [Explaining Reinforcement Learning Agents via Inductive Logic Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13655)
*Celeste Veronese,Edoardo Zorzi,Daniele Meli,Alessandro Farinelli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces objective, planning-oriented explainability metrics for RL policies using ILP-extracted logical rules, quantifying activation rate, feature coverage, syntactic/semantic distance to enable fine-grained, transferable analysis across single and multi-agent RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current XRL relies heavily on user studies with no shared evaluation metrics, while logic-based XAI lacks systematic quantification of explainability beyond common-sense assessments and simple propositional fragments.

Method: Use Inductive Logic Programming (ILP) to extract symbolic policy representations; define four novel metrics: activation rate, feature coverage, syntactic distance and semantic distance to quantify alignment, feature roles, and policy evolution.

Result: Metrics reveal action-specific learning dynamics beyond global return, provide finer-grained feature insights than traditional importance estimation, uncover coordination/specialization/adaptation in MARL, and support policy transfer and generalization.

Conclusion: The proposed metrics advance XRL by providing objective, transferable explainability quantification and contribute to logic-based XAI by principled explainability measurement of logical rules.

Abstract: Explainable Reinforcement Learning (XRL) seeks to make Reinforcement Learning (RL) policies more transparent and interpretable, a key requirement in safety-critical and human-centric scenarios. However, it is mostly based on user studies, thus targeting the needs of a specific audience and lacking shared evaluation metrics. On the other hand, logic-based approaches within eXplainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) provide compact, human-readable abstractions of decision-making. However, the systematic quantification of the explainability degree of logical representations remains an open problem. This work aims to advance the state of the art in XRL by introducing objective and planning-oriented metrics for policy explainability in RL settings. At the same time, it contributes to the field of logic for XAI by providing a principled way to quantify the explainability of logical rules, moving beyond common-sense assessments and simple propositional fragments. We employ Inductive Logic Programming (ILP) to extract symbolic representations of RL policies and define a novel set of explainability metrics, including activation rate, feature coverage, syntactic distance and semantic distance. These metrics quantify alignment between symbolic rules and agent behavior, the role of features in decision-making, and the evolution of policies during training and across agents in single and multi-agent RL. Experiments across different RL domains show that the proposed metrics highlight action-specific learning dynamics beyond global return, provide fine-grained insights into domain features beyond classical approaches for global feature importance estimation, and uncover coordination, specialization, and adaptation patterns in MARL. Moreover, they provide crucial insights for the transfer and generalization of action-specific policies.

</details>


### [113] [When Bots Join the Team: Bot Adoption and the Institutional Fabric of Open-Source Software Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13679)
*Yongren Shi,Wenyi Gong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analyzing 2,991 GitHub projects before and after bot adoption, this study finds that introducing bots as participants (not tools) is associated with improved group organization: more repeated collaboration, better role differentiation, fewer conflict cascades, and more distinctive outputs. The effects cluster around adoption time, with human and bot capabilities playing different functional roles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether automated agents participating in human teams strengthen or weaken group organization, addressing a fundamental question about human-AI collaboration in real-world settings.

Method: Quantitative analysis of 2,991 open-source GitHub projects, comparing two years of data before and after each project's first bot adoption. Measured three institutional theory capabilities (repeated engagement, social memory, role differentiation) and two outcomes (conflict cascades, output distinctiveness).

Result: Bot adoption is associated with increased repeated collaboration, greater recognition of bots in discussion, fewer conflict cascades, and more distinctive outputs. Changes cluster around adoption time. Capabilities predict outcomes by their function (coordination vs. differentiation) rather than by whether humans or bots provide them. Human-side capabilities mediate the bot-conflict association but not the bot-distinctiveness association.

Conclusion: Predictable, rule-based agents can become part of a community's social infrastructure. The bot serves as the occasion for organizational change, but social organization is the mechanism driving the observed effects.

Abstract: AI agents are joining human teams, raising a basic question: when an automated agent becomes a regular participant, does group organization strengthen or weaken? We study this question in open-source software, where bots open pull requests, review code, and merge changes alongside people, leaving a public record of every interaction. Treating bots as participants rather than tools, we examine 2,991 GitHub projects for two years before and after each adopted its first bot. We measure three capabilities that institutional theory links to durable coordination - repeated engagement, social memory, and role differentiation - and two outcomes: conflict cascades and output distinctiveness. Bot adoption is followed by more repeated collaboration, greater recognition of specific bots in discussion, fewer conflict cascades, and more distinctive outputs. These changes cluster around adoption rather than accumulating gradually. Because we lack an untreated comparison group, we interpret the results as precisely timed associations, not causal effects. Two patterns are difficult for alternative explanations to account for: capabilities predict outcomes according to their function - coordination versus differentiation - rather than whether humans or bots provide them, and human-side capabilities account for the bot-conflict association but not the bot-distinctiveness association. The findings are consistent with a specific interpretation: predictable, rule-based agents can become part of a community's social infrastructure. The bot is the occasion; social organization is the mechanism.

</details>


### [114] [CAVA: Canonical Action Verification and Attestation for Runtime Governance of Agentic AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13716)
*Zexun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAVA is a runtime-semantics layer that converts heterogeneous agent activity into canonical runtime action objects, enabling verification, approval binding, and independent reproducibility for AI governance across diverse execution environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems operate across heterogeneous runtimes (local coding hooks, SDK tools, browser automation, API gateways, etc.), producing incompatible runtime records for the same action. This makes it difficult to answer basic governance questions: what action was approved, what evidence binds approval to execution, and can an independent verifier reproduce the action identity later.

Method: CAVA defines a runtime-semantics layer for converting heterogeneous agent activity into canonical runtime action objects. It formalizes canonical action identity, semantic pattern detection, approval binding, receipt integrity, runtime-portable projection, and optional attestation substrates. It is positioned below Proof-Carrying Agent Actions (PCAA), where PCAA defines the deployer-owned route-review-prove governance process and CAVA defines the stable action object that process governs.

Result: Evaluated via a 96-seed, 384-variant benchmark covering semantic equivalence, semantic separation, wrapper bypass, false-positive control, approval binding, receipt reproducibility, attestation tamper detection, runtime portability, semantic pattern detection, policy degradation, and Azure deployment drills.

Conclusion: The contribution is a systems formulation of action-level canonicalization and policy-addressable semantic patterns as a necessary substrate for deployer-side AI governance.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems increasingly act through heterogeneous runtimes: local coding hooks, SDK tools, browser automation, managed-agent traces, API gateways, and workflow engines. A single operational act such as publishing code, changing identity state, moving money, or exporting data may therefore be represented by many incompatible runtime records. This makes a basic governance question difficult to answer: what action was actually approved, what evidence binds the approval to execution, and can an independent verifier reproduce the same action identity later?
  This paper presents Canonical Action Verification and Attestation (CAVA), a runtime-semantics layer for converting heterogeneous agent activity into canonical runtime action objects. CAVA is positioned below Proof-Carrying Agent Actions (PCAA): PCAA defines the deployer-owned route-review-prove governance process, while CAVA defines the stable action object that process governs. The paper formalizes canonical action identity, semantic pattern detection, approval binding, receipt integrity, runtime-portable projection, and optional attestation substrates. We study a reference implementation through a 96-seed, 384-variant benchmark covering semantic equivalence, semantic separation, wrapper bypass, false-positive control, approval binding, receipt reproducibility, attestation tamper detection, runtime portability, semantic pattern detection, policy degradation, and Azure deployment drills. The contribution is a systems formulation of action-level canonicalization and policy-addressable semantic patterns as a necessary substrate for deployer-side AI governance.

</details>


### [115] [Experience Memory Graph: One-Shot Error Correction for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13884)
*Wenjun Wang,Yuchen Fang,Fengrui Liu,Zibo Liang,Kai Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Experience Memory Graph (EMG) reformulates LLM agent failure recovery as a graph matching problem, converting failed and successful trajectories into directed graphs to extract corrective subgraphs and edit paths, enabling single-pass, loop-free test-time execution that outperforms reflection-based baselines on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents suffer from compounding errors in complex, long-horizon tasks and struggle to recover from failures. Existing self-correction via prompt-based reflection is brittle, costly (time/API), relies on iterative trial-and-error, and produces task-specific memory that generalizes poorly.

Method: EMG converts failed exploration and successful expert trajectories into directed action decision graphs at training time, matches them to extract common subgraphs (successful workflows) and graph edit paths (add/delete/relabel actions), stores them in a memory graph with intra-task nodes and cross-task edges. At test time, it retrieves relevant insights for single, loop-free execution.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art reflection baselines in success rate and average reward on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld, with no test-time trial-and-error required.

Conclusion: Graph-based failure recovery is a promising alternative to brittle prompt-based reflection, enabling more efficient and generalizable agent self-correction.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents have shown remarkable capabilities in autonomous decision-making by generating sequential trajectories of states, actions, and observations. However, in complex, long-horizon tasks, these agents frequently suffer from compounding errors and struggle to recover from failures. Existing self-correction mechanisms rely on prompt-based reflection, which is inherently brittle, incurs heavy time and API costs due to iterative trial-and-error loops, and produces task-specific memory that may be hard to generalize to new scenarios. To address this, we propose Experience Memory Graph (EMG), a framework that reformulates agent failure recovery as a graph matching problem. At training time, we convert both failed exploration trajectories and successful expert trajectories into directed action decision graphs. By matching these graphs, we extract common subgraphs (successful workflows) and graph edit paths that explicitly indicate how to correct failures (e.g., which actions to add, delete, or relabel under a given observation), and store them in a memory graph with intra-task nodes and cross-task edges. At test time, EMG retrieves relevant insights and guides the agent in a single, loop-free execution. Experiments on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld show that EMG consistently outperforms state-of-the-art reflection baselines in success rate and average reward, while requiring no test-time trial-and-error.

</details>


### [116] [AIMO Interpretability Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13899)
*Michal Štefánik,Philipp Mondorf,Andreas Waldis,Qianying Liu,Chuan Yang,Michal Spiegel,Josef Kuchař,Marek Kadlčík,Adam Vawda-Oomerjee,Chaoran Liu,Simon Frieder,Barbara Plank,Fazl Barez,Pontus Stenetorp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes the AIMO Interpretability Challenge, a competition to distinguish robust from spurious reasoning in frontier math LMs by analyzing internal mechanisms, providing olympiad-level problems, symbolic representations, model access, adversarial robustness assessments, and computing support to create open benchmarks connecting interpretability and generalization research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard reasoning benchmarks measure only final-answer accuracy, which cannot distinguish whether a model uses stable, generalizable reasoning mechanisms versus brittle, spurious shortcuts. This limitation motivates the need for interpretability-based methods to assess reasoning robustness.

Method: The competition provides (1) newly-published olympiad-level math problems with symbolic representations for generating functional variants, (2) access to frontier reasoning models, (3) adversarial robustness assessments, and (4) computing infrastructure. Participants develop methods to identify which models solve problems robustly using these resources.

Result: The competition aims to create a new open robustness benchmark and baseline systems, providing a lasting foundation for standard benchmarking in mathematical reasoning and interpretability.

Conclusion: The challenge connects interpretability and generalization research around the central question of whether frontier AI decision-making is generalizable and thus reliable, addressing a fundamental limitation in current evaluation practices.

Abstract: We propose the AIMO Interpretability Challenge, a competition on distinguishing robust from spurious reasoning in frontier mathematical language models based on the models' internal mechanisms. The challenge is motivated by a central limitation of standard reasoning benchmarks: strong final-answer accuracy does not reveal whether a model relies on stable reasoning mechanisms or exploits brittle reasoning shortcuts. Building on AI Mathematical Olympiad (AIMO) problems and submissions, together with resources from the Fields Model Initiative, the competition will provide (1) newly-published olympiad-level math reasoning problems and their symbolic representations, allowing generation of novel functional variants, (2) access to frontier reasoning models, and (3) assessments of models' adversarial robustness on these problems. Participants will use these resources, along with our computing infrastructure support, to develop methods for identifying which models solve problems robustly. Our competition will also create a new, open robustness benchmark and baseline systems, aiming to provide a lasting foundation for standard benchmarking in mathematical reasoning and interpretability. Scientifically, the competition connects interpretability and generalization research around a central question in AI research: can we determine if, and to what extent, the decision-making of frontier AI models is generalizable and thus, reliable?

</details>


### [117] [A Self-Evolving Agent for Longitudinal Personal Health Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13940)
*Haoran Li,Jiebi Deng,Tong Jin,Jinghong Han,Yuxin Wang,Zexin Wang,Qingyi Si,Weikang Gong,Xiahai Zhuang,Jia You,Wei Cheng,Jianfeng Feng,Hongcheng Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HealthClaw is an open-source agent architecture for longitudinal personal health management that maintains evolving memory (profile, procedures, episodes) separated from shared safety/medical knowledge. It updates memory via induction after each episode, achieving 45.7% answer accuracy (vs 0.2% baseline), 71.7% lower context exposure, and +27.0 percentage points mean gain across nine biomedical tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current health AI systems treat each request in isolation, failing to adapt to changes in a person's routines, preferences, measurements, and risks over repeated encounters.

Method: HealthClaw separates shared safety rules and medical knowledge from private longitudinal memory (profile facts, reusable procedures, episodic traces). After each episode, induction determines which content updates the profile, revises a procedure, remains episodic, or is excluded.

Result: Answer accuracy improved from 0.2% to 45.7% on 900 longitudinal probes; prompt-side context exposure reduced by 71.7% vs full-history prompting; higher privacy-aware answer quality with fewer unsafe disclosures in 100 privacy probes; mean absolute gain of 27.0 percentage points across nine biomedical tasks.

Conclusion: Offline benchmarks support governed, self-evolving memory for longitudinal personal health agents, though clinical effectiveness requires prospective evaluation.

Abstract: Personal health management unfolds over repeated encounters, yet most health AI systems treat each request in isolation. We developed HealthClaw, an open-source agent architecture that updates support as a person's routines, preferences, measurements and risks change. It separates shared safety rules and medical knowledge from private longitudinal memory containing profile facts, reusable procedures and episodic traces. After each episode, induction determines what should update the profile, revise a procedure, remain episodic or be excluded. We evaluated HealthClaw with a synthetic year-long benchmark and nine 200-case biomedical tasks. Across 900 longitudinal support probes, answer accuracy increased from 0.2% with current-query prompting to 45.7% with HealthClaw, while prompt-side context exposure was 71.7% lower than with full-history prompting. In 100 privacy probes, HealthClaw produced higher privacy-aware answer quality and fewer unsafe disclosures than both baselines. Across the biomedical tasks, the mean absolute gain in the task-specific primary metric was 27.0 percentage points, and seven gains remained significant after false-discovery-rate correction. These offline benchmarks support governed, self-evolving memory for longitudinal personal health agents, although clinical effectiveness requires prospective evaluation. HealthClaw is publicly available at https://github.com/HC-Guo/HealthClaw.

</details>


### [118] [Do Agent Optimizers Compound? A Continual-Learning Evaluation on Terminal-Bench 2.0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14004)
*Wenxiao Wang,Priyatham Kattakinda,Soheil Feizi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper challenges one-shot agent optimization by studying whether gains compound under recursive optimization across new tasks. Using Terminal-Bench 2.0, they compare GEPA, Meta Harness, and RELAI-VCL. Only RELAI-VCL, which builds regression control into the optimization loop, achieves compounding gains: positive transfer to unseen tasks and continued improvement after re-optimization, reaching 76.4% lifelong average pass rate vs. 58.7% baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most agent optimization methods are evaluated in a one-shot, static setting that does not reflect real-world deployment, where agents face new tasks and failures over time. The central question is whether optimizer-driven gains compound under recursive optimization without eroding previous improvements.

Method: A two-phase continual-learning evaluation on Terminal-Bench 2.0 hard tasks, comparing three agent-harness optimization approaches (GEPA, Meta Harness, RELAI-VCL) under identical optimization budgets. The evaluation measures transfer to unseen tasks and ability to improve after a second optimization round.

Result: All methods improve over baseline in the static single-phase setting. Under new tasks, GEPA transfers below baseline; Meta Harness transfers well but fails to improve further; RELAI-VCL is the only method that both transfers positively and continues improving, achieving highest pass rates at all stages and 76.4% lifelong average pass rate.

Conclusion: Optimization gains compound only when regression control is built into the optimization loop, providing an inductive bias against shortcut solutions that fail to generalize.

Abstract: Most reported gains from agent-optimization methods are one-shot: an agent is optimized against a fixed benchmark and the resulting improvement is reported as if it were a stable property of the method. This does not test the setting that matters for deployed agents, where optimization is applied recursively as new failures and new tasks appear over time. The central question this raises is whether optimizer-driven gains compound: after an agent has been optimized once, can it be optimized again on newly arrived tasks without eroding the gains the first round produced? We study this question with a two-phase continual-learning evaluation built from hard tasks in Terminal-Bench 2.0, comparing three approaches to agent-harness optimization (GEPA, Meta Harness, and RELAI's Verifiable Continual Learning, RELAI-VCL) under identical optimization budgets. All three methods improve over the baseline agent in the conventional, static, single-phase setting. However, once new tasks are introduced, the methods diverge sharply: GEPA's optimized agent transfers below the unoptimized baseline, Meta Harness transfers well but fails to improve further once given a second optimization budget, and RELAI-VCL is the only method that both transfers positively to unseen tasks and continues improving after those tasks are folded into the optimization objective, reaching the highest pass rate at every evaluated stage and the highest lifelong average pass rate overall (76.4% vs. 66.0% for GEPA, 64.6% for Meta Harness, and 58.7% for the baseline). Our key observation was that optimization gains compounded only when regression control was built into the optimization loop, providing an inductive bias against shortcut solutions that fail to generalize.

</details>


### [119] [AI-accelerated End-to-End Framework for Rapid Professional Upskilling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14044)
*Tam Nguyen,Hung Nguyen,Robert Ogburn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An end-to-end AI-accelerated framework for enterprise upskilling, validated by professional board approval, rapid learner certification, and downstream risk analysis capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing enterprise skills gap (reskilling needs for 59/100 workers by 2030) and the widening time-to-close gap (3 days in 2014 to 36 days in 2018) necessitate a more efficient upskilling approach, as current frameworks lack industry validation and only accelerate single stages.

Method: An end-to-end framework applying AI acceleration across five stages: knowledge acquisition, content development, content review/verification, teaching, and assessment development, optimized for both production and learning efficiency.

Result: Three validation signals: (1) US NASBA approved the framework-based program for CPE credits; (2) 3 learners passed the NVIDIA Certified Professional in Agentic AI exam in significantly reduced time (14 more in progress); (3) The knowledge base enabled production of a 1,267-item risk dataset for multi-agent AI systems.

Conclusion: The proposed AI-accelerated end-to-end framework is validated through professional certification, rapid learner outcomes, and robust downstream analytics, offering a scalable solution for enterprise upskilling.

Abstract: By 2030, 59 of every 100 workers will need reskilling or upskilling, yet the average time to close an enterprise skills gap grew from roughly 3 days in 2014 to 36 days in 2018. Most current frameworks accelerate single stages of upskilling programs and generally lack industry validation. We present an end-to-end framework that applies AI acceleration across five stages of knowledge acquisition, content development, content review and verification, teaching, and assessment development; with a strong focus on both production and learning efficiency. Three strong external signals validates the framework: the US National Association of State Boards of Accountancy reviewed and approved an upskilling program built on the framework for continuing-professional-education credits; 3 learners followed the program and passed the NVIDIA Certified Professional in Agentic AI exam in a significantly short amount of time, with 14 more in progress; the program's knowledge base supports complex downstream analysis such as the production of a robust 1,267 risk item dataset for managing multi-agent AI system risks.

</details>


### [120] [Earthquaker-AI: A Retrieval-Augmented Generation Framework with Rubric-Based Assessment for Primary School Earthquake Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14046)
*Xanthi Kokkinou,Chaido Mizeli,Nafsika Koulaxidou,Marina Delianidi,Konstantinos Diamantaras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Earthquaker-AI is a hybrid educational framework that integrates a Retrieval-Augmented Generation conversational AI assistant with a Lego WeDo2 robotics project to teach earthquake preparedness to primary-school students, progressing from mechanical simulation to cognitive/metacognitive processing with rubric-based assessment across grade levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance earthquake preparedness and conscious action among primary-school students by extending a STEM robotics project with AI-assisted cognitive and metacognitive learning, moving beyond purely mechanical simulation.

Method: Integrates Lego WeDo2 robotics for tangible seismic response simulation with a RAG-based conversational AI assistant that provides rubric-guided verbal feedback. Follows a progressive learning trajectory (basic recognition → action sequences → verbal production) assessed via increasingly complex multi-dimensional rubrics across grade levels.

Result: Experimental evaluation demonstrates high groundedness and accuracy with low hallucination rate in the dialogic module.

Conclusion: Combining robotics, rubrics, and RAG-based AI promotes technological literacy, self-regulation, and responsible use of digital systems, contributing to early crisis-management skills development.

Abstract: This paper presents Earthquaker-AI, a hybrid educational framework building upon a previously implemented educational robotics project by integrating a conversational AI assistant based on Retrieval-Augmented Generation. It aims to enhance earthquake preparedness and conscious action among primary-school students. The system extends the award-winning STEM project Earthquaker moving from mechanical simulation with Lego WeDo2 to cognitive and metacognitive processing. The robotics component uses Lego WeDo2 automation to simulate seismic response, letting students interact with sensors and actuators as tangible representations of protective actions. The assistant operates as a guided learning mechanism aligning student responses with safety guidelines, while providing rubric-based verbal feedback that supports self-regulated learning and calmness under emergency conditions. Earthquaker-AI follows a progressive learning trajectory aligned with cognitive development. In early grades, the focus is on basic recognition of safety actions through multiple-choice questions, assessed via a two-dimensional rubric. In middle grades, students identify correct action sequences through multiple-choice questions, evaluated via a three-axis rubric. In upper grades, the approach shifts to verbal production, requiring short written responses assessed via a four-dimensional rubric that includes clarity of expression. The dialogic module uses RAG to match student queries semantically with official guidelines, generating safe, accurate responses. Experimental evaluation shows high groundedness and accuracy, with a low hallucination rate. Overall, Earthquaker-AI combines hands-on engagement, information processing, and reflective practice. Combining robotics, rubrics, and AI promotes technological literacy, self-regulation, and responsible use of digital systems, contributing to early crisis-management skills.

</details>


### [121] [Deep Interaction: An Efficient Human-AI Interaction Method for Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14049)
*Hefeng Zhou,Jinxuan Zhang,Jiong Lou,Yuxin Liu,Chaochao Lu,Jingjing Qu,Jie Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Deep Interaction is a human-in-the-loop method that allows direct editing of Chain-of-Thought reasoning errors in LLMs, achieving >25% higher correction success rate and ~40% token reduction on STEM tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM error correction methods are inefficient: re-generation often repeats mistakes, and flagging errors in follow-up turns leads to similar recurring errors with wasted tokens.

Method: Users directly edit the erroneous parts in the original CoT response, preserving correct steps. The edited CoT is refined into a distilled prompt that steers the LLM along the corrected reasoning path.

Result: Over 25% improvement in correction success rate and approximately 40% reduction in token usage on STEM reasoning tasks compared to baseline approaches.

Conclusion: Direct editing of reasoning chains with distilled prompt steering is an efficient and effective human intervention mechanism for correcting LLM reasoning errors.

Abstract: The emergence of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has significantly enhanced the ability of large language models (LLMs) to tackle complex, multi-step tasks. However, when errors occur, current interaction approaches typically involve re-generating another response that may make mistakes again, or users laboriously flag the faulty step in follow-up turns that may get responses <You are right, I made a mistake here> followed by similar errors recurring. To address this issue, we propose an efficient human intervention mechanism for precisely correcting reasoning errors in LLMs, termed Deep Interaction. Our approach enables direct editing of the original response, allowing erroneous parts to be corrected while preserving accurate reasoning steps. We refine the edited CoT into a distilled prompt, which then steers the LLM along the corrected reasoning path. Experimental results show that our method achieves over a 25% improvement in correction success rate and reduces token usage by approximately 40% on STEM tasks reasoning compared to baseline approaches.

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [122] [Volumetric Inverse Rendering via Neural Radiative Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13695)
*Ntumba Elie Nsampi,Adarsh Djeacoumar,Hans-Peter Seidel,Tobias Ritschel,Thomas Leimkühler*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: The paper proposes a neural volumetric inverse rendering method that recovers optical properties of participating media from multi-view images by jointly optimizing neural fields for both medium properties and light field, enforcing physically accurate global illumination through a differential form of the Radiative Transfer Equation, while using volume rendering along primary rays to address low-frequency bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing volumetric inverse rendering methods either rely on complex differentiable Monte Carlo light transport simulation or use simplified models that cannot capture global illumination effects. A more practical approach that reconciles physically complete light transport with general-purpose neural optimization is needed.

Method: The optical properties of the medium (scattering, absorption, phase function) and the full light field are represented as neural fields. A joint optimization process is used, with a residual objective derived from the local differential form of the Radiative Transfer Equation to enforce global illumination, plus a volume rendering term along primary viewing rays to reduce low-frequency bias.

Result: The method demonstrates successful reconstruction of spatially varying, color-resolved scattering, absorption, and phase function parameters from multi-view images. It also supports learning generative models of participating media with physically accurate optical properties under global illumination.

Conclusion: The proposed formulation effectively bridges physically complete light transport and neural optimization, enabling physically accurate volumetric inverse rendering without requiring complex differentiable light transport simulation, while also supporting generative modeling of participating media.

Abstract: Volumetric inverse rendering seeks to recover the optical properties of participating media from images. Existing approaches either rely on differentiable stochastic light transport simulation, which require substantial algorithmic effort, or use simplified models that fail to capture global illumination. We propose a formulation that reconciles physically complete light transport with general-purpose neural optimization. The optical properties of the medium and the full light field are represented as neural fields and estimated through a joint optimization process. Global illumination is enforced via a residual objective derived from the Radiative Transfer Equation in local differential form, complemented by a volume rendering term along primary viewing rays to mitigate \rev{low-frequency} bias. We demonstrate reconstruction of spatially varying, color-resolved scattering, absorption, and phase function parameters from multi-view images. Beyond reconstruction, the same framework supports learning generative models of participating media with physical optical properties under global illumination.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [123] [Automatic Differentiation from Scratch: How PyTorch Computes Gradients in Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13042)
*Abdeladhim Tahimi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper numerically traces PyTorch's automatic differentiation engine during PINN training, detailing how it computes physics derivatives and parameter gradients through a 1-3-3-1 MLP on a simple ODE, verifying every adjoint value against hand-derived results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a concrete, numerically verified exposition of how PyTorch's autograd handles the two-level differentiation required in PINNs (physics derivatives and parameter gradients), bridging the gap between automatic differentiation internals and the P/Q sensitivity framework.

Method: Using a 1-3-3-1 MLP and the IVP y'(t)+y(t)=0, y(0)=1, the paper traces the forward computational graph, the reverse-mode backward pass computing all 22 parameter gradients, and the create_graph=True mechanism for higher-order differentiation through the physics-informed residual. Numerical adjoint values are verified against hand derivations.

Result: Complete numerical trace of forward graph, reverse-mode gradient computation (all 22 gradients in one pass), and the graph-on-graph mechanism enabled by create_graph=True, with all adjoints matching the hand-derived P/Q sensitivity framework.

Conclusion: The paper establishes a direct numerical correspondence between PyTorch's automatic differentiation engine and Tahimi's hand-derived P/Q sensitivity framework, providing a pedagogical resource for understanding AD internals in the context of PINN training.

Abstract: This paper traces, with explicit numerical values, how PyTorch's automatic differentiation (AD) engine computes gradients for Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) training -- a setting that requires two levels of differentiation: computing the physics derivative $\hat{y}'(t)=d\hat{y}/dt$ through the network, and computing parameter gradients $\nabla_θL$ of a loss that itself depends on $\hat{y}'(t)$. Using a 1-3-3-1 multilayer perceptron and the initial value problem $y'(t)+y(t)=0$, $y(0)=1$, we trace the complete pipeline at every node: the computational graph built during the forward pass, the reverse-mode backward traversal that computes all 22 parameter gradients in a single pass, and the graph-on-graph mechanism by which \texttt{create\_graph=True} enables correct differentiation through the physics-informed residual. Every adjoint value is verified against the hand derivations of Tahimi (2026), connecting the $P/Q$ sensitivity framework to the vector--Jacobian products used by PyTorch's autograd engine.

</details>


### [124] [Beyond Backbone Backpropagation: A Decoupled Strategy for Efficient Transfer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13043)
*Daniel Vila-Cruz,Laura Morán-Fernández,Verónica Bolón-Canedo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a lightweight training strategy that adapts normalization layers, decouples feature extraction from classifier optimization, and uses a margin-based weighted loss to reduce training time and CO2 emissions while maintaining accuracy across multiple CNN and Transformer architectures on medical datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models achieve state-of-the-art image classification but face deployment challenges due to computational costs and energy demands, motivating the need for more efficient training methods.

Method: A lightweight training strategy that: (1) adapts normalization layers of the model to the new domain, (2) decouples feature extraction from classifier optimization by precomputing features only once, and (3) uses a redesigned classifier head with margin-based weighted loss to minimize ambiguity without end-to-end backpropagation.

Result: Evaluated on four CNN architectures (ResNet18, ResNet50, MobileNet, DenseNet121), three Transformer models (ViT, Swin, DeiT), and three medical datasets (Brain Cancer MRI, BreakHis, PatchCamelyon). The approach significantly reduces training time with only marginal accuracy trade-off, often matching or surpassing baseline performance, and reduces CO2 emissions by orders of magnitude.

Conclusion: The proposed method offers a practical and environmentally sustainable solution for resource-constrained clinical or prototyping environments.

Abstract: Deep learning models achieve state-of-the-art image classification but face deployment challenges due to computational costs and energy demands. We propose a lightweight training strategy that adapts normalization layers of the model to the new domain and decouples feature extraction from classifier optimization, reducing overhead by precomputing features only once. A redesigned classifier head with margin-based weighted loss further minimizes ambiguity without end-to-end backpropagation. Evaluated across four CNN architectures (ResNet18, ResNet50, MobileNet, DenseNet121), three Transformer models (ViT, Swin and DeiT) and three medical datasets (Brain Cancer MRI, BreakHis and PatchCamelyon), our approach significantly reduces the required training time with only a marginal accuracy trade-off, often matching or surpassing baseline performance. This efficiency translates to reducing CO2 by orders of magnitude, offering a practical and environmentally sustainable solution for resource-constrained clinical or prototyping environments.

</details>


### [125] [Federated Explainable Artificial Intelligence: Roles, Architectures, Evaluation, and Open Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13045)
*Masoume Gholizade,Fabrizio Ruffini,Pietro Ducange,Francesco Marcelloni*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey systematically reviews the emerging field of Federated Explainable AI (FedXAI), covering the transition of explainability from a post-hoc tool to an integral component of the FL lifecycle, proposing a taxonomy of methods, and identifying key challenges and gaps in evaluation practices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FL preserves data privacy but inherits the opacity of modern ML models; XAI addresses transparency needs. Their intersection (FedXAI) aims to jointly satisfy privacy and explainability, which is critical for high-stakes domains.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic literature review, propose a taxonomy classifying FedXAI methods by role of explainability, model/explainer types, explanation scope, integration level, FL settings, and data heterogeneity, and review evaluation practices.

Result: Explainability supports aggregation, personalization, robustness, coordination, and system-level decision making. The taxonomy organizes approaches from model-agnostic explanations to interpretable federated models and explainability-aware aggregation. The review identifies lack of standardized benchmarks and metrics for explanation quality, stability, privacy leakage, and computational overhead.

Conclusion: Key challenges include explainability under non-IID data, explanation-centric security threats, communication-efficient XAI, continual FedXAI, and integration of domain knowledge and regulatory constraints. The survey serves as a reference framework for designing trustworthy, transparent, and privacy-preserving federated AI systems.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) has emerged as a key paradigm for privacy-preserving collaborative model training across distributed and heterogeneous data sources. By keeping raw data local, FL addresses data confidentiality concerns, yet it does not resolve the opacity of modern machine learning models. In parallel, Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) has gained attention for improving transparency, trust, and accountability, particularly in high-stakes domains. Their intersection has given rise to Federated Explainable Artificial Intelligence (FedXAI) paradigm, which aims to jointly satisfy privacy and explainability requirements. This survey provides a systematic review of FedXAI, highlighting the transition of explainability from a post-hoc tool to an integral component of the FL lifecycle. We show how explainability supports aggregation, personalization, robustness, coordination, and system-level decision making. To organize the literature, we introduce a taxonomy that classifies FedXAI methods by the role of explainability, model and explainer types, explanation scope, integration level, FL settings, and data heterogeneity. We review approaches ranging from model-agnostic explanations to interpretable federated models and explainability-aware aggregation mechanisms. We also examine evaluation practices and discuss the lack of standardized benchmarks and metrics for measuring explanation quality, stability, privacy leakage, and computational overhead.
