Finally, we identify key challenges, including explainability under non-IID data, explanation-centric security threats, communication-efficient XAI, continual FedXAI, and the integration of domain knowledge and regulatory constraints. By consolidating existing work and identifying key gaps, this survey serves as a reference framework for designing trustworthy, transparent, and privacy-preserving federated AI systems.

</details>


### [126] [What Your Model Threw Away and Why You'll Want It Back: Masking, Fingerprinting, and Privacy from Discarded Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13046)
*Zachary P. Bradshaw*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a mathematical framework to characterize information discarded by machine learning models when inputs carry a Lie group action, introducing the null fiber and stabilizer concepts, proving dimensionality bounds via the preimage theorem, and providing spectral characterizations via Peter-Weyl theorem. Applications include data masking, model fingerprinting, and privacy-preserving computation, tested on SO(3) molecular property prediction and PSL(2,C) spherical image classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and quantify what symmetry-related information is discarded by machine learning models whose inputs are acted upon by Lie groups, enabling model transparency, privacy preservation, and security applications.

Method: Defines the null fiber N_G(f,x) as group elements whose inverse action is undetectable by f, and the stabilizer Stab_G(f) as the largest invariance subgroup. Uses the preimage theorem to prove null fiber dimension ≥ dim G - 1 at generic points. Applies Peter-Weyl theorem for spectral characterization via Fourier coefficient matrices. Computes null fiber elements efficiently via Newton iteration on the orbit map.

Result: Null fibers have dimension at least dim G - 1 at generic inputs regardless of architecture. For compact groups acting on themselves, Fourier analysis yields a spectral characterization. Newton iteration computes null fiber elements at cost comparable to a few gradient evaluations. Experiments validate on SO(3) molecular property prediction and PSL(2,C) spherical image classification, with applications to data masking, model fingerprinting, and privacy-preserving computation.

Conclusion: The framework provides a unified, architecture-agnostic approach to analyzing symmetry-invisible information in ML models, applicable to both classical neural networks and variational quantum circuits, with practical utility for data masking, model fingerprinting, and privacy preservation.

Abstract: We develop a framework for the information discarded by machine learning models whose inputs carry a Lie group action. Given a representation $π$ of a Lie group $G$ on a space $V$ and a learned function $f\colon V \to \mathbb{R}$, we define two objects measuring the symmetry invisible to $f$. The null fiber at a point $x \in V$ is the set $N_G(f,x) = \{g \in G : f(π(g^{-1}) \cdot x) = f(x)\}$ of group elements whose inverse action on $x$ is undetectable by $f$. When $N_G(f,x)$ is independent of $x$, it coincides with the stabilizer $\mathrm{Stab}_G(f)$, the largest subgroup of $G$ under which $f$ is invariant. For smooth maps to $\mathbb{R}$, the preimage theorem guarantees that null fibers have dimension at least $\dim G - 1$ at generic inputs, regardless of architecture. For compact groups acting on themselves, the Peter--Weyl theorem yields a spectral characterization of both objects in terms of the Fourier coefficient matrices of $f$. We show that null fiber elements can be computed efficiently via Newton iteration on the orbit map, at a cost comparable to a few gradient evaluations. Applications to data masking, model fingerprinting, and privacy-preserving computation are developed and tested experimentally on molecular property prediction under $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ and spherical image classification under the Möbius group $\mathrm{PSL}(2, \mathbb{C})$. The framework applies uniformly to classical neural networks and variational quantum circuits.

</details>


### [127] [Targeted Recovery of Weight-Space Mechanisms From Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13047)
*Antoine Vigouroux,Lee Sharkey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Targeted Parameter Decomposition (tPD) efficiently scales neural network decomposition by focusing only on components processing specific inputs, using a high-rank catch-all component for non-target data, reducing FLOPs by 93% in one case while maintaining faithfulness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Parameter Decomposition (PD) is computationally prohibitive for large models. The authors propose a targeted approach to reduce cost and risk when decomposing large neural networks.

Method: tPD introduces a high-rank catch-all component that handles all non-target data, enabling the decomposition to focus only on components processing specific inputs of interest (isolated prompts to large subtasks).

Result: tPD recovers reproducible, mechanistically faithful circuits in toy models and transformer LMs trained on The Pile. It extracts a CSS-only submodel of a 4-block transformer using only 7% of the FLOPs of the original decomposition. In a 12-block transformer, it surgically ablates and rewires memorized sequences with negligible side effects on other inputs.

Conclusion: Targeted PD is an efficient and scalable method for decomposing large neural networks into interpretable, faithful components, significantly reducing computational cost while maintaining mechanistic interpretability.

Abstract: Parameter decomposition (PD) decomposes neural networks into interpretable computational components that faithfully reflect the original network's operations. However, scaling PD to large models requires vast compute, making it a costly and risky endeavor. Here we propose targeted PD (tPD), which identifies only the components that process specific inputs of interest -- from isolated prompts to large subtasks -- by introducing a high-rank catch-all component that handles all non-target data. We validate tPD on toy models and on transformer language models trained on The Pile, where it recovers reproducible, mechanistically faithful circuits. We extract a CSS-only submodel of a 4-block transformer using 7% of the FLOPs of its published decomposition, and in a 12-block transformer we surgically ablate and rewire memorized sequences, with negligible side effects on other inputs.

</details>


### [128] [Uncertainty-Aware Sequential Decision Rules for Event-Triggered LLM Invocation in Streaming Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13048)
*Zhaohui Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper formalizes the LLM invocation problem in streaming inference pipelines as a risk-based sequential stopping problem, proving theoretical guarantees (optimality, regret bounds, convergence) and empirically validating on CMAPSS data against six baselines, showing sublinear regret and 92.9% diagnosis quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The central question of when to invoke an expensive LLM in streaming pipelines (where lightweight models handle most cases) has received limited formal treatment, despite the widespread practice of pairing fast models with LLMs for semantic understanding.

Method: Cast the LLM triggering decision as a risk-based sequential stopping problem where a policy fires when a risk functional exceeds a threshold; provides six theoretical proofs; expresses classical trigger families (event-triggered, optimal stopping, SPRT, CUSUM, Bayesian) as special cases.

Result: Empirically validated on CMAPSS turbofan data with real LLM calls: sublinear regret (α<1), 92.9% of 1600 LLM diagnoses achieved grounding score ≥0.75, anomaly-score-driven risk functions dominate alternatives by ~order-of-magnitude on Pareto AUC.

Conclusion: The proposed risk-based framework provides a principled, theoretically grounded approach to LLM invocation that outperforms baselines including RouteLLM-style routers and contextual bandits.

Abstract: Streaming inference pipelines increasingly pair lightweight fast models with Large Language Models (LLMs) that provide rich semantic understanding at substantial cost. The central question of when to invoke the LLM has received limited formal treatment. We cast this as a risk-based sequential stopping problem, where a trigger policy fires when a risk functional over the observation history exceeds a threshold. Within this framework, we prove six results: a minimum inter-event time bound excluding trigger chattering; optimality of threshold policies via smooth pasting; approximate SPRT guarantees under estimated parameters; O(sqrt(T log T)) regret for stationary streams, extending to O(sqrt((C_T + 1) T log T)) under C_T changepoints; O(1/sqrt(T)) convergence of online gradient descent for adaptive thresholds; and a calibration-to-miss-rate transfer inequality. Several classical trigger families, including event-triggered, optimal stopping, SPRT, CUSUM, and Bayesian triggers, can be expressed as special cases of this framework. On turbofan degradation data (CMAPSS) with real LLM calls, we empirically verify the theoretical assumptions, ablate the risk function design, compare against six baselines including a RouteLLM-style router and contextual bandits, and analyze cost sensitivity and LLM failure modes. The results confirm sublinear regret, with alpha < 1 for all principled triggers; high diagnostic quality, with 92.9 percent of 1600 LLM diagnoses reaching grounding score >= 0.75 under our rubric; and that anomaly-score-driven risk functions dominate alternatives by roughly an order of magnitude on the Pareto AUC.

</details>


### [129] [TSSM: Triaxial State Space Model for Global Station Weather Forecasting with Temporal-Variable-Historical Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13101)
*Songru Yang,Zili Liu,Tao Han,Ben Fei,Fenghua Ling,Lei Bai,Chang Liu,Xiangyang Ji,Zhenwei Shi,Zhengxia Zou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TSSM introduces a Triaxial State Space Model with a history-enhanced Temporal-Variable-Historical paradigm that stacks period-aligned historical weather data to capture long-term patterns, achieving SOTA on the Weather-5K dataset with 10% accuracy gain, 61% improvement in extreme event metrics, and robust performance under up to 80% missing observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GSWF methods rely too heavily on short-term look-back windows, leading to limited accuracy, poor extreme event prediction, and error accumulation, as short-term patterns cannot capture chaotic weather dynamics, especially under partial observations.

Method: TSSM uses a Triaxial State Space Model with a Temporal-Variable-Historical paradigm that stacks period-aligned historical weather batches. It performs temporal, variable, and historical scanning to capture axial temporal dependencies, variable correlations, and historical evolution, with hierarchical sharing to model seasonal to extreme events while alleviating misalignment across historical patterns.

Result: TSSM achieves SOTA on Weather-5K (10% accuracy gain, 61% extreme event improvement), 95% best/second-best on human-involved datasets, 37.5% gain at 240h, 103.5% gain under 48h×5 iterative setting, and retains >90% performance under 80% missing observations vs. <43% for baselines.

Conclusion: TSSM demonstrates that incorporating period-aligned historical data significantly improves forecasting accuracy and robustness, offering practical potential for reliable GSWF in global in-situ observation networks even under severe data sparsity.

Abstract: Global Station Weather Forecasting (GSWF) is pivotal for localized and extreme weather prediction over key regions. Despite efforts to exploit look-back windows, existing methods show limited accuracy gains and struggle with extreme events and error accumulation. These limitations stem from overreliance on short-term patterns, which are insufficient to capture chaotic weather dynamics, especially under partial observations. To address this problem, we propose a novel Triaxial State Space Model (TSSM) with a history-enhanced Temporal-VariableHistorical paradigm, which incorporates period-aligned historical weather data to compensate for long-term, large-scale periodic, and full-window weather patterns beyond the temporal lookback window. Specifically, TSSM stacks historical samples into period-aligned batches, where forecasting is causally supported by historical and current observations. Temporal, variable, and historical scanning are designed to capture axial temporal dependencies, variable correlations, and historical evolution. This structure is hierarchically shared to model seasonal to extreme events while alleviating misalignment across historical patterns. TSSM achieves SOTA performance on Weather-5K, the largest station weather dataset to date, with 10% and 61% gains in accuracy and extreme event metrics, and obtains 95% best or second-best results on human-involved datasets. Its advantages are more pronounced in long-horizon and iterative forecasting, reaching a 37.5% gain at 240h and up to 103.5% under a 48h times 5 iterative setting. Moreover, TSSM retains > 90% performance under up to 80% missing observations, compared with < 43% for baselines, demonstrating robustness and practical potential for reliable GSWF in global in-situ observation networks.

</details>


### [130] [Disentangling Knowledge States with Ability and Proficiency Modeling for Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13103)
*Duantengchuan Li,Yingqian Bi,Jinsong Chen,Rui Zhang,Mingwen Tong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PAKT decomposes student learning interactions into ability and proficiency phases, using a multi-branch Transformer with type-aware readout to improve knowledge tracing performance, achieving up to 1.33% AUC gain over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KT methods treat raw interaction sequences as a unified behavioral process, ignoring the phase-specific nature of learning behaviors where students transition from ability-building to proficiency-oriented learning.

Method: PAKT introduces a tailored decomposition mechanism to separate interactions into ability and proficiency phases, then employs a multi-branch Transformer with a type-aware readout module to jointly capture phase-specific and holistic knowledge states. A causal analysis reveals confounding bias in phase-agnostic models.

Result: Outperforms representative baselines on six public benchmarks with max AUC gain of 1.33% and average gain of 0.82%.

Conclusion: Modeling phase-specific learning behaviors improves KT performance; PAKT effectively captures the transition from ability-building to proficiency-oriented phases.

Abstract: Knowledge tracing (KT) aims to predict students' future performance by modeling their evolving knowledge states from historical interactions. Existing KT methods usually treat the raw interaction sequence as a unified behavioral process, overlooking the phase-specific nature of learning behaviors. Our preliminary observations show that students are more likely to correctly answer previously failed knowledge concepts after sufficient practice, suggesting a transition from ability-building to proficiency-oriented learning. Motivated by this, we propose Phase-Aware Knowledge Tracing (PAKT), a KT framework that decomposes student interactions into ability and proficiency phases based on the tailored decomposition mechanism. To effectively exploit the decomposed sequences, we design a multi-branch Transformer with a type-aware readout module to jointly capture phase-specific and holistic knowledge states. We further provide a causal analysis to reveal the confounding bias caused by entangling complex learning behaviors in phase-agnostic KT models. Extensive experiments on six public benchmarks demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms representative baselines, with a maximum AUC gain of 1.33% and an average gain of 0.82%.

</details>


### [131] [MetaPerch: Learning from metadata for bioacoustics foundation models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14072)
*Mustafa Chasmai,Vincent Dumoulin,Jenny Hamer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MetaPerch is a bioacoustic foundation model that leverages metadata (location, time, etc.) as auxiliary supervision signals alongside vocalizations, improving species identification and robustness to distribution/domain shifts for passive acoustic monitoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing bioacoustic models underutilize readily available metadata (e.g., location, time) from citizen science platforms like Xeno-Canto, which could provide auxiliary supervision to improve representation learning beyond vocalization-only supervision.

Method: MetaPerch incorporates metadata as auxiliary supervision signals during training, encouraging the model to learn species-metadata correlations. The study evaluates 9 diverse metadata sources across 17 bioacoustic datasets.

Result: MetaPerch achieves strong species identification performance across multiple challenging domains, with improved generalization to species distribution shifts and acoustic domain shifts in real-world PAM settings.

Conclusion: Metadata-based auxiliary losses provide additional information beyond vocalizations, leading to richer and more robust representations. This approach is promising for deployment in real-world passive acoustic monitoring.

Abstract: Bioacoustic foundation models rely on large-scale citizen science platforms like Xeno-Canto for geographically and ecologically diverse data. Recent work has shown that supervision alone can produce SotA species detection models when trained on this large-scale data -- however, there remains unutilized potential in the form of recording metadata readily available within these community-driven data hubs. In this work, we explore the use of metadata -- such as location and time -- as auxiliary supervision signals, allowing the model to leverage species-metadata correlations in its learned representation. Auxiliary metadata losses provide additional information beyond vocalizations alone that can encourage a richer, more robust representation that generalizes better to species distribution and acoustic domain shifts -- important challenges for deployment in real-world passive acoustic monitoring (PAM) settings. We introduce MetaPerch, a new foundation model that achieves strong species identification performance across multiple challenging domains and present an extensive empirical study of the effects of 9 diverse metadata sources on 17 bioacoustic datasets.

</details>


### [132] [STKAN: Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks for Spatio-Temporal Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13108)
*Sicong Lai,Yuehong Hu,Siru Zhong,Si Qiao,Yuxuan Liang,Guangyin Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STKAN introduces Taylor-polynomial Kolmogorov-Arnold Network modules into spatio-temporal forecasting, achieving competitive performance on traffic benchmarks by enhancing nonlinear function approximation in spatial and temporal token mixing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world traffic data exhibit heterogeneous spatial correlations and nonlinear temporal dynamics, and existing approaches focus on architectural innovations (graph, attention, decomposition) while the influence of the underlying nonlinear function approximator has been comparatively overlooked.

Method: STKAN integrates Taylor-polynomial Kolmogorov-Arnold Network (KAN) modules into spatial and temporal token mixing. It first constructs high-level spatial representations via a learnable soft node-group assignment mechanism, applies group-wise spatial mixing, models temporal dependencies over compressed sequences, and employs spatial/temporal self-attention for long-range interactions.

Result: Competitive performance on five traffic forecasting benchmarks, outperforming the evaluated MLP-based variant in tested settings.

Conclusion: The design of nonlinear function approximators can serve as a useful complement to architectural design in spatio-temporal forecasting.

Abstract: Real-world traffic data exhibit heterogeneous spatial correlations and nonlinear temporal dynamics, posing substantial challenges for accurate spatio-temporal forecasting. Existing approaches have developed increasingly sophisticated graph, attention, and decomposition architectures, while the influence of the underlying nonlinear function approximator has received comparatively less attention. In this work, we propose STKAN, a spatio-temporal forecasting architecture that introduces Taylor-polynomial Kolmogorov--Arnold Network modules into spatial and temporal token mixing. STKAN first constructs high-level spatial representations through a learnable soft node-group assignment mechanism, applies group-wise spatial mixing, and subsequently models temporal dependencies over the compressed sequence. Spatial and temporal self-attention layers are further employed to capture long-range interactions. Experiments on five traffic forecasting benchmarks show that STKAN achieves competitive performance and performs better than the evaluated MLP-based variant in the tested settings. These results suggest that the design of nonlinear function approximators can serve as a useful complement to architectural design in spatio-temporal forecasting.

</details>


### [133] [A Hybrid Mamba for Audio-Visual Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13110)
*Yi Wang,Yinfeng Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Samba replaces traditional CNN/RNN-based backbones in audio-visual navigation with a hybrid Mamba architecture featuring Mamba State Encoder (M-SE) and Audio Mamba Encoder (AME), achieving 11.3% higher success rate on Matterport3D with lower computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CNN/RNN-based navigation backbones have remained unchanged for over five years since 2020, and are inadequate for representing dynamic multimodal sequences efficiently.

Method: Proposes Samba with two key components: (1) Mamba State Encoder (M-SE) using adaptive selection to replace conventional GRUs for temporal aggregation, and (2) Audio Mamba Encoder (AME) to overcome CNN limitations in capturing global time-frequency dependencies in spectrograms.

Result: On Matterport3D, SR improves by 11.3% over SOTA; performance gains are even larger on the Replica dataset with finer scene structures. The model also excels in generalization to unheard sound sources and unseen scenes.

Conclusion: Modernized architectural reconstruction with Mamba unlocks stronger embodied representation at lower computational cost, offering a robust pathway for paradigm evolution in audio-visual navigation.

Abstract: Since the paradigm centered on convolutional neural networks and recurrent architectures was established in 2020, the fundamental backbone networks for audio-visual navigation have undergone no essential changes for more than five years, making them inadequate to support efficient representation of dynamic multimodal sequences. This paper proposes Samba(A Hybrid Mamba for Audio-Visual Navigation). It uses the adaptive selection-enabled Mamba State Encoder (M-SE) to replace conventional GRUs for temporal aggregation, and constructs an Audio Mamba Encoder (AME) to remedy the limitations of convolutional operators in capturing global time-frequency dependencies in spectrograms. Experiments demonstrate that Samba exhibits exceptional generalization performance when facing unheard sound sources and unseen scenes. On the Matterport3D dataset, it improves the navigation success rate (SR) by 11.3\% compared with existing state-of-the-art models, and the performance gain is even more pronounced on the Replica dataset, which features finer scene structures. Such modernized architectural reconstruction unlocks stronger embodied representation capabilities at a lower computational cost, thereby providing a highly robust technical pathway for paradigm evolution in the field of audio-visual navigation.

</details>


### [134] [CoDiffGRN: Rethinking Gene Regulatory Network Inference via the BEELINE-KGC Benchmark and Co-evolutionary Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13120)
*Jiaze Song,Runhao Zhao,Minghao Xu,Bin Cui,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a new benchmark (BENCHMARK) for inductive, ranking-centric GRN inference and proposes METHOD, a co-evolutionary discrete diffusion framework with TF-ALL Subgraph Sampling, achieving SOTA performance in novel regulatory discovery from single-cell data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GRN inference approaches misalign with real-world needs: researchers want high-confidence novel regulatory interactions under inductive settings, but current benchmarks use transductive splits with global classification metrics, and models fail to generalize to unseen genes.

Method: Reformulates GRN inference as an inductive, ranking-centric graph completion problem. Proposes METHOD, a co-evolutionary discrete diffusion framework jointly modeling discretized gene expression states and regulatory interactions, with TF-ALL Subgraph Sampling (TASS) for scalable training.

Result: METHOD establishes new SOTA on the proposed benchmark, significantly outperforming existing methods in novel regulatory discovery; ablation studies confirm design effectiveness.

Conclusion: The proposed benchmark and co-evolutionary diffusion framework bridge the gap between practical needs and existing GRN inference methods, enabling robust inductive generalization and improved top-ranked regulatory discovery.

Abstract: Inferring gene regulatory networks (GRNs) from single-cell transcriptomic data is crucial for biological discovery, yet existing approaches suffer from a fundamental misalignment with real-world needs. Researchers typically seek a small set of high-confidence regulatory interactions for experimental validation, often involving previously unseen genes. However, current benchmarks rely on transductive splits with global classification metrics, while prevailing models struggle to generalize under inductive settings. To bridge this gap, we reformulate GRN inference as an inductive, ranking-centric graph completion problem and introduce \textbf{\benchmark}, a new benchmark that incorporates an inductive gene-holdout split together with knowledge graph completion metrics to better evaluate top-ranked predictions. Building on this, we propose \textbf{\method}, the first co-evolutionary discrete diffusion framework that jointly models biologically coherent discretized gene expression states and regulatory interactions for robust inductive generalization and improved top-ranked regulatory discovery. We further introduce TF-ALL Subgraph Sampling (TASS) for scalable training. Extensive experiments on {\benchmark} show that {\method} establishes new state-of-the-art performance, significantly outperforming existing methods in novel regulatory discovery, and ablation studies further verify the effectiveness of our design.

</details>


### [135] [ShortOPD: Recovering Pruned LLMs with Short-to-Long On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13124)
*Qingyu Zhang,Qianhao Yuan,Hongyu Lin,Yaojie Lu,Xianpei Han,Le Sun,Xiang Li,Ming Xu,Jiarui Li,Xiuyin Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Structured pruning of LLMs hurts free-form generation more than recognition tasks. The paper identifies suffix repetition as the main failure mode and proposes ShortOPD, a short-to-long On-Policy Distillation schedule that allocates training budget to non-repetitive effective prefix lengths, achieving ~9x recovery in generation scores and matching long-rollout performance with 71% fewer tokens and 4x less training time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured pruning compresses LLMs efficiently but collapses on free-form generation tasks that deployment requires, while existing validation mostly uses multiple-choice recognition tasks. Two key observations: (1) greedy Pass@1 nearly vanishes but Pass@k recovers with repeated sampling (generations are demoted, not erased); (2) failure manifests primarily as suffix repetition. This gap motivates a recovery method specifically targeting generation quality.

Method: Proposes ShortOPD (short-to-long On-Policy Distillation): (1) uses the pre-compression model as a frozen teacher to provide dense token-level supervision on the compressed model's own on-policy states; (2) detects teacher-confirmed repetitive suffixes during rollouts; (3) treats the surviving non-repetitive prefix as each rollout's effective length; (4) allocates future rollout budgets to these effective lengths that the policy can currently handle.

Result: ShortOPD raises compressed model's score to ~9x its unrecovered value and 1.6–4.4x over standard recovery recipes (SFT w/o KD, KD, SeqKD). It matches a fixed 8192-token rollout horizon within 2 points using a quarter of training time (8.5 vs 35.9 hours) and 71% fewer rollout tokens.

Conclusion: ShortOPD provides a practical, efficient recipe to recover free-form generation quality after structured pruning, moving beyond marginal gains on perplexity and multiple-choice benchmarks toward deployment-ready performance.

Abstract: Structured pruning is a hardware-friendly way to compress LLMs, but it is mostly validated on multiple-choice recognition tasks, while the same compressed checkpoints can collapse on the free-form generation that deployment actually requires. Two observations trace this gap. First, greedy \textsc{pass}@$1$ nearly vanishes after compression, yet \textsc{pass}@$k$ recovers substantially under repeated sampling: useful generations are demoted, not erased. Second, the recoverable regime fails mainly through suffix repetition. Recovery should therefore train on the compressed model's own on-policy states with dense token-level supervision, which On-Policy Distillation (OPD) provides by reusing the pre-compression model as a frozen teacher. However, long on-policy rollouts spend early recovery budget on low-information repetitive suffixes, delaying loss descent. To mitigate this waste, we propose \textbf{\shortopd}, a short-to-long OPD schedule that detects teacher-confirmed repetitive suffixes, treats the surviving prefix as each rollout's effective length, and allocates future rollout budgets to the effective lengths the policy can currently use. Across math, code, and open-ended generation, \shortopd\ raises the compressed model's score to about $9\times$ its unrecovered value and $1.6$--$4.4\times$ standard recovery recipes (SFT w/o KD, KD, and SeqKD), and it matches a fixed $8192$-token rollout horizon within two points using a quarter of the training time ($8.5$ vs.\ $35.9$ hours) and $71\%$ fewer rollout tokens. We hope this recipe helps move structured pruning beyond marginal gains on perplexity and multiple-choice benchmarks, a step closer to deployment-ready generation quality.

</details>


### [136] [HEDGEHOG: Hierarchical Evaluation of Drug Generators Through Rigorous Filtration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13155)
*Daria A. Ryabchenko,Pavel Gurevich,Shamil Kadyrov,Daria Frolova,Kseniia Fedisheva,Sergei A. Nikolenko,Alexander Shapeev,Marina A. Pak*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Only 0.65% of molecules generated by 23 molecular generators pass a six-stage medicinal chemistry filtration benchmark (HEDGEHOG), exposing a critical gap between standard evaluation metrics and practical drug discovery requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation metrics for molecular generators produce false positives and do not reflect medicinal plausibility, synthesis feasibility, and multiparameter design constraints needed for real-world drug discovery.

Method: Introduces HEDGEHOG, a six-stage filtration benchmark inspired by industrial hit identification: (i) preprocessing, (ii) physicochemical screening, (iii) structural alerts/sanity checks, (iv) synthesis feasibility, (v) docking/binding affinity, (vi) 3D pose/interaction checks. Evaluated 23 generators across 3 model classes under a standardized protocol.

Result: Only 0.65% of 230,000 generated molecules survive all six filtration stages, demonstrating that molecules acceptable on isolated criteria rarely satisfy medicinal chemistry, synthesis, docking, and 3D pose filters simultaneously.

Conclusion: Current molecular generators have a central limitation: they fail to produce compounds that meet the multifaceted constraints of medicinal chemistry, motivating the need for more realistic evaluation benchmarks like HEDGEHOG.

Abstract: Generative molecular models can support early drug discovery by proposing new candidate compounds de novo. In practice, useful candidates must balance target-relevant activity, synthetic accessibility, physicochemical properties, and other multiparameter design constraints. However, metrics commonly used to evaluate molecular generators only weakly reflect whether the generated compounds are medicinally plausible and suitable for downstream computation. This can produce false positives in model evaluation, incorrect assumptions, and inefficient use of computational resources. We introduce HEDGEHOG, a unified six-stage filtration benchmark that is inspired by industrial hit identification workflows: (i) preprocessing; (ii) physicochemical descriptor screening; (iii) structural alerts and graph-sanity checks; (iv) synthesis feasibility; (v) docking and binding affinity estimation; and (vi) three-dimensional pose and interaction checks. We evaluate 23 molecular generators across three model classes under a standardized protocol. Across 230,000 generated molecules, only 0.65% of initial molecules survive all stages. Our results expose a central limitation of current molecular generators: molecules that appear acceptable under isolated criteria rarely satisfy medicinal chemistry, synthesis, docking, and 3D pose filters simultaneously.

</details>


### [137] [SteinGate: Tail-Sensitive Safe Reinforcement Learning via Stein Discrepancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13175)
*Yassine Chemingui,Chenhua Fan,Honghao Wei,Janardhan Rao Doppa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SteinGate is a boundary-aware distributional safety certificate that uses Kernelized Stein Discrepancy to detect catastrophic tail events in safe RL, dynamically switching between reward optimization and recovery behavior based on cost tail consistency with a safe reference distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional safe RL bounds expected cumulative costs, which fails to detect rare but catastrophic tail events.

Method: SteinGate replaces fragile tail fitting with a robust consistency check using Kernelized Stein Discrepancy while accounting for boundary atoms from clipped costs, providing a non-parametric safety certificate to dynamically adapt the learning regime.

Result: SteinGate significantly reduces both the frequency and severity of constraint violations during training while maintaining competitive returns relative to state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: Boundary-aware distributional safety certificates can effectively mitigate catastrophic tail events in safe RL without sacrificing reward performance.

Abstract: Safe reinforcement learning typically enforces safety by bounding expected cumulative costs, a criterion that often fails to detect rare but catastrophic tail events. To overcome these limitations, this paper introduces SteinGate, a boundary-aware distributional safety certificate that replaces fragile tail fitting with a robust consistency check using Kernelized Stein Discrepancy while accounting for boundary atoms induced by clipped costs. SteinGate evaluates whether observed policy rollout costs remain consistent with a safe reference distribution, providing a non-parametric safety certificate. This certificate is used to dynamically adapt the learning regime: favoring reward-improving policy updates when rollouts remain consistent with the safe reference and switching to recovery behavior when the cost tail deviates. Experiments on continuous-control benchmarks demonstrate that SteinGate significantly reduces both the frequency and severity of constraint violations during training while maintaining competitive returns relative to state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [138] [Concurrent Image Understanding and Generation: Self-Correcting Coupled Markov Jump Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13188)
*Minh-Quan Le,Armand Comas,Alexandros Lattas,Stylianos Moschoglou,Pedro Vélez,Amit Raj,Aaron Germuth,Thabo Beeler,Dimitris Samaras,Di Qiu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces SC-CMJP (Self-Correcting Coupled Markov Jump Processes) and its training-free sampler CO₂Jump for joint multimodal generation, where text and image modalities are coupled within each denoising step via cross-modal attention-weighted confidence scores and a remasking mechanism that retracts commitments upon cross-modal contradiction. The method achieves state-of-the-art performance on image understanding, editing, and visual reasoning tasks, with performance scaling monotonically with denoising steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human cognition does not separate understanding and generation; modalities (speech and drawing) evolve together, each reshaping the other. Existing samplers for Masked Diffusion Models either decode text and image interleavedly or update them independently in parallel branches, sharing only previous-step history but not the other modality's latest decisions within the same step. Combined with MDMs' inability to remask, cross-modal contradictions are neither detected nor repaired.

Method: SC-CMJP defines transition rates for one modality as functionals of the other modality's confidence score, weighted by cross-modal attention. A remasking jump retracts commitments when cross-modal evidence contradicts them. CO₂Jump is a training-free single-pass sampler implementing this framework.

Result: CO₂Jump achieves best joint performance for image understanding, image editing, and visual reasoning (maze and nonogram solving). Performance scales monotonically with the number of denoising steps, showing that cross-modal coupling benefits compound across the trajectory. Three large-scale corpora (JEdit-1M, JMaze-200K, JNono-200K) were created and released.

Conclusion: Coupled cross-modal correction within the same denoising step, enabled by remasking, significantly improves joint multimodal generation. The compounding benefits across the trajectory suggest that tighter intra-step coupling is a promising direction for multimodal generative models.

Abstract: Human cognition does not separate understanding and generation. A teacher at a whiteboard speaks and draws $\textit{together}$, each modality reshapes the other. In this paper, we bring this coupled loop to artificial systems. Masked Diffusion Models (MDMs) are ideally suited to this task, yet existing samplers either decode text and image interleavedly or independently update them in parallel branches that share only previous-step history, but not the other modality's latest decisions $\textit{within}$ the same step; combined with MDMs' inability to remask, cross-modal contradictions are neither detected nor repaired. We introduce $\textbf{Self-Correcting Coupled Markov Jump Processes (SC-CMJP)}$, a framework in which one modality's transition rates are functionals of the other modality's confidence score, as weighted by cross-modal attention. Furthermore, a remasking jump retracts commitments the moment cross-modal evidence turns against them. In conjunction with SC-CMJP, we introduce $\texttt{CO}_\texttt{2}\texttt{Jump}$ (Self-$\underline{\text{CO}}$rrecting $\underline{\text{CO}}$upled $\underline{\text{Jump}}$), a novel training-free single-pass sampler for joint multimodal geneneration. For training and evaluation purposes, we have created and will release three large-scale joint multimodal generation corpora: $\text{JEdit-1M}$, $\text{JMaze-200K}$, $\text{JNono-200K}$, with matching in- and out-of-distribution benchmarks. $\texttt{CO}_\texttt{2}\texttt{Jump}$ achieves best joint performance for image understanding and editing as well as visual reasoning (maze and nonogram solving). The performance of the sampler scales monotonically with the number of denoising steps, evidence that the benefits of cross-modal coupling $\textit{compound}$ across the trajectory. Project page: https://coupled-jump.github.io

</details>


### [139] [EMAGN: Efficient Multi-Attention Graph Network via Learned Clustering for Scalable Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13241)
*Mingxing Xu,Rakesh Chowdary Machineni,Ke Liu,Xi Cheng,Chengqi Lu,Xin Hu,Lyuhao Chen,Xiangyu Li,Junwei You,Oliver Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EMAGN linearizes spatial self-attention using learned clustering matrices (inspired by Gaussian filtering), reducing complexity from O(N²d) to O(NMd), achieving near-GMAN accuracy with 32% faster training, 38% faster inference, 58% less GPU memory, and operating where full-attention GMAN runs out of memory on 11 GB GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-attention mechanisms in traffic forecasting achieve state-of-the-art performance but suffer from quadratic O(N²d) complexity, limiting scalability and GPU memory feasibility.

Method: EMAGN linearises spatial attention via two learned clustering matrices C_k and C_v that adaptively group key/value vectors into M super-clusters, inspired by fast high-dimensional Gaussian filtering theory, reducing complexity to O(NMd).

Result: On PEMS-BAY and METR-LA: EMAGN achieves within 2.7-3.2% MAE of full-attention GMAN; reduces training time by 32%, inference by 38%, GPU memory by 58%; at K=16 heads, GMAN runs out of memory on 11 GB GPU while EMAGN operates; surpasses Linformer and Performer in accuracy and efficiency.

Conclusion: Adaptive clustering-based linearised attention is an effective, scalable alternative to full self-attention for traffic forecasting, enabling larger model configurations under fixed GPU memory budgets while maintaining competitive accuracy.

Abstract: Traffic forecasting is highly challenging due to complex and nonlinear spatial and temporal dependencies. Self-attention mechanisms have been widely adopted to model dynamic and long-range dependencies, achieving state-of-the-art performance, but suffer from limited scalability due to quadratic computational and memory complexity. To address this, we propose an Efficient Multi-Attention Graph Network (EMAGN) that linearises the spatial attention mechanism itself, inspired by the theory of fast high-dimensional Gaussian filtering. Two learned clustering matrices C_k and C_v adaptively group key and value vectors into M super-clusters, reducing complexity from O(N^2 d) to O(NMd) without sacrificing the flexibility of attention for dynamic dependency modelling. Experimental results on PEMS-BAY and METR-LA show that EMAGN achieves accuracy within 2.7-3.2% MAE of full-attention GMAN while reducing training time by 32%, inference time by 38%, and GPU memory by 58%. Critically, at K=16 attention heads, full-attention GMAN runs out of memory on a standard 11 GB GPU entirely while EMAGN continues to operate, demonstrating a categorical expansion of feasible model configurations. EMAGN also surpasses Linformer and Performer in both accuracy and efficiency within the same backbone, owing to its traffic-network-aware adaptive clustering.

</details>


### [140] [Reassessing Muon for Matrix Factorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13246)
*Ali Parviz,Gal Mishne,Alex Cloninger*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Muon does not consistently outperform AdamW on low-rank matrix factorization, revealing that some of its previously reported advantages are sensitive to hyperparameter choices and may stem from scale, architecture, or data rather than its update rule.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To isolate Muon's optimizer effects from confounding factors like scale, architecture, and data, since it's unclear which of its advantages stem from the update rule itself versus artifacts of modern deep network training.

Method: Controlled comparison of Muon against carefully tuned adaptive baselines (e.g., AdamW) on a simple, well-understood, spectrally structured problem: low-rank matrix factorization.

Result: Muon does not consistently outperform AdamW in this setting; several previously reported advantages are sensitive to hyperparameter choices.

Conclusion: Spectrum-aware orthogonalization is not universally beneficial; modern optimizers should be evaluated on controlled problems in addition to end-to-end benchmarks.

Abstract: Muon has recently emerged as a strong optimizer for large-scale deep learning, where it reshapes gradient updates through approximate orthogonalization and has been reported to outperform Adam and AdamW in large language model training. Its empirical success has motivated a growing body of theoretical work that interprets Muon as steepest descent under the spectral norm. Yet it remains unclear which of Muon's advantages stem from its update rule itself and which are artifacts of the scale, architecture, and data of modern deep networks. In this work, we isolate the optimizer from these confounding factors by studying Muon on a simple, well-understood, and spectrally structured problem: low-rank matrix factorization. Through a controlled comparison against carefully tuned adaptive baselines, we find that Muon does not consistently outperform AdamW in this setting and that several previously reported advantages are sensitive to hyperparameter choices. Our results provide a more nuanced picture of when spectrum-aware orthogonalization is beneficial and argue for evaluating modern optimizers on controlled problems in addition to end-to-end benchmarks.

</details>


### [141] [Deconstructing Actor-Critic: A Large-scale Empirical Study of Design Components for Practitioners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13274)
*Haseeb Shah,Lingwei Zhu,Adam White,Martha White*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes over 33,000 experiments on actor-critic algorithms for real-world control tasks (using a water treatment plant as testbed), finding that common defaults like Gaussian action distributions with pathwise gradient estimators are unreliable, while bounded distributions with adaptive update schedules are robust across settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning is increasingly applied to real-world systems where reliability is critical and tuning budgets are limited, but the impact of component-level design decisions in actor-critic algorithms on variability and hyperparameter sensitivity is poorly understood.

Method: Using a control task derived from a real water treatment plant, the authors conducted over 33,000 experiments to systematically analyze how actor-critic design components (policy update method, action distribution representation, gradient estimation, update frequency relative to value estimator) affect variability across runs and sensitivity to hyperparameters.

Result: Common defaults such as Gaussian action distributions with pathwise gradient estimators are among the least reliable configurations. Bounded distributions with adaptive update schedules remain robust across a wide range of settings.

Conclusion: These empirical findings provide guidance for practitioners across scientific and engineering domains on making component-level decisions when adapting actor-critic methods to new real-world control settings.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning is increasingly being considered for controlling real-world systems, from fusion plasma and autonomous vehicles to drug discovery and drinking water treatment, where reliability is essential and tuning budgets are limited. Actor-critic algorithms share a set of design decisions, such as how the policy is updated, how it represents the distribution over actions, how its gradient is estimated, and how often it is updated relative to the value estimator. Using a control task derived from a real water treatment plant, we analyze over 33,000 experiments to determine how these components affect variability across runs and sensitivity to hyperparameters. Common defaults, such as Gaussian action distributions with pathwise gradient estimators, are among the least reliable configurations, whereas bounded distributions with adaptive update schedules remain robust across a wide range of settings. These findings offer empirical guidance to practitioners across scientific and engineering domains for understanding and making component-level decisions when adapting actor-critic methods to new real-world control settings.

</details>


### [142] [Tabular Foundation Models for Discrete Choice Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13314)
*Liu Liu,Dan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) struggle with discrete choice due to set-valued data and preference heterogeneity; a reformulation encoding choice-set dependence and individual heterogeneity outperforms hierarchical Bayesian estimation by 8% in log-likelihood and 3.6% in hit rate, running 16× faster, with largest gains in medium-data regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether tabular foundation models (TFMs) can be effectively applied to discrete choice demand estimation, a core framework in marketing and operations, given that TFMs assume row-independent observations while discrete choice is inherently set-valued and characterized by persistent consumer preference heterogeneity.

Method: Proposes a reformulation that encodes both choice-set dependence and individual heterogeneity within a row-based learning framework; evaluated on yogurt scanner panel data; fine-tuning on population choice data for consumers with shallow purchase histories.

Result: Individual-level heterogeneity encoding is the dominant driver of predictive accuracy. Best reformulation outperforms hierarchical Bayesian estimation by 8% in holdout log-likelihood and 3.6% in hit rate, with 16× speedup. Largest advantage in medium-data regime (10–40 purchase occasions per consumer). Fine-tuning benefits consumers with shallow purchase histories.

Conclusion: Establishes a principled approach for applying foundation models to consumer choice problems, demonstrating that TFMs can be adapted to structured choice settings by properly encoding the structure of the underlying data-generating process.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) generate predictions on structured data via in-context learning, without task-specific estimation. We ask whether TFMs can be effectively applied to discrete choice, a central demand estimation framework in marketing and operations, and find that directly applying TFMs yields limited performance. The gap is structural: TFMs assume row-independent observations, whereas discrete choice is inherently set-valued and subject to persistent consumer preference heterogeneity. We propose a reformulation that encodes both choice-set dependence and individual heterogeneity within a row-based learning framework. Evaluated on a yogurt scanner panel, individual-level heterogeneity encoding is the dominant driver of predictive accuracy. The best reformulation outperforms hierarchical Bayesian estimation by 8\% in holdout log-likelihood and 3.6\% in hit rate, running 16 times faster, a practical advantage for large-scale demand estimation. The advantage is largest in the medium-data regime (10--40 purchase occasions per consumer), where parametric Bayesian shrinkage most distorts estimates for atypical consumers. Fine-tuning on population choice data provides additional gains for consumers with shallow purchase histories, where in-context learning has limited individual-specific signal to condition on. These results establish a principled approach for applying foundation models to consumer choice problems more broadly.

</details>


### [143] [Accuracy-Preserving Stability Regularization for Large-Scale Retail Demand Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13331)
*Jize Li,Jiani He,Dishu Yang,Dingyan Shang,Jingjing Liu,Shiqi Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a training-time penalty to stabilize retail demand forecasts across consecutive time steps, improving forecast stability without significantly hurting point accuracy, outperforming post-hoc smoothing on M5 demand series.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Point-error objectives do not constrain abrupt movement between adjacent forecasts, and post-hoc smoothing acts only after model fitting, failing to address forecast-path instability during training.

Method: A stability-aware hybrid model with a training-time penalty on consecutive within-series movement is introduced, combined with a temporal-structured pipeline including recent-demand embeddings, calendar, price, hierarchy, item, and store features.

Result: On M5 demand series at 1000, 3000, and 4000 scales, the model improves Forecast Stability Score over XGBoost by 6.91%, 6.66%, and 7.68%, while RMSE changes stay within 0.72%. Post-hoc smoothing reduces movement more but with larger RMSE cost.

Conclusion: Extends forecast evaluation from point-error minimization to an accuracy-stability trade-off perspective for operational retail forecasting.

Abstract: Retail demand forecasts are reused across replenishment, capacity, labor, and transportation planning cycles. Point-error objectives do not constrain abrupt movement between adjacent forecasts, while post-hoc smoothing acts only after model fitting. We ask whether a training-time penalty on consecutive within-series movement can improve horizontal forecast-path stability without materially changing point accuracy. The penalty is evaluated in a temporal-structured pipeline combining recent-demand embeddings with calendar, price, hierarchy, item, and store features. On selected M5 demand series at 1000, 3000, and 4000-series scales, the stability-aware hybrid model improves Forecast Stability Score over XGBoost by 6.91%, 6.66%, and 7.68%, respectively, while RMSE changes remain within 0.72% across three random seeds. Post-hoc exponential smoothing attains lower raw movement but incurs a larger RMSE cost; training-time regularization preserves more point accuracy and performs favorably under normalized stability. These findings extend forecast evaluation from point-error minimization toward an accuracy-stability trade-off perspective for operational retail forecasting.

</details>


### [144] [Agora: Collective and Permissionless Internet-Scale Pretraining of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13332)
*Gil Avraham,Violetta Shevchenko,Hadi Mohaghegh Dolatabadi,Karol Pajak,James Snewin,Harry Xi,Rodney O'Donnell,Thalaiyasingam Ajanthan,Sameera Ramasinghe,Chamin Hewa Koneputugodage,Shamane Siriwardhana,Alexander Long*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Agora enables training large language models (e.g., Pluralis-8B, an 8.6B-parameter model) across heterogeneous, preemptible consumer GPUs connected via the internet, achieving 63% efficiency of centralized H100 clusters through pipeline-parallel sharding and fault-tolerant collective operations, with no single party holding full model weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier LLM training is restricted to datacenters with homogeneous accelerators and high-speed interconnects, concentrating development among few entities. A vast pool of consumer/professional GPUs remains unused for training due to heterogeneity, preemptibility, and internet-grade connectivity.

Method: Agora combines bandwidth-efficient pipeline-parallel model sharding over internet links with multi-party, fault-tolerant collective operations. Each participant holds only one model stage; no single party possesses full weights. The system integrates communication-efficient parallelism, asynchronous optimization, and fault-tolerant systems design.

Result: Demonstrated Pluralis-8B, an open pretraining run of an 8.6B-parameter model on 500B tokens of FineWeb-Edu over 40 days by 330 contributor nodes (mostly consumer GPUs). Sustained ~170k tokens/s and 4.2 tokens/TFLOP (63% of centralized H100 baseline), converging close to a centralized reference run.

Conclusion: Agora enables Protocol Learning—collectively trained and owned models—paving the way for open-source frontier training with economic sustainability, leveraging otherwise idle distributed compute resources.

Abstract: Training large language models at the multi-billion to trillion parameter scale is confined to datacenters, where data-parallel (DP) and model-parallel (MP) techniques presume homogeneous accelerators, high-speed interconnects, and a single orchestrating entity. Frontier model development is thereby concentrated among the few groups able to assemble such clusters. Meanwhile, an enormous pool of compute remains unusable for training: consumer and professional GPUs that are heterogeneous, preemptible, individually owned, and connected only by the internet. We present Agora, a system that makes efficient use of this compute. Agora combines bandwidth-efficient pipeline-parallel model sharding over internet-grade links with multi-party, fault-tolerant collective operations. Each participant holds only one stage of the model, and no single party ever possesses the full weights. We term this setup Protocol Learning: it enables collectively trained, collectively owned models, opening a path to open-source frontier training with economic sustainability. This report presents the outcome of a research effort spanning communication-efficient parallelism, asynchronous optimization, and fault-tolerant systems design. It culminates in the first demonstration of its kind: Pluralis-8B, an open, permissionless pretraining run of an 8.6B-parameter model on 500B tokens of FineWeb-Edu. The model was trained over 40 days by 330 contributor nodes, predominantly consumer GPUs on internet connections, joining and leaving throughout. The run sustained ~170k tokens/s and 4.2 tokens per TFLOP of pooled compute, 63% of the efficiency of a centralized H100 baseline, and converged to within a small margin of a centralized reference run.

</details>


### [145] [Weight Feedback Computes the Jacobian Transpose Locally in Modern Deep Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13380)
*Junlong Shen,Xingyu Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Predictive Coding's error routing dependency on autograd Jacobian-transpose (Jᵀ) can be largely avoided by factoring it into three locally available terms for layers with frozen normalization statistics. The resulting WF-Act-PC method removes the autograd backward pass, improves accuracy with depth, and matches/exceeds tuned backpropagation on deeper CIFAR-10 architectures and Tiny-ImageNet while trailing on deeper CIFAR-100 VGG cells.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predictive Coding (PC) offers a biologically motivated alternative to backpropagation, but still relies on autograd Jacobian-transpose (Jᵀ) product for routing error between layers—the last non-local operation in PC. The authors aim to show this dependency is largely avoidable.

Method: For any layer f(x)=Act(Norm(L(x))) with frozen normalization statistics, the exact Jᵀ factors into three locally available terms: Jᵀv = Lᵀ(s ⊙ σ'(z) ⊙ v), where σ' is activation derivative, z is pre-activation, and s=γ/σ_run is normalization gain. Substituting this identity into PC yields WF-Act-PC, which removes the autograd backward pass from error transport. The method relies on three assumptions: weight symmetry (Lᵀ mirrors forward operator), soft spectral-norm control, and nearest-neighbour approximation for MaxPool.

Result: On CIFAR-10/100 (50 epochs, 5 seeds), WF-Act-PC is the only PC method whose accuracy improves with depth, surpassing iPC by 2.7-22.3 pp on CIFAR-10. With both methods tuned per architecture, it matches or exceeds tuned backpropagation on deeper CIFAR-10 architectures (VGG-9: 93.57% vs. 92.43%; ResNet-18: 92.76% vs. 91.54%) and on Tiny-ImageNet, while trailing tuned BP on deeper CIFAR-100 VGG cells.

Conclusion: The autograd Jᵀ dependency in Predictive Coding can be largely eliminated by factoring it into locally available terms for a broad class of layers. The resulting WF-Act-PC method closes the transport gap, improves accuracy with depth, and achieves competitive performance against tuned backpropagation on several benchmarks.

Abstract: Predictive Coding (PC) offers a biologically motivated alternative to backpropagation via local weight updates, yet routing error between layers still relies on an autograd Jacobian-transpose ($J^\top$) product - the last non-local operation in PC. We show that this dependency is largely avoidable. For any layer $f(x)=\mathrm{Act}(\mathrm{Norm}(L(x)))$ with frozen normalization statistics, the exact $J^\top$ factors into three locally available terms, $J^\top v = L^\top(s \odot σ'(z) \odot v)$, where $σ'$ is the activation derivative, $z$ is the pre-activation, and $s=γ/σ_{\mathrm{run}}$ is the normalization gain. Prior weight-feedback methods omitted both corrections; restoring them closes the transport gap for this layer class. Locality here holds up to three assumptions, which we state upfront: weight symmetry ($L^\top$ mirrors the forward operator, as assumed by all PC), a soft spectral-norm control that is not synapse-local, and a nearest-neighbour approximation for MaxPool. Substituting the identity into PC yields WF-Act-PC, which removes the autograd backward pass from error transport. On CIFAR-10/100 (50 epochs, 5 seeds), WF-Act-PC is the only PC method whose accuracy improves with depth, surpassing iPC - the strongest classical PC baseline - by 2.7-22.3 pp on CIFAR-10. With both methods tuned per architecture, it matches or exceeds a comparably-tuned backpropagation baseline on the deeper CIFAR-10 architectures (VGG-9: 93.57% vs. 92.43%; ResNet-18: 92.76% vs. 91.54%) and on the harder Tiny-ImageNet benchmark, while trailing tuned BP on the deeper CIFAR-100 VGG cells. Our WF-Act-PC implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/jlshen025/pcax

</details>


### [146] [Where Should RL Post-Training Compute Go? Model Size, Search, Learning, and Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13389)
*Patrick Wilhelm,Odej Kao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies how to optimally allocate a fixed FLOP budget in RL post-training of foundation models across policy size, rollout/search, learning updates, and reward feedback systems, finding conditional allocation frontiers that depend on model size, budget, and reward type.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL post-training is increasingly used for foundation models, but practitioners face a fixed-budget decision problem: under the same FLOP budget, should they use a larger policy, train longer, generate more rollouts, or spend compute on stronger reward feedback? No principled framework existed to guide this allocation decision.

Method: The authors introduce a FLOP-accounting framework for GRPO post-training that decomposes compute into rollout/search, policy-update/learning, and reward/feedback-model evaluation. They conduct experiments across LoRA-adapted Qwen2.5 policies, varying model size, compute budget, reward system (rule-based vs. PRM-style), and evaluation target to map conditional allocation frontiers.

Result: The best allocation varies conditionally with model size, compute budget, reward system, and evaluation target. Larger policies consume more per-token compute, thus buying fewer updates or rollouts under the same budget, coupling model choice with training allocation. Rule-based rewards spend nearly all non-update compute on rollouts, while PRM-style feedback allocates significant compute to reward-model inference.

Conclusion: RL post-training papers should report total FLOPs together with a breakdown of how compute is divided among model size, search, learning, and feedback. The authors present RACE as a diagnostic pilot-grid protocol to identify allocation regimes before expensive validation runs.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) post-training is increasingly used to adapt foundation models for reasoning, planning, and feedback-driven robot-learning pipelines, but constrained post-training resources are often summarized by a single total FLOP budget. We study the fixed-budget decision problem behind this practice: under the same post-training budget, should one use a larger policy, train a smaller policy longer, generate more rollout search, or spend compute on stronger reward feedback? We introduce a FLOP-accounting framework for GRPO post-training that decomposes compute into rollout/search, policy-update/learning, and reward- or feedback-model evaluation. Across LoRA-adapted Qwen2.5 policies, we find conditional allocation frontiers: the best observed allocation changes with model size, compute budget, reward system, and evaluation target. Same-FLOP model-size comparisons show that model choice and training allocation are coupled because larger policies consume more per-token compute and therefore buy fewer updates or rollouts under the same budget. Reward systems also change the accounting: rule-based rewards spend nearly all non-update compute on policy rollouts, while PRM-style feedback allocates a visible part of the budget to reward-model inference. We present RACE as a diagnostic pilot-grid protocol, not a guarantee of held-out improvement, for identifying allocation regimes before expensive validation runs; our results suggest that RL post-training papers should report total FLOPs together with how compute is divided among model size, search, learning, and feedback.

</details>


### [147] [Self-Improving is Often Sudden: Enlightenment-style Finetuning for Large-Scale Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13395)
*Jing-Xiao Liao,Tianwei Zhang,Yu-Hao Jiang,Feifei Zhang,Hang-Cheng Dong,Feng-Lei Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Enlightenment is a training-free post-tuning paradigm for large-scale models that unlocks latent capabilities through shortcut modifications to key modules/layers, with two architecture-specific instantiations: attention head-mixing shortcuts for LLMs and scalar-modulated residual connections for VLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inspired by human "enlightenment" or "aha moment," the authors hypothesize that large models possess a latent capacity for sudden capability boost, and aim to unlock this potential without additional training.

Method: A training-free post-tuning paradigm that modifies shortcuts for key modules/layers without weight updates. For LLMs: attention head-mixing shortcuts linking initial head output to all target heads with adaptive scaling factor initialization. For VLMs: lightweight scalar-modulated factor applied to residual connections in decoder layers to regulate information flow.

Result: Enlightenment efficiently unlocks latent potential of pre-trained networks, yielding remarkable performance improvements across diverse benchmarks and models.

Conclusion: Training-free post-tuning via shortcut modifications can effectively elicit sudden capability boosts in large-scale models, analogous to the human enlightenment phenomenon.

Abstract: The pursuit of autonomously self-improving models has attracted growing interest in the era of large-scale foundation models. Drawing inspiration from the concept of "enlightenment" or "aha moment" in human brain, we hypothesize that large models exhibit an analogous enlightenment phenomenon-a latent capacity for sudden capability boost. Then, we propose Enlightenment, a novel training-free post-tuning paradigm for large-scale models. Our approach modifies shortcuts for key modules/layers without weight updates, while existing training-free ones predominantly manipulate attention weights. We introduce two architecture-specific instantiations: i) For large language models, we propose attention head-mixing shortcuts that recalibrate attention weights by linking the initial attention head's output to all other target heads, modulated by an adaptive scaling factor initialization strategy. ii) For vision-language models, we apply a lightweight scalar-modulated factor to residual connections in the decoder layers, regulating information flow. Extensive experiments show that Enlightenment efficiently unlocks the latent potential of pre-trained networks, yielding remarkable performance improvements across diverse benchmarks and models.

</details>


### [148] [Is the Statistical Advantage Worth the Cost? An Empirical Comparison of KANs and MLPs for Structured Data Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13413)
*Matthew Steven P. Toledo,Justine Raphael H. Jacinto,Vivekjeet Singh Chambal,Rodolfo C. Camaclang,Jamlech Iram N. Gojo Cruz,Reginald Neil C. Recario*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study compares KANs and MLPs on tabular classification tasks, finding KANs statistically outperform MLPs in binary and multiclass problems with superior generalization, but at the cost of substantially higher parameter and computational complexity (medium effect size d=-0.46).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing interest in KANs as an alternative function-approximating architecture motivated the need for a systematic empirical benchmarking comparison against MLPs on structured tabular classification tasks.

Method: Evaluated both models on 12 publicly available datasets spanning binary, multiclass, multilabel, and ordinal problems under standardized preprocessing, architecture, and fixed hyperparameter settings. Performance assessed using test accuracy, F1-Score, paired hypothesis testing, and effect size analysis.

Result: KANs statistically outperform MLPs in binary and multiclass domains with significant aggregate advantage. However, a medium effect size (d=-0.46) indicates this advantage comes with substantially higher parameter and computational complexity.

Conclusion: KANs are preferred for high-precision applications, while MLPs remain robust and efficient for resource-constrained environments. Future work should extend to additional data modalities.

Abstract: This study presents an empirical benchmarking comparison between Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) and Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLPs) on structured tabular classification tasks. Motivated by the growing interest in KANs as an alternative function-approximating architecture, we evaluate their out-of-the-box performance on twelve publicly available datasets spanning binary, multiclass, multilabel, and ordinal problems. Both models were trained under standardized preprocessing, architecture, and fixed hyperparameter settings, with performance assessed using test accuracy and F1-Score, paired hypothesis testing, and effect size analysis. Results show that KANs statistically outperform MLPs in binary and multiclass domains and achieve a significant aggregate advantage across all datasets. However, the observed medium effect size (d = -0.46) raises an important cost-benefit consideration: while KANs offer superior generalization through adaptive spline-based mappings, this advantage comes with substantially higher parameter and computational complexity relative to the MLP baseline. These findings suggest KANs are the preferred choice for high-precision applications, while MLPs remain a robust and efficient option for resource-constrained environments. Future work should extend this analysis to additional data modalities to further refine these architectural selection criteria.

</details>


### [149] [EXPLORE: Exploration with Guided Search for Analog Topology Generation using Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13416)
*Guanglei Zhou,Chen-Chia Chang,Yikang Shen,Jonathan Ku,Isaac Jacobson,Jingyu Pan,Yiran Chen,Xin Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EXPLORE integrates Monte Carlo Tree Search with transformer-based decoding for analog circuit topology generation, achieving 65% success rate (vs 12% one-shot, 33% baseline) and >20% MSE reduction under tight tolerance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automating analog circuit topology design is crucial to reduce manual effort for increasingly diverse applications, but existing one-shot generation methods fail on complex circuits due to exponential search spaces and limited datasets.

Method: EXPLORE combines simulator-guided Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) with transformer-based decoding, bypassing high-confidence structural tokens to focus simulator budget on topology-altering decisions during search, enabling test-time scaling.

Result: On a 6-component benchmark at 0.01 tolerance, EXPLORE achieves 65% success rate (vs 12% one-shot, 33% sampling-and-filter), and lowers MSE by over 20% relative to sampling-and-filter under the same search budget.

Conclusion: EXPLORE is the first framework to integrate structured test-time search with LM decoding for analog topology generation, representing a practical step toward scaling LLM-driven design automation.

Abstract: Automating analog circuit topology design is essential to reduce the extensive manual effort required to meet increasingly diverse and customized application demands. Recent advances have applied sequence-to-sequence fine-tuning on pretrained language models to directly generate circuit topologies from user specifications in a single pass. However, these one-shot generation methods failed to generate complex circuits due to their exponentially growing search spaces and limited training datasets. In this paper, we present EXPLORE, a search-enhanced framework that integrates simulator-guided Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) with transformer-based decoding to enable test-time scaling for analog topology generation. By leveraging language-model priors and bypassing high-confidence structural tokens, EXPLORE allocates expensive simulator budget primarily toward topology-altering decisions during search. On a 6-component benchmark at a tight tolerance of 0.01, EXPLORE raises the success rate from 12% for one-shot generation and 33% for a sampling-and-filter baseline to 65%, and lowers MSE by over 20% relative to sampling-and-filter under the same search budget. These results establish EXPLORE as the first framework to integrate structured test-time search with LM decoding for analog topology generation, and a practical step toward scaling LLM-driven design automation.

</details>


### [150] [OrDA: Orthogonal Disentanglement of Access Habits Framework for Homepage Marketing Block Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13420)
*Lingxiao Zhang,Xiaobo Li,Tao Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OrDA is a framework that disentangles genuine content interest from access habit bias in homepage marketing clicks using orthogonal regularization and causal intervention, improving recommendation quality by 5.64% UCTR on Zhima.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Homepage marketing clicks are driven by both content interest and access habits; habitual clicks create Pseudo-Positives where position advantage masks mediocre content, biasing recommendation ecosystems.

Method: OrDA uses a dual-tower architecture with a gated allocation layer for adaptive feature routing, orthogonal regularization to enforce geometric perpendicularity between interest and habit manifolds, and do-calculus causal intervention during inference to rank by purified interest scores.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art methods in predictive accuracy on large-scale datasets; online AB test shows 5.64% improvement in user click-through rates (UCTR) on Zhima homepage marketing block and rent-floor recommendation.

Conclusion: OrDA effectively eliminates access-habit bias, delivering purer interest-based recommendations and demonstrable online gains.

Abstract: Clicks on homepage marketing blocks are driven by a dual-mechanism of content interest and access habits. However, habitual clicks often create Pseudo-Positives in marketing slots, where position advantage masks mediocre content quality, leading to biased recommendation ecosystems. We propose a framework called Orthogonal Disentanglement of Access habits (OrDA) to purify interest signals. OrDA utilizes a dual-tower structure with a gated allocation layer to adaptively route features and minimize interference. To ensure rigorous separation, we employ orthogonal regularization to constrain the latent interest and habit manifolds to be geometrically perpendicular. OrDA performs causal intervention (do-calculus) during inference to rank items solely by purified interest scores. Empirical online evaluations on large-scale datasets demonstrate that OrDA effectively eliminates access-habit bias, outperforming state-of-the-art methods in predictive accuracy. Online AB test 5.64% shows user click-through rates (UCTR) improvement on the Zhima homepage marketing block, Zhima rent-floor recommendation.

</details>


### [151] [Temperature Scaling Is Not Enough: Calibration Gaps Under Human Label Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13423)
*Wisdom Dogah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Temperature scaling, the dominant post-hoc calibration method for deep learning, performs worse when ground-truth labels are soft/distributional (reflecting real annotator disagreement) compared to when labels are one-hot deterministic. This "soft-label calibration gap" grows with model scale in vision and language domains, is larger in language than vision, and holds across all nine configurations tested using CIFAR-10H and ChaosNLI datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Temperature scaling assumes labels are one-hot and deterministic, but real-world labels are often soft (crowd-sourced, reflecting annotator disagreement). The authors investigate whether violating this assumption degrades calibration and whether degradation depends on model scale.

Method: Using CIFAR-10H (vision) and ChaosNLI (language) with human-annotated soft label distributions, they evaluate three model scales per modality under both hard one-hot and soft distributional targets. They compute Brier Score gaps between temperature scaling calibrated on hard labels vs. an oracle calibrated on soft labels, and also compare against multiclass isotonic regression.

Result: A positive soft-label calibration gap exists in all nine configurations (Brier Score gaps 0.002–0.134). The gap grows monotonically with model scale in vision and on the SNLI split of ChaosNLI, and is larger in language (mean 0.079) than vision (mean 0.003). The MNLI split showed a scale-ordering reversal attributed to near-chance accuracy. Multiclass isotonic regression confirmed the same qualitative pattern.

Conclusion: Calibration protocols built on majority-vote labels systematically misstate model reliability when label ambiguity is structural, with direct implications for deployment in safety-critical settings.

Abstract: Temperature scaling is the dominant post-hoc calibration method in modern deep learning. Its theoretical justification rests on an assumption that is rarely stated explicitly: that ground-truth labels are one-hot and deterministic.
