In practice, labels are frequently soft, crowd-sourced, or genuinely distributional, reflecting real disagreement among human annotators rather than annotation noise. We study whether temperature scaling retains its calibration properties when this assumption is violated, and whether any resulting degradation depends on model scale. Using CIFAR-10H and ChaosNLI, two publicly available datasets with human-annotated soft label distributions, we evaluate three model scales per modality under both hard one-hot and soft distributional label targets. Across all nine configurations we find a positive soft-label calibration gap: temperature scaling calibrated on hard labels consistently underperforms an oracle calibrated directly on soft labels, with Brier Score gaps ranging from 0.002 to 0.134. The gap grows monotonically with model scale in the vision domain and on the SNLI-derived split of ChaosNLI, and is substantially larger in the language domain (mean gap 0.079) than in vision (mean gap 0.003). A scale-ordering reversal on the MNLI-derived split remains after matched-domain training; we treat it as inconclusive for the scale hypothesis and attribute it primarily to near-chance accuracy on that split. As a second post-hoc baseline, multiclass isotonic regression yields the same qualitative conclusion: positive soft-label gaps in all nine configurations, and larger gaps in language than in vision. These findings suggest that calibration protocols built on majority-vote labels systematically misstate model reliability wherever label ambiguity is structural, with direct consequences for deployment in safety-critical settings.

</details>


### [152] [Data-Efficient Adaptation of LLMs via Attention Head Reweighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13425)
*Tuomas Oikarinen,Zixiao Chen,Charlotte Siska,Tsui-Wei Weng,Chandan Singh,Jianfeng Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AHR adapts LLMs to text classification with limited data by learning just one scalar per attention head (~0.0001% of parameters), outperforming LoRA with 200-1000x fewer trainable parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning effectively from limited data is critical in domains like security where labeled examples are scarce; existing LLM adaptation methods struggle with few-shot difficult tasks.

Method: Attention Head Reweighting (AHR) learns a single scalar per attention head to reweight attention heads, leveraging their functional specialization, drastically reducing trainable parameters.

Result: AHR outperforms standard baselines like LoRA on diverse text classification datasets under limited samples, despite having 200-1000x fewer trainable parameters (modifying ~0.0001% of parameters).

Conclusion: AHR is data-efficient, interpretable, and enables analysis of attention heads responsible for in-context learning abilities in LLMs.

Abstract: Learning effectively from limited data is critical in domains like security where labeled examples are scarce. Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated some capabilities for data-efficient learning, especially through parameter-efficient adaptation methods, but continue to struggle when faced with few samples for difficult tasks. To meet this challenge, we propose Attention Head Reweighting (AHR), a data-efficient method that adapts LLMs to new text-classification tasks by learning only a single scalar per attention head. This drastically reduces the number of parameters that need to be learned by making use of the functional specialization of individual attention heads. Experiments on diverse open-source text classification datasets show that AHR can outperform standard baselines like LoRA when learning from limited samples, despite having 200-1000x fewer trainable parameters, as our AHR only modifies ~0.0001% of the model's parameters. In addition, our learned weights are easy to interpret and can be analyzed to better understand the mechanisms and attention heads responsible for in-context learning abilities in LLMs.

</details>


### [153] [PUe: Biased Positive-Unlabeled Learning Enhancement by Causal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13428)
*Xutao Wang,Hanting Chen,Tianyu Guo,Yunhe Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a PU learning enhancement (PUe) framework based on normalized propensity scores and normalized inverse probability weighting (NIPW) to address selection bias in PU learning where positive labels are non-randomly distributed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cost-sensitive PU methods assume positive labels are selected completely at random, but real-world PU problems exhibit uneven label distributions and selection bias, motivating the need for a propensity-weighted approach that accounts for biased labeling.

Method: The PUe framework builds on SAR-PU propensity weighting, introducing: (1) a normalized inverse-probability-weighted PU risk formulation; (2) theoretical analysis of normalized sample-weight error and PU estimators under biased labeling; (3) regularized deep propensity-score estimation; (4) integration with modern cost-sensitive PU methods; and (5) support for selectively labeled negative classes.

Result: Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and ADNI datasets show improvements over several PU baselines under non-uniform label distributions.

Conclusion: Normalized propensity weighting effectively mitigates selection bias in PU learning, and the proposed PUe framework generalizes well across different datasets and can be integrated with existing cost-sensitive PU methods.

Abstract: Positive-Unlabeled (PU) learning aims to achieve high-accuracy binary classification with limited labeled positive examples and numerous unlabeled ones. Existing cost-sensitive-based methods often rely on strong assumptions that examples with an observed positive label were selected entirely at random. In fact, the uneven distribution of labels is prevalent in real-world PU problems, indicating that most actual positive and unlabeled data are subject to selection bias. Building on the SAR-PU propensity-weighted framework of Bekker et al., we study a PU learning enhancement (PUe) framework using normalized propensity scores and normalized inverse probability weighting (NIPW). PUe's main contributions are a normalized inverse-probability-weighted PU risk formulation; additional theoretical analyses of normalized sample-weight error and common PU estimators under biased labeling; regularized deep propensity-score estimation; integration with modern cost-sensitive PU methods; and support for selectively labeled negative classes. Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and ADNI demonstrate improvements over several PU baselines under non-uniform label distributions.

</details>


### [154] [Discrete Diffusion Models: A Unified Framework from Tokenization to Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13431)
*Ye Yuan,Weien Li,Rui Song,Zeyu Li,Haochen Liu,Xiangyu Kong,Zixuan Dong,Linfeng Du,Zipeng Sun,Weixu Zhang,Jiaxin Huang,Changjiang Han,Yonghan Yang,Zichen Zhao,Xiuyuan Hu,Haolun Wu,Yankai Chen,Fengran Mo,Jikun Kang,Bowei He,Philip S. Yu,Xue Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified framework viewing discrete diffusion models through the lens of discrete state space construction (tokenization, vocabulary topology, structural alphabets), revealing that transition-matrix, masking, and score-based approaches are different instantiations of a common design space, and exposing key trade-offs in training, inference, scaling, systems, and evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a unified conceptual understanding of how the construction of the discrete state space fundamentally shapes discrete denoising diffusion models, given that unlike continuous diffusion, DDMs are not fixed but vary by tokenization scheme, vocabulary topology, and domain-specific alphabets.

Method: Introduces a conceptual framework that centers on discrete state space construction, then analyzes existing DDM formulations (transition-matrix, masking/absorbing-state, score/ratio-based) as different instantiations within this shared design space, and examines trade-offs across training objectives, inference, scaling, systems optimization, and evaluation.

Result: A unified framework showing that diverse DDM approaches share a common design space governed by how the discrete state space is constructed, and a comprehensive mapping of design trade-offs across multiple dimensions (training, inference, scaling, systems, evaluation).

Conclusion: The framework suggests several promising directions for future research by exposing common design trade-offs across key aspects of DDM development.

Abstract: Discrete denoising diffusion models (DDMs) have recently emerged as a compelling alternative to autoregressive (AR) modeling for discrete data, offering parallel generation and iterative global refinement capabilities. Unlike continuous diffusion, where the state space is fixed, DDMs are fundamentally shaped by how the discrete state space is constructed: the tokenization scheme, the vocabulary topology, and domain-specific structural alphabets. This work introduces a unified conceptual framework that views discrete diffusion models through the construction of the underlying discrete state space. Within this framework, existing formulations, including transition-matrix, masking/absorbing-state, and score/ratio-based approaches, emerge as different instantiations of a common design space. The framework further exposes common design trade-offs across training objectives, inference algorithms, scaling behavior, systems optimization, and evaluation protocols, suggesting several promising directions for future research.

</details>


### [155] [Local Redundancy: An Information-Theoretic Measure of Plasticity from Synthetic Memorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13432)
*Jiaxuan Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces local redundancy, an information-theoretic measure derived from universal compression theory, as a principled metric for neural network plasticity. It is defined as worst-case redundancy of local model family, shown to predict downstream task performance better than existing measures like effective rank or dead neuron fraction, and enables checkpoint selection when validation loss plateaus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing measures of plasticity (effective rank, dead neuron fraction, weight norm) lack theoretical grounding and correlate poorly with performance on new tasks, motivating a more principled measure.

Method: Local redundancy is defined as the worst-case redundancy of a local model family in an infinitesimal neighborhood along gradient directions. The expected squared gradient norm on a synthetic memorization task is proven to be an efficiently computable lower bound.

Result: Local redundancy predicts downstream performance better than existing measures on continual image classification and time series transfer learning, and enables pretraining checkpoint selection where validation loss plateaus.

Conclusion: Local redundancy is a principled, theoretically grounded measure of plasticity that outperforms existing heuristic-based measures.

Abstract: Plasticity -- a neural network's ability to adapt to new tasks -- is critical for continual and transfer learning. Existing measures, such as effective rank, dead neuron fraction, and weight norm, lack theoretical grounding and correlate poorly with performance on new tasks. We introduce local redundancy, an information-theoretic measure derived from universal compression theory. We define local redundancy as the worst-case redundancy of a local model family -- parameters in an infinitesimal neighborhood along gradient directions -- and show this is a principled measure of plasticity. Although local redundancy is intractable to compute exactly, we prove that the expected squared gradient norm on a synthetic memorization task provides an efficiently computable lower bound. Experiments on continual image classification and time series transfer learning demonstrate that local redundancy predicts downstream performance better than existing measures and enables pretraining checkpoint selection where validation loss plateaus.

</details>


### [156] [Distributionally Robust and Safe Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13436)
*Ahmed Aboudonia,Naira Hovakimyan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a distributionally robust and safe imitation learning framework that addresses both policy-induced and uncertainty-induced distribution shifts by combining Taylor Series Imitation Learning (TaSIL) with distributionally robust adaptive control, demonstrated on a UAV task in uncertain environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation learning suffers from performance degradation due to distribution shifts, which can lead to safety risks. Existing methods do not jointly address both policy-induced shifts (from compounding errors) and uncertainty-induced shifts (from environmental variability).

Method: A unified framework that integrates Taylor Series Imitation Learning (TaSIL) to mitigate policy-induced distribution shifts and distributionally robust adaptive control to handle uncertainty-induced shifts, formulating an IL problem that optimizes performance under distributional uncertainty while enforcing safety constraints.

Result: Demonstrated on a UAV case study where the agent performs tasks in an uncertain environment while avoiding unsafe regions, showing effectiveness of the combined framework.

Conclusion: The proposed distributionally robust and safe IL framework effectively addresses both types of distribution shifts, enabling safer and more robust imitation learning in uncertain environments.

Abstract: Imitation learning (IL) has achieved remarkable success in complex decision-making tasks. However, its performance is highly sensitive to distribution shifts, which can pose significant safety risks. We propose a distributionally robust and safe IL framework that explicitly addresses both policy-induced and uncertainty-induced distribution shifts. Our approach develops a unified framework leveraging Taylor Series Imitation Learning (TaSIL) to mitigate policy-induced shifts and distributionally robust adaptive control to handle uncertainty-induced shifts. This architecture enables the formulation of an IL problem that optimizes performance under distributional uncertainty while systematically accounting for safety constraints. We demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach on an unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) case study where the UAV performs a task in an uncertain environment while avoiding unsafe regions.

</details>


### [157] [PQFA: Parallel Quantum Feature Augmentation of Fused Representations for Multimodal Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13466)
*Mingzhu Wang,Yun Shang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PQFA is a hybrid quantum-classical framework that applies parallel variational quantum circuits to augment fused multimodal features, consistently outperforming classical baselines with far fewer parameters (2.2K vs 24.0K) and showing robustness to missing modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-fusion enhancement in multimodal learning remains underexplored. The authors aim to leverage quantum circuits as a parameter-efficient strategy for augmenting fused multimodal representations beyond standard fusion techniques.

Method: Text and image features from frozen RoBERTa/ViT encoders are fused via bidirectional cross-attention, attentive pooling, and adaptive gated fusion. The fused vector is amplitude-encoded into multiple shallow variational quantum circuits; their measurement readouts are concatenated with the classical representation for final prediction.

Result: PQFA outperforms the fusion backbone alone and a width-matched MLP augmentation baseline on MM-IMDb and N24News, using only ~2.2K parameters vs 24.0K. It shows improved robustness under missing-modality scenarios, especially when text is severely degraded. Gains are not reproduced by random mappings, increased width, or untrained quantum circuits.

Conclusion: PQFA is an effective, parameter-efficient post-fusion augmentation method for hybrid quantum-classical multimodal learning, with stable performance under simulated noise and distinct branch-specific transformations.

Abstract: Most multimodal learning methods improve how heterogeneous representations are aligned and fused, while post-fusion enhancement remains less explored. We propose Parallel Quantum Feature Augmentation (PQFA), a hybrid quantum-classical framework that applies multiple shallow variational quantum circuits to fused multimodal features. Text and image representations extracted by frozen RoBERTa and ViT encoders are processed through bidirectional cross-attention, attentive pooling, and adaptive gated fusion. The fused feature is then amplitude-encoded into parallel quantum circuits, whose measurement readouts are concatenated with the classical representation for prediction. We evaluate PQFA on MM-IMDb and N24News through controlled comparisons using the same encoders, fusion backbone, data splits, projection dimension, and augmentation output width. PQFA consistently outperforms both the fusion backbone without quantum augmentation and a width-matched MLP augmentation baseline, while using approximately 2.2K augmentation parameters compared with 24.0K for the MLP branch. Missing-modality experiments further show improved robustness when textual or visual inputs are incomplete, with particularly clear gains when the more informative textual modality is severely degraded. Controlled ablations and feature-space analyses indicate that the improvement cannot be reproduced by random feature mappings, increased classical width, or untrained quantum transformations. Quantum-state diagnostics additionally show stable predictive performance across the tested simulated noise levels and distinct branch-specific transformations of the encoded states. These results establish PQFA as an effective and parameter-efficient strategy for post-fusion augmentation in hybrid quantum-classical multimodal learning.

</details>


### [158] [Explainable Artificial Intelligence for Anomaly Detection in Banking Transactions: An Internal Audit Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13469)
*Anupa Lodhi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents an XAI framework combining Isolation Forest for fraud anomaly detection with SHAP explanations, deployed via a Streamlit dashboard, achieving 0.91 precision/0.88 recall on synthetic banking data and improving auditor decision confidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional rule-based fraud detection systems suffer from high false-positive rates and lack explanatory outputs, limiting their usefulness for compliance teams in banking.

Method: An Isolation Forest (iForest) model performs unsupervised anomaly scoring, a SHAP layer provides transaction-level feature-attributed explanations grounded in cooperative game theory, and a Streamlit dashboard renders outputs for non-expert auditors.

Result: Achieves 0.91 precision and 0.88 recall on a synthetic banking dataset, outperforming three unsupervised baselines. Expert feedback confirms feature-level explanations improve auditor confidence and decision quality.

Conclusion: The framework advances the practical deployment of accountable, transparent AI in regulated financial environments.

Abstract: The banking sector increasingly relies on automated systems to monitor electronic transactions for signs of fraud, yet conventional rule-based approaches struggle with high false-positive rates and offer no justification for their outputs, limiting their utility for compliance teams. This paper introduces an Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) framework tailored for banking transaction anomaly detection within internal audit workflows. An Isolation Forest (iForest) model performs unsupervised anomaly scoring, while a SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations) layer provides transaction-level, feature-attributed explanations grounded in cooperative game theory [8]. A lightweight Streamlit dashboard renders these outputs in a form accessible to audit professionals without machine learning expertise. Evaluation on a synthetic banking dataset yields 0.91 precision and 0.88 recall, outperforming three unsupervised baselines. Expert feedback confirms that feature-level explanations measurably improve auditor confidence and decision quality. The framework advances the practical deployment of accountable, transparent AI in regulated financial environments.

</details>


### [159] [DeepLoop: Depth Scaling for Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13491)
*Shuzhen Li,Yifan Zhang,Jiacheng Guo,Quanquan Gu,Mengdi Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Looped Transformers reuse a stack of physical blocks across multiple rounds, introducing a tied-depth effect that changes residual scaling. The paper proposes DeepLoop, which adjusts scaling exponents based on visit-alignment coefficient, improving stability and performance when recurrent depth is activated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the residual-scaling problem in looped (weight-tied) Transformers, where shared parameter updates from repeated visits create a tied-depth effect not present in untied Transformers, requiring different scaling rules for stable training.

Method: Formalizes tied-depth effect via a first-order perturbation bound controlled by a visit-alignment coefficient κ_R. Derives scaling exponents based on whether visits are decorrelated (DeepNorm, exponent 1/4) or aligned (exponent increases to 1/2). Proposes DeepLoop with α=(2N)^{1/2}, β=(8N)^{-1/2} for unrolled depth N, using Post-LN DeepNorm architecture.

Result: On GPT-2 small and medium scale looped language models, DeepLoop is neutral when no physical block is revisited, and improves validation loss and downstream accuracy once recurrent depth is activated.

Conclusion: Stable recurrent depth requires residual scaling rules that account for parameter visits (tied-depth), not just nominal layer count. DeepLoop provides an effective scaling strategy for looped Transformers.

Abstract: Looped Transformers scale sequential computation by applying a compact stack of physical blocks for multiple rounds, increasing unrolled depth without increasing stored parameters. This reuse changes the residual-scaling problem: in an untied Transformer, each residual branch receives and applies its own parameter update, whereas in a looped Transformer one shared update aggregates gradients from repeated visits and is read back by those same visits in the next linearized forward pass. We formalize this tied-depth effect through a first-order perturbation bound controlled by a visit-alignment coefficient $κ_R$. The bound recovers the DeepNorm exponent when visits decorrelate, but in the conservative aligned regime it requires the exponent to increase from $1/4$ to $1/2$ as loop count grows at fixed physical depth. The resulting method, \textbf{DeepLoop}, keeps the Post-LN DeepNorm architecture and sets $α=(2N)^{1/2}$ and $β=(8N)^{-1/2}$ for unrolled depth $N$. On GPT-style looped language models at GPT-2 small and GPT-2 medium scale, DeepLoop is neutral when no physical block is revisited and improves validation loss and downstream accuracy once recurrent depth is activated. These results show that stable recurrent depth requires residual scaling rules that account for parameter visits, not only nominal layer count.

</details>


### [160] [A VAE-Driven Multi-Task Satellite-Aided Semantic Communication Framework for 6G-Enabled Connected Autonomous Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13494)
*S. M. Abtahiul Alam,Niloy Das,Apurba Adhikary,Yu Qiao,Zhu Han,Choong Seon Hong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a VAE-based multi-task semantic communication framework for satellite-assisted autonomous driving that transmits task-relevant semantic features instead of raw data, achieving 87.23%-98.17% bandwidth reduction while maintaining robust traffic sign reconstruction and classification across varying SNR conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional communication systems in satellite-assisted CAV networks transmit raw data regardless of task relevance, which is inefficient due to scarce uplink bandwidth and large propagation losses. Semantic communication can address this by transmitting only task-relevant information.

Method: A Variational Autoencoder (VAE)-based multi-task semantic communication framework that uses probabilistic latent representations for robust encoding, trained end-to-end to jointly optimize traffic sign reconstruction and classification over noisy wireless channels.

Result: Achieves 87.23% to 98.17% bandwidth reduction while maintaining stable performance across varying signal-to-noise ratio conditions for both traffic sign reconstruction and classification tasks.

Conclusion: VAE-based probabilistic semantic communication is more robust and efficient than deterministic autoencoder methods for satellite-assisted autonomous driving, significantly reducing bandwidth while preserving task performance.

Abstract: The development of smart transportation systems and the introduction of 6G wireless communication technologies have significantly changed vehicle network topologies. Future connected autonomous vehicle (CAV) networks require bandwidth-efficient, reliable, and low-latency communication for safety-critical applications such as traffic sign recognition and decision-making. Conventional communication systems transmit raw data regardless of task relevance, which is inefficient in resource-constrained satellite channels where uplink bandwidth is scarce and propagation losses are large. Semantic communication addresses this limitation by transmitting task-relevant information instead of full signal representations. It extracts and conveys essential semantic features and leverages deep learning to optimize task performance at the receiver. Therefore, we present a Variational Autoencoder (VAE)-based multi-task semantic communication framework for satellite-assisted autonomous driving. Unlike deterministic autoencoder-based methods, the proposed model uses probabilistic latent representations for more robust and efficient encoding. The learned features are transmitted over noisy wireless channels to perform traffic sign reconstruction and classification. The framework is trained end-to-end to jointly optimize both tasks. Results show that the proposed approach achieves significant bandwidth reduction of up to 87.23\% to 98.17\% while maintaining stable performance across varying signal-to-noise ratio conditions.

</details>


### [161] [Factorized Spectral Representations for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13498)
*Junyi Wu,Dan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FaStR is a spectral representation method for deep RL that uses CP decomposition of the transition kernel tensor (states, actions, next states) with a noise contrastive objective, producing separate encoders per mode. This factored form reduces sample complexity by a factor scaling with the smaller of state/action dimensions, and shows gains on high-dimensional locomotion tasks with transferable state encoders under actuator shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing spectral representation methods treat the transition kernel as a matrix, but it is naturally a three-mode tensor (states, actions, next states). Representing it as a matrix loses the inherent factored structure, requiring more samples than necessary.

Method: FaStR fits a CP decomposition of the transition kernel tensor using a noise contrastive objective, learning separate encoder networks for states, actions, and next states. The factored form reduces the hypothesis class size, lowering the sample complexity by a factor proportional to min(state_dim, action_dim).

Result: FaStR achieves its largest performance gains on high-dimensional locomotion tasks where dynamics align with the factored structure. The learned state encoder transfers intact under actuator shift (only the action encoder needs retraining), demonstrating modularity.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling the three-mode tensor structure of the transition kernel via CP decomposition is beneficial for sample efficiency and representation transfer in deep RL, particularly when the underlying dynamics admit a natural factored form.

Abstract: Learning a compact model of the world from interaction data is central to sample-efficient deep reinforcement learning. Spectral representation methods have become the leading paradigm for representation learning in continuous control by taking a matrix view of the transition kernel, with state-action pairs on one side and next states on the other, and learning a low-rank factorization through self-supervised contrastive objectives. We take this view one step further. The transition kernel is naturally a three-mode tensor over states, actions, and next states, and a CP decomposition gives one feature map per mode. We propose FaStR, which fits this decomposition with a noise contrastive objective, producing separate state, action, and next-state encoders that together form a single spectral representation. The factored form yields a smaller hypothesis class, and the sample size needed for representation learning shrinks by a factor that scales with the smaller of the state and action dimensions. Empirically, FaStR delivers its largest gains on high-dimensional locomotion tasks whose dynamics align with the factored structure, and the learned state encoder transfers intact across actuator shift while only the action encoder is retrained.

</details>


### [162] [CDS: Counterfactual Directionality Score for Structured Interventions in Spatial Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13508)
*Humaira Anzum,Md Ishtyaq Mahmud,Jagan Mohan Reddy Dwarampudi,Tania Banerjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Counterfactual Directionality Score (CDS) using a Neighbor Influence Model (NIM) with constrained interventions to quantify directional influence between node populations in graph-based models, validated on synthetic spatial graphs and spatial transcriptomics data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods (attention, attribution, correlation) capture associations but lack a principled framework for evaluating directional effects under controlled perturbations in spatial biological systems where cell-cell interactions shape functional outcomes.

Method: The framework trains a Neighbor Influence Model (NIM) to predict node states from local neighborhoods, applies constrained counterfactual interventions that modify neighborhood composition while preserving spatial/structural properties, defines the Counterfactual Directionality Score (CDS) as a finite-difference measure of local intervention sensitivity, and uses a core-level bootstrap procedure for valid uncertainty estimation accounting for spatial dependencies.

Result: On synthetic spatial graphs, CDS recovers known directional structure, remains well calibrated under null conditions, and is robust to confounding signals. On spatial transcriptomics data, it reveals biologically plausible and consistent interactions across tissue cores.

Conclusion: CDS provides a principled framework for estimating directional influence between node types in graph-based models via counterfactual interventions, with demonstrated validity in synthetic settings and biological plausibility in real spatial transcriptomics applications.

Abstract: Quantifying directional influence between node populations is a fundamental problem in graph-based modeling, particularly in spatial biological systems where cell-cell interactions shape functional outcomes. Existing approaches based on attention, attribution, or correlation capture associations but do not provide a principled framework for evaluating directional effects under controlled perturbations. We introduce a framework for structured counterfactual interventions in graph-based models to estimate directional influence between node types. Our approach trains a Neighbor Influence Model (NIM) to predict node states from local neighborhoods and applies constrained interventions that modify neighborhood composition while preserving key spatial and structural properties. We define the Counterfactual Directionality Score (CDS), which measures the change in predicted node state induced by targeted perturbations, and provide a theoretical interpretation of CDS as a finite-difference measure of local intervention sensitivity. To obtain valid uncertainty estimates, we introduce a core-level bootstrap procedure that accounts for dependencies within spatial samples. Experiments on synthetic spatial graphs with known directional structure show that CDS recovers directional influence, remains well calibrated under null conditions, and is robust to confounding signals, while preliminary results on spatial transcriptomics data reveal biologically plausible and consistent interactions across tissue cores.

</details>


### [163] [ExTernD: Expanded-Rank Ternary Decomposition Ternary LLM PTQ with Accuracy Approaching Any Quantization Level](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13511)
*Chethan Reddy G. P*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ExTernD is a post-training factorization method that decomposes LLM weight matrices into ternary factors with an expanded inner rank, enabling accuracy that approaches bf16 with ~5.2-5.7 effective bits per weight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ternary quantization schemes are limited by fixed-rank constraints, causing quantization error that cannot be reduced below a certain threshold. ExTernD aims to overcome this by expanding the inner rank beyond full rank, allowing the residual error to be driven arbitrarily low.

Method: Each weight matrix A is factorized as A ≈ B·diag(D)·C where B and C are ternary matrices ({-1,0,+1}) and D is a real scale vector. The inner rank k = μ·min(m,n) is expanded with μ > 1, so components beyond full rank compensate for quantization error of earlier components. Sparsity is controlled by a threshold τ.

Result: ExTernD matches Q4_K accuracy at 5.2-5.5 effective bpw on Gemma-4-E2B and Qwen3.5-4B. A full Qwen3.5-4B conversion at μ=3 achieves 10.10 perplexity (vs 9.78 for bf16, +3.2%), near the Q4_K/Q5_K band at ~5.7 effective bpw.

Conclusion: ExTernD provably approaches bf16 precision arbitrarily closely through rank expansion, offering a continuous trade-off between memory/compute and accuracy without being restricted to discrete bit-widths.

Abstract: We introduce ExTernD (Expanded-rank Ternary Decomposition), a post-training factorization of each LLM weight matrix $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ into $A \approx B \mathrm{diag}(D) C$ with ternary factors $B \in \{-1,0,+1\}^{m \times k}$, $C \in \{-1,0,+1\}^{k \times n}$ and a real scale vector $D \in \mathbb{R}^k$. The inner rank $k = μ\min(m,n)$ is deliberately expanded beyond full rank ($μ> 1$), so that components past full rank correct the quantization error of earlier ones. We prove the residual decreases monotonically in $k$ and can be driven below any $\varepsilon > 0$: ExTernD approaches bf16 accuracy arbitrarily closely, which no ternary scheme with a fixed plane count can do. Memory and compute scale continuously with $μ$, and factor sparsity continuously with a threshold $τ$, so an accuracy target is hit exactly rather than rounded to the next bit-width. ExTernD matches Q4_K's per-matrix accuracy at 5.2-5.5 effective bpw (5.1-5.5 with importance weighting) on Gemma-4-E2B and Qwen3.5-4B, and a full Qwen3.5-4B conversion at $μ= 3$ reaches 10.10 wikitext-2 perplexity against 9.78 for bf16 (+3.2%), placing it near the Q4_K/Q5_K accuracy band at ~5.7 effective bpw.

</details>


### [164] [Clustering algorithms for multivariate wind farm SCADA data filtering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13544)
*Nicolò Italiano,Vasilis Pettas,Tuhfe Göçmen,Nicolaos A. Cutululis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares multiple clustering algorithms for filtering SCADA data from offshore wind turbines, introducing robust evaluation metrics for unlabeled data and showing that cluster-based methods can outperform manual filtering in detecting outliers, though expert involvement is still needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wind farm SCADA systems record numerous anomalies, transients, and operational modes, yet many applications require only normal-operation data. Manual filtering by experts via visual inspection is inefficient, motivating the need for automated filtering methods that can replace or reduce this labor-intensive process.

Method: Multiple clustering algorithms are compared against manual filtering on SCADA data from three offshore wind turbines using 10-minute statistics across multiple data channels. Evaluation metrics suitable for unlabeled data are introduced, and analysis extends beyond the power curve to include multi-dimensional feature selection.

Result: Cluster-based methods achieve higher accuracy than manual filtering in most cases, detecting both evident and subtle outliers. However, accuracy and data retention vary considerably across models. The dataset includes non-evident outliers from field tests, and extending analysis beyond the power curve is crucial.

Conclusion: Cluster-based filtering can reduce but not eliminate expert involvement. Recommendations are provided for generalizing model calibration to different datasets, and the importance of multi-dimensional feature selection and evaluation metrics beyond the power curve is emphasized.

Abstract: During wind farm operation, Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA) systems record numerous anomalies, transients, and specific operational modes, leading to large datasets. However, for a wide range of applications, only measurements corresponding to normal operation are required and, therefore, the SCADA data must be filtered. For this purpose, several methods have been proposed to automate and replace manual filtering conducted by experts via visual inspection of the data. In this paper, we compare the filtering accuracy of multiple clustering algorithms against manual filtering, introducing evaluation metrics that are suitable for unlabeled data and robust across potential applications. Based on the results, we provide recommendations for generalizing model calibration to different datasets and discuss potential use cases for each model. The models are applied to the SCADA data of three turbines of an existing offshore wind farm, using 10-minute statistics across multiple data channels. In addition to the anomalies and operational modes typically recorded, the dataset presents a large number of non-evident outliers due to several field tests. Overall, the results highlight the importance of extending the analysis beyond the power curve, both in feature selection and in the design of evaluation metrics. In most cases, cluster-based methods are able to detect both evident and subtle outliers, achieving higher accuracy than manual filtering. However, the accuracy and the amount of data retained vary considerably depending on the model, and expert involvement remains necessary, though to a reduced extent compared to manual filtering.

</details>


### [165] [From Novice to Expert: Cost-Aware Bandits for Evolving Worker Performance in Crowdsensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13546)
*Yin Huang,Qingsong Liu,Jie Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies budget-constrained online worker recruitment in mobile crowdsensing where worker sensing quality improves with experience and converges to a plateau, and costs are unknown. The authors propose a cost-aware structured bandit framework that learns evolving reward trajectories and heterogeneous costs, detects saturation, and allocates budget to maximize long-term utility, with theoretical guarantees and empirical improvements over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MC recruitment methods assume stationary worker sensing quality and known costs, but in practice, worker performance improves with experience and stabilizes, while sensing costs are unknown due to time-varying device/context states.

Method: Formulates a structured bandit model where each worker's expected reward is an unknown increasing-then-converging function of participation count, with unknown expected cost. Develops a cost-aware online learning framework that jointly learns evolving reward trajectories and heterogeneous costs, detects saturation, and allocates budget under budget constraints.

Result: Provides theoretical performance guarantees and demonstrates consistent improvements over baselines that ignore experience-driven dynamics or assume known costs through extensive experiments.

Conclusion: The proposed cost-aware structured bandit approach effectively addresses the realistic dynamics of worker performance improvement and cost uncertainty in mobile crowdsensing recruitment under budget constraints.

Abstract: Mobile crowdsensing (MC) recruits mobile users to perform sensing tasks using their smartphones, enabling large-scale applications such as traffic monitoring and environmental sensing. A fundamental challenge is online worker recruitment under uncertainty, where the platform must learn workers' sensing performance while operating with a limited budget. Existing learning-based MC recruitment methods typically assume that each worker's sensing quality is stationary with a fixed mean over time. In practice, however, worker performance often improves with experience and eventually stabilizes, while the incurred sensing cost can be unknown in advance due to time-varying device and context states. In this paper, we study a budget-constrained online recruitment problem in which the platform selects one worker in each round, observes the sensing quality and incurred cost, where the expected sensing quality of each worker increases with experience and eventually converges to a plateau, and repeats until the budget is exhausted. We formulate this problem as a structured bandit model where each worker's expected reward evolves according to an unknown increasing-then-converging function of its participation count, and each worker has an unknown expected cost. We develop a cost-aware online learning framework that jointly learns evolving reward trajectories and heterogeneous costs, detects performance saturation, and allocates the limited budget to maximize long-term sensing utility. We provide theoretical performance guarantees and validate the proposed approach through extensive experiments, demonstrating consistent improvements over baselines that ignore experience-driven dynamics or assume known costs.

</details>


### [166] [Structured Reinforcement Learning for Bayesian Persuasion : Application to Intelligent Interactive Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13576)
*Merlin Paul,Anup Aprem*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Bayesian persuasion framework where a lead vehicle (principal) uses structured reinforcement learning to design computationally efficient signaling strategies that persuasively guide a farsighted connected vehicle (agent), achieving 30% cost efficiency in lane selection tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the computational challenge of designing signaling strategies for a lead vehicle to guide connected vehicles' route choices, where the agent's farsighted (long-term reward maximizing) behavior makes the principal's signaling strategy optimization difficult.

Method: The authors propose (i) MAPL (structured policy learning for monotonic agents with approximate best response), (ii) sufficient conditions for supermodular Q-function structure, (iii) sufficient conditions for persuasiveness, and (iv) Supermodular Q learning for Principal (SQP) that leverages supermodular structure to synthesize efficient signaling strategies.

Result: Numerical analysis on Bayesian persuasive driving for lane selection shows the proposed method achieves 30% cost efficiency in optimizing traveling rewards for both lead and connected vehicles compared to existing signaling strategy design methods.

Conclusion: The structured reinforcement learning framework (SQP) effectively synthesizes computationally efficient and persuasive signaling strategies for interactive driving scenarios with farsighted agents.

Abstract: Interactive driving, wherein an intelligent lead vehicle equipped with real-time traffic data coordinates route choices of connected vehicles, offers a promising approach to dynamic traffic management. To address the challenge of harmonising decisions, this paper considers the strategic information revealing framework of Bayesian persuasion. Here, the principal (lead vehicle) aims to guide the agent's (connected vehicle) partially observable sequential decision making towards its own objectives by selectively revealing information, such as real-time traffic ahead, using signals. However, the agent's farsighted response to maximize its long-term reward, renders the principal's signaling strategy design computationally challenging. We propose an online structured reinforcement learning framework to synthesize computationally efficient signaling strategy which is persuasive for a far-sighted agent. The main contributions of the paper are as follows: (i) For a monotonic agent with approximate best response, we propose MAPL, a structured policy learning algorithm for faster online learning, (ii) Identification of sufficient conditions for the supermodular structure of the Q function of the principal for a monotonic agent, (iii) Identification of sufficient conditions to ensure the persuasiveness of the principal's signaling strategy, (iv) Supermodular Q learning for Principal (SQP), which leverages the supermodular structure of principal's action value to synthesize computationally efficient signaling strategy that is persuasive for a monotonic learning agent, (v) Numerical analysis considering a real-time application of Bayesian persuasive driving for lane selection demonstrates that the proposed method is 30% cost efficient for optimising travelling rewards of both the lead and connected vehicle compared to the existing methodologies for signaling strategy design.

</details>


### [167] [Gauge-Invariant, Parameter-Insensitive Regularization for Potential Recovery from Flow on Directed Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13609)
*Mohammad Forouhesh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Recovering latent potentials on directed graphs is ill-posed; ridge regularization collapses and reverses ordering. The gauge-invariant graph Dirichlet energy avoids this, delivering stable estimates across wide λ ranges. This insight extends to graph neural networks, preventing oversmoothing in deep directed GCNs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ridge regularization for recovering latent potentials on directed graphs inadvertently collapses and reverses the recovered ordering due to gauge-meaningless origin shrinkage, making the inverse problem ill-posed.

Method: Replace ridge regularization with gauge-invariant graph Dirichlet energy (H¹ seminorm); prove the reduced solve is SPD and preserves dynamic range; localize absorbing boundaries via Poisson residual; validate on clickstream corpora and extend to GNNs.

Result: Gauge-invariant estimate retains 28–41% interior dynamic range across three public clickstream corpora, while ridge collapses to as little as 0.2%. Rank correlation against ground truth drops from +0.81 to -0.42 under ridge; gauge-invariant method is stable across four orders of magnitude in λ.

Conclusion: Gauge-invariant regularization removes the hazard of ridge shrinkage, yields parameter-insensitive recovery, and the same principle neutralizes the constant mode in GNNs to prevent oversmoothing, linking an inverse problem to graph learning.

Abstract: Recovering a latent potential from observed flow on a directed graph (a discrete Poisson problem with Dirichlet boundaries) is ill-posed, and the standard fix backfires: ridge regularization shrinks toward a gauge-meaningless origin, collapsing and reversing the recovered ordering ($+0.81\to-0.42$ rank correlation against a planted ground truth). The gauge-invariant graph Dirichlet energy removes the hazard and delivers parameter-insensitivity: the estimate is stable across four orders of magnitude in $λ$, whereas ridge inverts the ordering for every $λ>0$. We prove the reduced solve is SPD and preserves dynamic range exactly where ridge collapses it, and localize absorbing boundaries from flow alone via a Poisson residual. The $H^1$ seminorm is classical; what is new is the gauge diagnosis, the parameter-insensitivity it buys, and an ablation showing the result is robust to the extraction method. On three public clickstream corpora the gauge-invariant estimate retains $28$--$41\%$ of the interior dynamic range while ridge collapses to as little as $0.2\%$. The same gauge invariance carries into graph neural networks -- neutralizing the constant mode per layer prevents the oversmoothing that collapses a deep directed GCN -- linking this classical inverse problem to a central question in graph learning.

</details>


### [168] [The SIGReg Objective as Variational Free Energy: A Theoretical Active-Inference Account of JEPA World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13612)
*Fabio Arnez,Alexandra Gomez-Villa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper establishes a theoretical foundation for Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) by proving that their training objective can be interpreted as a valid Active Inference (AIF) variational free energy, contingent on the choice of anti-collapse regulariser. The authors organize four non-contrastive regularisers into an entropy-estimator hierarchy, prove a correspondence theorem linking JEPA objectives to information bottleneck and AIF terms under SIGReg regularization, and identify the state-epistemic value as the one AIF term missing from current JEPA world models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: JEPAs are widely used for latent world models but lack a normative (first-principles) justification; they are typically validated only empirically. The authors aim to provide a theoretical grounding by connecting JEPA objectives to Active Inference, a principled framework for perception and action under uncertainty.

Method: The paper analyzes four non-contrastive regularisers (VICReg, LogDet, PairDist, SIGReg), organizing them into an entropy-estimator hierarchy indexed by a prior-miscalibration gap. It proves a correspondence theorem showing that under SIGReg enforcement (producing isotropic-Gaussian embeddings), the JEPA objective becomes an exact information bottleneck and preserves AIF surprise bounds, while VICReg leaves an irreducible second-order anisotropy term. The analysis extends to multi-step expected free energy, ensemble epistemic value, and learned-policy regimes. Full proofs are provided in appendices and machine-verified in Lean 4.

Result: The sign of the prior-miscalibration gap decides whether the AIF surprise bound survives: VICReg and LogDet are unsafe upper bounds, PairDist a safe lower bound, and SIGReg eliminates the gap entirely. Under SIGReg, the objective becomes an exact information bottleneck and the latent goal cost becomes an exact proxy for AIF pragmatic value. The paper identifies that no current JEPA world model computes the state-epistemic value (a future-state coverage signal).

Conclusion: The choice of anti-collapse regulariser in JEPAs is not a minor implementation detail but determines whether the architecture is theoretically grounded as a valid AIF model. SIGReg is identified as the only regulariser among those studied that produces exact correspondence. The state-epistemic value is a key missing component in current JEPA world models, with predictions differing "in kind, not degree."

Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) are the dominant design for latent world models, yet they are usually justified by empirical performance rather than a normative principle. We show that the choice of anti-collapse regulariser determines whether a JEPA's training objective, a prediction loss plus a weighted embedding regulariser, is a valid Active Inference (AIF) variational free energy. We organise four non-contrastive regularisers (VICReg, LogDet, PairDist, and SIGReg) into an entropy-estimator hierarchy indexed by a prior-miscalibration gap, and show that the gap's sign, whether the estimator bounds the latent entropy from above or below, decides whether the AIF surprise bound survives: VICReg and LogDet are unsafe upper bounds, PairDist a safe lower bound, and SIGReg eliminates the gap. We then prove a correspondence theorem: under the standard constant-noise encoder model and successful SIGReg enforcement (isotropic-Gaussian embeddings), the gap vanishes, the objective becomes an exact information bottleneck, the surprise bound is preserved, and the latent goal cost becomes an exact proxy for AIF pragmatic value, whereas VICReg leaves an irreducible second-order anisotropy term. We extend the correspondence to multi-step expected free energy, ensemble epistemic value, and a learned-policy regime, and we identify the one AIF term no current JEPA world model computes: the state-epistemic value, a future-state coverage signal. The predictions differ in kind, not degree, and are stated here as theoretical consequences left for empirical test in separate work; full proofs are in Appendix A, and the algebraic core of every result is machine-verified in Lean 4 (Appendix D).

</details>


### [169] [FastCentNN: Accelerating Centroid Neural Network with Entropy Proxy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13613)
*Le-Anh Tran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FastCentNN accelerates Centroid Neural Network by introducing an early splitting strategy based on total centroid movement per epoch, reducing runtime by up to 16% on synthetic 2D datasets and ~5% on high-dimensional data while maintaining comparable clustering quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CentNN suffers from prolonged low-movement training phases before model expansion due to requiring strict local stabilization before centroid splitting, leading to inefficiency.

Method: FastCentNN introduces an early splitting strategy using total centroid movement per epoch as a training entropy proxy, with both absolute and stage-relative movement thresholds for adaptive or fixed splitting criteria.

Result: FastCentNN achieves clustering quality comparable to CentNN while reducing runtime by up to 16% on synthetic 2D datasets and about 5% on high-dimensional datasets.

Conclusion: FastCentNN is a practical, efficient drop-in replacement for CentNN that preserves online adaptive learning behavior and offers a configurable speed-stability trade-off through simple, interpretable splitting thresholds.

Abstract: Centroid neural network (CentNN) is an unsupervised competitive learning algorithm in which centroid splitting is triggered only after strict local stabilization, often leading to prolonged low-movement training phases before model expansion. This report proposes FastCentNN, an accelerated variant that addresses this inefficiency by introducing an early splitting strategy based on the total centroid movement per epoch, which serves as a training entropy proxy. As a result, FastCentNN reduces unnecessary reassignment epochs while preserving the original winner-loser learning dynamics. FastCentNN supports both absolute and stage-relative movement thresholds, allowing the splitting criterion to remain either fixed or adaptive throughout training. Experiments on some benchmark datasets show that FastCentNN consistently achieves clustering quality comparable to CentNN while reducing runtime by up to 16% on synthetic 2D datasets and about 5% on high-dimensional datasets. FastCentNN therefore provides a practical and efficient drop-in replacement for CentNN, retaining its online adaptive learning behavior while offering a simple and interpretable speed-stability trade-off through configurable splitting thresholds.

</details>


### [170] [How the Hessian-Spectrum of Neural Networks Depends on Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13631)
*Jasraj Singh,Enea Monzio Compagnoni,Antonio Orvieto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper derives the eigenvalues of the Hessian matrix for linear neural networks of arbitrary width/depth and datasets of arbitrary size, showing that for classification with MSE loss, solution sharpness relates to the maximum class proportion. Empirical validation confirms robustness of these predictions even when impractical assumptions are relaxed and nonlinearities are added.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a theoretical understanding of the Hessian matrix in deep learning beyond overly simplified settings, enabling better analysis of loss landscapes, optimization dynamics, generalization measures, and second-order learning algorithms.

Method: The authors derive closed-form expressions for Hessian eigenvalues of linear networks with arbitrary widths, depths, and dataset sizes. They then empirically validate by systematically relaxing impractical assumptions one at a time and incorporating nonlinearities.

Result: For classification with MSE loss, the sharpness of the solution is directly linked to the maximum proportion of samples belonging to any class. Theoretical predictions remain robust in most cases when simplifying assumptions are removed and nonlinearities are introduced.

Conclusion: The derived Hessian eigenvalue results for linear networks generalize well to more practical settings, offering a theoretically grounded understanding of loss landscape geometry in deep learning.

Abstract: The Hessian matrix is an important quantity of interest when it comes to studying the loss landscape and optimization dynamics in deep learning, as well as designing measures of generalization, second-order learning algorithms, etc. Prior works have focused on empirical results or pursued a theoretical treatment under overly simplified settings. In this work, we derive the eigenvalues of the Hessian of linear networks with arbitrary widths and depths, and datasets with an arbitrary number of samples, features, and labels. Importantly, for classification tasks with MSE loss, we identify that the sharpness of the solution is directly related to the maximum proportion of samples belonging to any class. We empirically validate our predictions and systematically analyze the effects of shedding the impractical assumptions one at a time, as well as incorporating nonlinearities. We observe that our predictions are considerably robust in most cases, allowing us to extend our conclusions to more practical learning setups.

</details>


### [171] [Consensus as Privileged Context for Label-Free Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13643)
*John Gkountouras,Josip Jukić,Ivan Titov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CANON converts consensus among multiple sampled solutions into dense, token-level supervision for self-distillation, improving reasoning accuracy without labels by up to 12 points pass@1 while using 1/7th the compute of label-free RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing consensus-based methods (filtering, preference, scalar reward) discard most information from agreeing solutions; the goal is to leverage the full distributional signal from consensus for more effective label-free training.

Method: For each unlabeled prompt, CANON samples multiple solutions, extracts the majority answer, conditions a frozen teacher on a solution that reaches that consensus answer, and uses this teacher to supervise the model's own rollouts at every token level.

Result: CANON improves pass@1 by up to 12 points, outperforms label-free RL by 6 points at 1/7th compute, approaches gold-solution teacher performance, and transfers to held-out benchmarks matching gold-label methods. The model solves problems it never solved in 32 attempts before.

Conclusion: Consensus-anchored token-level distillation is a highly efficient label-free training paradigm; improvements go beyond distribution sharpening as majority vote accuracy itself increases post-training.

Abstract: Sampling multiple solutions and returning the majority answer is among the most reliable ways to improve the reasoning accuracy of large language models without labels, and a growing family of methods converts this consensus signal into training supervision. However, existing approaches use consensus only in restricted forms: as a filter that selects solutions for fine-tuning, as a preference between answers, or as a scalar reward for reinforcement learning, discarding most of the information that the agreeing solutions contain. We present CANON (Consensus-ANchored self-distillatiON), a label-free training method that turns consensus into dense, token-level supervision. For each unlabeled prompt, CANON samples multiple solutions, extracts the majority answer, and conditions a frozen snapshot of the model on a solution that reaches it; this consensus-anchored teacher then supervises the model on its own rollouts at every token. Experiments on mathematical and scientific reasoning benchmarks show that CANON improves pass@1 by up to 12 points, outperforming label-free reinforcement learning by 6 points at a seventh of its compute and approaching a teacher conditioned on gold solutions; trained on pooled unlabeled data, it transfers to held-out benchmarks, matching training methods that use gold labels. Analysis suggests that the improvements are not pure distribution sharpening: after training, the model solves problems it previously never solved in 32 attempts, and its majority vote itself becomes more accurate.

</details>


### [172] [Maximally Robust Satisficing Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13652)
*Samuli Kinnunen,Petrus Mikkola,Antti Niskanen,Arto Klami*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Bayesian optimization method for finding satisficing (good enough) solutions that are robust to input perturbations during deployment, rather than finding the global optimum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many design tasks require a sufficiently good solution rather than the exact optimum, and there are often multiple satisfactory solutions forming a superlevel set. The key question is which one to prefer among them.

Method: The authors introduce a Bayesian optimization approach that efficiently searches for satisficing solutions within the superlevel set that are robust to maximally large input perturbations during deployment, assuming inputs are accurately controllable during optimization but perturbed afterward.

Result: The method identifies solutions that are robust to input perturbations, addressing a gap where previous works assumed perturbations during optimization rather than deployment.

Conclusion: Robustness to input perturbations during deployment is a compelling criterion for selecting among multiple satisfactory solutions, and the proposed Bayesian optimization method effectively finds such robust satisficing solutions.

Abstract: Many design tasks can be cast as black-box function optimization, enabling use of Bayesian optimization to find an ideal design with minimal number of trials. However, often we do not actually need the optimum but instead a sufficiently good solution is enough, for instance a material that is durable enough for its intended use. In most cases there are multiple satisfactory solutions, forming a superlevel set of the function, raising a key question of which one to prefer. We answer this by explaining why robustness to input perturbations that may occur when the solution is deployed is a good criterion and by introduce a Bayesian optimization method that efficiently finds satisficing solutions that are robust to maximally large perturbations. In contrast to previous works, we assume the inputs can be accurately controlled during optimization, but will be perturbed after the deployment.

</details>


### [173] [The Hyperspherical Geometry of CLIP Latent Space: A Semantic Mixture Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13660)
*Zijie Yu,Gaowen Liu,Ramana Rao Kompella,Philip S. Yu,Yue Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CLIP embeddings live on a hypersphere, yet prior probabilistic models use Gaussian assumptions. The paper proposes Mixtures of von Mises-Fisher (MovMF) distributions, learned via EM, to model this directional structure, yielding better long-tailed/OOD detection and semantic decomposition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing probabilistic interpretations of CLIP representations rely on Gaussian assumptions that fail to capture the inherent hyperspherical geometry (cosine similarity) and multimodal structure of the embedding space.

Method: Model the CLIP latent space using Mixtures of von Mises-Fisher (MovMF) distributions on the unit hypersphere, learned via Expectation-Maximization (EM), yielding closed-form likelihoods aligned with the hyperspherical geometry.

Result: Significantly improves long-tailed and out-of-distribution detection; enables sparse probabilistic combination of interpretable semantic concepts.

Conclusion: CLIP latent space is better characterized as a hyperspherical semantic mixture than an isotropic Gaussian, establishing a geometrically consistent probabilistic framework.

Abstract: Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining (CLIP) representations form a semantic embedding space governed by cosine similarity, reflecting an intrinsic hyperspherical geometry. However, existing probabilistic interpretations typically rely on Gaussian assumptions, which fail to capture this directional and multimodal structure. We propose a principled density model for the CLIP latent space based on Mixtures of von Mises-Fisher (MovMF) distributions defined on the unit hypersphere. Using the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm, we efficiently learn a probabilistic model in which each mixture component corresponds to a coherent semantic concept. This formulation yields a closed-form likelihood naturally aligned with hyperspherical geometry, enabling accurate and interpretable density estimation. Empirically, our model significantly improves long-tailed and out-of-distribution detection and provides a natural semantic decomposition, representing each embedding as a sparse probabilistic combination of interpretable concepts. These results suggest that CLIP latent space is more faithfully characterized as a hyperspherical semantic mixture rather than an isotropic Gaussian, establishing a simple and geometrically consistent probabilistic framework for modeling and understanding multimodal representations. Project page is available at https://xiaoyuzhizi.github.io/movmf-clip/.

</details>


### [174] [Optimal and Efficient Contextual Combinatorial Semi-bandits with General Function Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13686)
*Hao Qin,Chicheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SquareCB.Comb is a computationally efficient algorithm for contextual combinatorial semi-bandit (CCSB) with general reward function approximation, achieving minimax optimal $O(\sqrt{m A T \log |\mathcal{F}|})$ regret with no structural assumptions on actions beyond a cardinality bound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To solve the CCSB problem with general reward function approximation, where the learner must select combinatorial actions (subsets of arms) and receive per-arm rewards, while scaling to large arm sets without restrictive structural assumptions on the action space.

Method: Proposes SquareCB.Comb, an algorithm that at each round solves a convex optimization problem to sample a combinatorial action balancing exploration and exploitation. It requires no structural assumptions beyond a cardinality bound $m$ per action.

Result: Achieves minimax optimal regret bound of $O(\sqrt{m A T \log |\mathcal{F}|})$, matching state-of-the-art policy search-based algorithms in slate recommendation settings while generalizing to arbitrary combinatorial action structures and general reward function approximation.

Conclusion: SquareCB.Comb provides a computationally efficient, scalable solution for CCSB with optimal regret guarantees and minimal assumptions, generalizing beyond restricted settings like slate recommendation.

Abstract: We study the contextual combinatorial semi-bandit (CCSB) problem with general reward function approximation. At each round, the learner observes a context, selects a combinatorial action consisting of a subset of basic arms, and receives the reward of each selected arm; the goal is to maximize the cumulative reward over time. We propose SquareCB.Comb, a computationally efficient algorithm that, at each round, solves a convex optimization problem to sample a combinatorial action that balances exploration and exploitation. SquareCB.Comb scales to large arm sets and imposes no structural assumptions on the action set beyond a cardinality bound of $m$ on each combinatorial action. We prove that SquareCB.Comb achieves a minimax optimal regret bound of $O(\sqrt{m A T \log |\mathcal{F}|})$, where $A$ is the number of arms, $m$ is the maximum number of arms in a combinatorial action, $T$ is the time horizon, and $\mathcal{F}$ is the reward function class. In the realizable setting, this bound matches the state-of-the-art regret guarantees achieved by policy search-based algorithms in the more restricted slate recommendation settings, while simultaneously generalizing to arbitrary combinatorial action structures and general reward function approximation.

</details>


### [175] [Microstructure-Conditioned Surrogate Models for Graded Multiscale Optimization of Mycelium Composites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13688)
*J. Storm,I. B. C. M. Rocha,S. Schyck,K. Masania,F. P. van der Meer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper develops a hypernetwork-conditioned hybrid physics-data surrogate model for multiscale simulations of mycelium-woodchip composites, enabling efficient optimization of functionally graded microstructures; it achieves 42% peak stress reduction in a graded disk and extends to direct conditioning on manufacturing variables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-driven surrogates for microscale simulations require large datasets, especially when a range of microstructures is considered (as in multiscale optimization). The authors aim to overcome this data bottleneck by conditioning surrogates on microstructural variables using a hypernetwork, enabling accurate predictions with small datasets.

Method: A hybrid physics-data surrogate model is conditioned on microstructural variables via a hypernetwork. The approach is validated against full FE^2 simulations for a mycelium-woodchip composite. The surrogate is used to optimize a graded multiscale disk, and further extended to condition directly on manufacturing variables.

Result: The conditioned surrogate enables tractable multiscale simulations of functionally graded structures. In the optimized graded multiscale disk, peak stress is reduced by 42% compared to a disk with random microstructure.

Conclusion: Conditioned surrogate models are a practical route to engineer microscale for desired macroscale behavior, accelerating the development and design of sustainable materials with microarchitectured structures.

Abstract: Emerging sustainable materials increasingly rely on engineered hierarchy and microstructure to achieve control of their properties and mechanical behavior. Optimizing these materials with controllable microstructures requires efficient multiscale simulations. Data-driven surrogate models for the microscale can accelerate multiscale simulations, but require large amounts of data even for a fixed microstructure. When a range of microstructures is considered, as is the case in multiscale optimization, even more data is needed to train a surrogate. To overcome this challenge, we condition a hybrid physics-data surrogate on microstructural variables using a hypernetwork. This approach enables accurate predictions of multiscale mechanical behavior for a mycelium-woodchip composite material, even when trained on small datasets. The conditioned surrogate makes multiscale simulations of functionally graded structures tractable, and we validate it against a full FE^2 simulation. We optimize a graded multiscale disk, and reduce the peak stress by 42% compared to one with a random microstructure. Then, we go one step further, conditioning the network directly on manufacturing variables that can have a complex influence on the microstructure. This is a practical route to engineer the microscale for desired macroscale behavior. This contribution highlights the benefits of microarchitectured structures and demonstrates how conditioned surrogate models enable their multiscale optimization, which will accelerate the development and design of future sustainable materials and structures.

</details>


### [176] [Conditional Invertible Neural Networks for Data-Driven UAV Control: A 2-D Proof of Concept](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13703)
*Christian Wittke,Stephan Myschik,Oliver Niggemann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Conditional invertible neural networks (cINNs) can serve as probabilistic inverse-dynamics models for multirotor control. Using a planar X8 coaxial multicopter, a cINN trained on an INDI teacher achieves good open-loop performance (R²=0.944, CRPS=0.0915) and closed-loop position RMSE comparable to INDI (9.7 vs 9.5 m), with 47% tracking acceptable; failures stem from command bandwidth and data coverage issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether conditional invertible neural networks (cINNs) can serve as probabilistic inverse-dynamics models for multirotor control, potentially offering uncertainty-aware alternatives to traditional model-based controllers like INDI.

Method: A cINN was trained on an INDI teacher to learn the conditional distribution p(u | s_t, c_t) for a planar X8 coaxial multicopter, using rational-quadratic spline coupling and invertible linear mixing. Open-loop and closed-loop performance were evaluated over 15 scenarios.

Result: Open-loop R²=0.944, mean CRPS=0.0915, log-probability-error correlation ρ=-0.60. Closed-loop position RMSE=9.7 m (cINN) vs 9.5 m (INDI), with 47% tracking acceptably. Failures due to attitude divergence under aggressive steps and phase lag under high-frequency references.

Conclusion: cINNs can approximate INDI-level performance in closed-loop control, but command bandwidth and data coverage are critical failure modes. The probabilistic nature of cINNs provides useful uncertainty information (negative ρ indicates good calibration).

Abstract: We investigate conditional invertible neural networks (cINNs) as probabilistic inverse-dynamics models for multirotor control. For a planar X8 coaxial multicopter, we learn $p(u \mid s_t, c_t)$ from an incremental nonlinear dynamic inversion (INDI) teacher using rational-quadratic spline coupling and invertible linear mixing. Open-loop reproduction reaches $R^2 = 0.944$, mean CRPS 0.0915, and log-probability-error correlation $ρ= -0.60$. Over 15 closed-loop scenarios, position RMSE matches INDI (9.7 vs. 9.5 m), with 47 percent tracking acceptably; failures separate into attitude divergence under aggressive steps and phase lag under high-frequency references, isolating command bandwidth and data coverage as dominant failure mechanisms.

</details>


### [177] [DAGR: State-Conditioned Goal Representations via Difference-Aware Goal Cross-Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13731)
*Xing Lei,Wenyan Yang,Xuetao Zhang,Donglin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DAGR refines state-independent goal embeddings into state-conditioned ones using multi-scale gated cross-attention, improving navigation on OGBench but not universally across manipulation and puzzle tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing goal-conditioned RL encoders (contrastive, metric, temporal-distance, information-theoretic) all produce state-independent goal embeddings, which cannot mark which part of the goal still requires action, forcing the policy to recover this cue by inverting both encoders.

Method: DAGR refines any late-fusion encoder's static embedding into a state-conditioned one via multi-scale gated cross-attention, using a near-identity gated residual to preserve the base representation and a Difference-aware Goal Cross-Attention mechanism that biases attention scores with a per-token state-goal discrepancy map.

Result: DAGR improves navigation performance on OGBench; ablation studies show the gain comes from the gated residual, not the difference bias. On manipulation and puzzle tasks, DAGR matches or falls below the base encoder.

Conclusion: DAGR is a structured refinement that provides selective improvement (navigation) but is not a universal enhancement across all task types.

Abstract: Goal-conditioned reinforcement learning hinges on how the goal is encoded. Contrastive, metric, temporal-distance, and information-theoretic encoders differ in objective. They still share one trait. None of them sees the current state. Such a state-independent embedding cannot mark which part of the goal still needs action. The policy must then recover that cue by inverting both encoders. We propose DAGR. It refines the static embedding of any late-fusion encoder into a state-conditioned one through multi-scale gated cross-attention. A near-identity gated residual preserves the base representation. Difference-aware Goal Cross-Attention then biases the attention scores using a per-token state-goal discrepancy map. On OGBench, DAGR improves navigation. Our ablations trace the gain to the gated residual, not to the difference bias that names the method. On manipulation and puzzle tasks it matches or falls below the base. DAGR is a structured refinement, not a universal improvement.

</details>


### [178] [Constraint-Driven Model Optimization: An Industry Framework for Selecting Compression and Acceleration Techniques in Modern Machine Learning Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13735)
*Dhruv Shivkant,Saket Mohanty,Utkarsh Wadhwa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a constraint-driven, multi-objective framework for model optimization in production ML deployments, mapping techniques (quantization, pruning, distillation, PEFT, inference optimization) to five operational constraints: data availability, latency budget, memory budget, accuracy tolerance, and retraining budget. It synthesizes empirical results from literature and provides prescriptive decision pipelines for four industrial scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners rely on heuristics rather than principled methodology when selecting optimization techniques (quantization, pruning, distillation, PEFT, etc.) for production ML systems across cloud, edge, and enterprise environments. The paper aims to formalize model optimization as a constraint-aware engineering process.
