Method: The authors introduce a unified framework with five constraint dimensions (data availability, latency budget, memory budget, accuracy tolerance, retraining budget), synthesize empirical gains from recent literature mapped to operational constraints, and propose a prescriptive decision framework with four industrial scenario case studies.

Result: A structured categorization of optimization techniques by operational constraints rather than algorithmic categories, and prescriptive optimization pipelines for four representative industrial deployment scenarios.

Conclusion: This is one of the first structured attempts to formalize model optimization as a constraint-aware, multi-objective engineering process, synthesizing quantitative evidence from research literature to guide practitioners.

Abstract: The rapid deployment of machine learning systems across cloud, edge, and enterprise environments has brought model optimization to the forefront of systems-engineering. Despite a rich literature spanning quantization, pruning, knowledge distillation, parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT), and inference-time optimization, practitioners are often left navigating these techniques through heuristics rather than principled methodology. We argue that optimization should be formulated as a constraint-driven, multi-objective engineering decision and introduce a unified framework that characterizes any production deployment along five interacting constraint dimensions: data availability, latency budget, memory budget, accuracy tolerance, and retraining budget. Building on this taxonomy, we synthesize empirical gains reported across the research literature and map them to operational constraints rather than algorithmic categories. To ensure practical relevance, we selected these techniques by reviewing recent literature for methods that report measurable improvements against critical deployment bottlenecks. We propose a prescriptive decision framework and provide optimization pipelines for four representative industrial scenarios to illustrate it in practice. To the best of our knowledge, this work provides one of the first structured attempts to formalize model optimization as a constraint-aware, multi-objective engineering process, synthesizing quantitative evidence from the research literature.

</details>


### [179] [Implementations of Quantum and Classical Topology-Aligned Architectures for Molecular Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13737)
*James T. Pegg,Hubert Okadome Valencia,Ronin Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes topology-aligned inductive bias for parameter-efficient learning in quantum chemistry, instantiating it in quantum (Iso-QGNN) and classical (Iso-CGNN) architectures. With only 64 parameters, they achieve competitive AUCs on QM9 benchmarks (0.88/0.91 for HOMO-LUMO, ~0.78 for dipole), reaching 90% asymptotic performance within ~250 training molecules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve parameter-efficient learning in low-data and resource-constrained regimes typical of quantum chemistry, by designing inductive biases that align model architecture with molecular bond topology.

Method: Proposes topology-aligned inductive bias where atoms map to fixed computational registers and bonds determine pairwise interactions via shared learnable parameters. Implemented in two architectures: a variational quantum circuit (Iso-QGNN) and a parameter-matched classical message-passing model (Iso-CGNN).

Result: With 64 trainable parameters, achieves test AUCs ~0.88 (quantum) and ~0.91 (classical) on HOMO-LUMO gap task, and ~0.78 (both) on dipole moment task. Models reach 90% asymptotic performance within ~250 training molecules with stable gradient norms.

Conclusion: Topology-aligned inductive bias is the key driver of parameter efficiency at QM9 scale, with implications for matched-baseline benchmarking in quantum machine learning.

Abstract: For low-data and resource-constrained regimes typical of quantum chemistry, parameter-efficient learning is a key objective. Here, we propose a topology-aligned inductive bias in which the model architecture mirrors the molecular bond graph: atoms map to a fixed register of computational units, and bonds determine which pairs interact through shared learnable parameters. This principle is instantiated in two architectures: a variational quantum circuit (Iso-QGNN), and a parameter-matched classical message-passing model (Iso-CGNN). The models are benchmarked on HOMO-LUMO and dipole moment binary classification tasks over the QM9 benchmark. With 64 trainable parameters, the implementations achieve test AUCs of approximately 0.88 (quantum) and 0.91 (classical) on the gap task, and close to 0.78 (both) on the dipole task. The models reach 90% of asymptotic performance within about 250 training molecules and gradient norms remain stable throughout training. These results indicate that the topology-aligned inductive bias is the active ingredient driving parameter efficiency at QM9 scale, with implications for matched-baseline benchmarking in quantum machine learning.

</details>


### [180] [Algebraic Representability as the Limiting Regime of Grokking: An Exactly Solvable Model with Holomorphic Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13749)
*Chon-Fai Kam,Xavier Cadet,Miloud Bessafi,Frederic Cadet*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Networks with monomial activation σ(z)=z^k on modular arithmetic tasks collapse to a (k+1)-dimensional subspace of characters, where tasks are representable iff their Fourier support lies on diagonal u+v≡k (mod p). Non-representable tasks cannot be memorized even on training data, yielding binary success/failure with no grokking—this is the limiting case of the capacity-grokking relationship.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the extreme of model capacity where the expressible function class collapses to a finite-dimensional algebraic variety, and how this affects grokking—specifically, what happens when architecture's capacity is so constrained that memorization itself becomes impossible for certain tasks.

Method: Study two-layer networks with holomorphic monomial activation σ(z)=z^k trained on modular tasks encoded via roots of unity; provide algebraic characterization of the representable subspace via discrete Fourier support constraints; prove lower bound on training loss for non-representable targets; conduct 585 experimental runs and bottleneck ablation connecting this extreme to standard networks.

Result: Task representability requires Fourier support on diagonal u+v≡k (mod p); non-representable tasks yield a provable positive lower bound on training loss regardless of width; 99.8% accuracy across 585 runs; no memorization or grokking observed—outcomes are binary (instant success or outright failure).

Conclusion: This binary behavior is the limiting case of the capacity-grokking relationship: when the expressible class shrinks to a fixed algebraic object, whether a network groks reduces to whether it can represent the target at all. A continuous path exists from representational failure through memorization without generalization to grokking as capacity grows.

Abstract: Neural networks trained on modular arithmetic exhibit grokking, a delayed transition from memorisation to generalisation known to depend on model capacity: too little and the network memorises slowly or not at all, too much and it generalises almost immediately. What happens at the extreme of this spectrum, when the architecture's expressible function class collapses to a finite-dimensional algebraic variety? We study two-layer networks with a holomorphic monomial activation sigma(z)=z^k, trained on modular tasks encoded via roots of unity. Here the network output, regardless of hidden width, is confined to a (k+1)-dimensional subspace of characters of (Z_p)^2, an O(k/p^2) slice of the full function space. We give a complete algebraic characterisation of this subspace: a task is representable if and only if its discrete Fourier support lies on the diagonal u+v = k (mod p), which for linear-phase targets reduces to the arithmetic criterion m+n=k. This is not merely a constraint on eventual generalisation but on memorisation itself: because the outputs are algebraically confined, a non-representable target cannot be fit even on the training set, and we prove a positive lower bound on the training loss, independent of width. Across 585 runs the algebraic prediction matches the observed outcome with 99.8% accuracy, with no memorisation regime and no grokking; outcomes split cleanly into instant success and outright failure. This binary behaviour is the limiting case of the capacity-grokking relationship: when the expressible class shrinks to a fixed algebraic object, the question of when a network will grok dissolves into whether it can represent the target at all. A bottleneck ablation connects this extreme to standard networks, tracing a continuous path from representational failure, through memorisation without generalisation, to grokking with a shrinking gap as capacity grows.

</details>


### [181] [MxGPS: Multiplex Graph Transformers for a Power Grid Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13763)
*Charilaos Papaioannou,Ioannis Tsantilas,Dimitris Giannakakos,Vasilis Michalakopoulos,Sotiris Pelekis,Vangelis Marinakis,Arsam Aryandoust,Antonello Monti,Ricardo J. Bessa,Perdo P. Vergara,Jochen Cremer,Elissaios Sarmas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MxGPS is a multiplex graph transformer that addresses "topology overfitting" in GNNs for power grids by jointly training on Static State Estimation and AC Power Flow tasks, achieving 0% boundary violation on unseen topologies with 12x fewer parameters than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-task fine-tuning of GNNs for power grids systematically fails under topology shift due to "topology overfitting"—models encode relational structure specific to training topologies rather than underlying physics, causing failure on unseen grids despite strong in-distribution performance.

Method: MxGPS (Multiplex GPS) uses K task-specialised GPS branches over a shared node encoder, jointly trained on SSE and PF via self-supervised pre-training and multi-task fine-tuning, with a cross-branch attention module.

Result: 0% boundary violation rate (BVR) on all four zero-shot Power Flow topologies (14-, 24-, 162-, 300-bus); only 39% degradation under topology shift vs. 190%-1400% for models with lower in-distribution error; 1.6M parameters (12x fewer than GridFM).

Conclusion: Multi-task joint training is a principled and parameter-efficient mechanism for topology-agnostic generalisation in power grid foundation models, directly implicating topology overfitting as the failure mechanism rather than insufficient model capacity.

Abstract: Single-task fine-tuning of graph neural networks (GNNs) for power grid problems exhibits a systematic failure mode: models that achieve the lowest in-distribution error degrade the most under topology shift. We term this topology overfitting: the tendency of task-specific gradient signals to encode relational structure particular to the training topologies rather than the underlying physics, causing models to fail on unseen grids despite strong in-distribution performance. To expose and address this failure mode, we introduce MxGPS (Multiplex GPS), a multiplex graph transformer that runs K task-specialised GPS branches over a shared node encoder, jointly trained on Static State Estimation (SSE) and AC Power Flow (PF) via a self-supervised pre-training and multi-task fine-tuning protocol, with a cross-branch attention module evaluated in ablation. The joint SSE+PF objective forces the shared encoder to simultaneously satisfy complementary gradient signals, preventing it from overfitting to topology-specific relational structure. Under a 3-fold sliding-window cross-validation spanning four unseen topologies (14-, 24-, 162-, and 300-bus), MxGPS attains 0% boundary violation rate (BVR) on all four zero-shot Power Flow topologies. Critically, models with substantially lower in-distribution PF error degrade by 190% to 1400% under topology shift, whereas MxGPS degrades by only 39%, an inversion that directly implicates topology overfitting as the failure mechanism rather than insufficient model capacity. With only 1.6M parameters (12x fewer than the GridFM reference baseline), MxGPS demonstrates that multi-task joint training is a principled and parameter-efficient mechanism for topology-agnostic generalisation in power grid foundation models.

</details>


### [182] [Mono-Z Dark Matter Search with Neural Spline Flows Using CMS Run 2015D Open Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13771)
*Hitesh Rasineni,Bhavishya Chebrolu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a dark matter search using CMS Run 2015D open data (2.32 fb⁻¹) in the mono-Z→ℓ⁺ℓ⁻ final state, employing Neural Spline Flows to model per-event likelihood ratios for signal vs. background discrimination across 37 kinematic observables. A simultaneous profile-likelihood fit in μμ and ee channels yields 95% CL upper limits on signal strength for scalar, vector, and axial-vector mediators. The observed limits are weaker than expected due to a residual high-MET background modeling discrepancy, not evidence for DM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To search for dark matter produced in association with a leptonically decaying Z boson using CMS open data, employing a likelihood-ratio method based on Neural Spline Flows that avoids hard MET thresholds, thereby leveraging full kinematic phase space for improved sensitivity.

Method: Events are selected in mono-Z→ℓ⁺ℓ⁻ (μμ and ee) final states. 40 kinematic observables from MINIAOD/MINIAODSIM are reduced to 37 features via physics-motivated cleaning. Five Neural Spline Flows are trained independently to model SM background and mediator-specific DM signal densities. A per-event test statistic is built from the log-likelihood ratio of signal vs. background density estimates. A simultaneous profile-likelihood fit combines both channels.

Result: Observed (expected) 95% CL upper limits on signal strength: scalar mediator μ<0.0177 (0.0018), vector mediator μ<0.0362 (0.0039), axial-vector mediator μ<0.0498 (0.0069). Observed limits are weaker than expected due to residual high-MET background modeling discrepancy, not DM signal.

Conclusion: First application of Neural Spline Flow likelihood-ratio scoring to a mono-Z dark matter search using CMS Run 2015D open data simultaneously in μμ and ee channels. The method demonstrates potential for sensitivity without hard MET thresholds, though background modeling at high MET requires improvement.

Abstract: We report a search for dark matter (DM) produced in association with a leptonically decaying \(Z\) boson at \(\sqrt{s}=13\) TeV using CMS Run 2015D open data corresponding to an integrated luminosity of \(2.32\,\mathrm{fb}^{-1}\) together with simplified-model Monte Carlo simulation. Events are selected in the mono-\(Z\rightarrow\ell^+\ell^-\) final state in both the \(μμ\) and \(ee\) channels. Forty kinematic observables are extracted from MINIAOD and MINIAODSIM, cleaned with physics-motivated selections, and reduced to a 37-dimensional feature vector. Five Neural Spline Flows are trained independently to model Standard Model background and mediator-specific DM signal densities. The per-event test statistic is constructed from the log-likelihood ratio between the signal and background density estimates, providing sensitivity across the full kinematic phase space without requiring a hard upper \(\mathrm{MET}\) threshold. A simultaneous profile-likelihood fit combining the two channels yields observed (expected) 95\% confidence level upper limits on the signal-strength parameter of \(μ<0.0177\) (\(0.0018\)) for the scalar mediator, \(μ<0.0362\) (\(0.0039\)) for the vector mediator, and \(μ<0.0498\) (\(0.0069\)) for the axial-vector mediator. The observed limits are weaker than expected because of a residual high-\(\mathrm{MET}\) background-modeling discrepancy rather than evidence for a DM signal. To our knowledge, this is the first application of Neural Spline Flow likelihood-ratio scoring to a mono-\(Z\) dark matter search using CMS Run 2015D open data simultaneously in the \(μμ\) and \(ee\) channels.

</details>


### [183] [NodeImport: Imbalanced Node Classification with Node Importance Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13837)
*Nan Chen,Zemin Liu,Bryan Hooi,Bingsheng He,Jun Hu,Jia Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NodeImport is a framework for class-imbalanced node classification on graphs that uses a balanced meta-set to measure node importance, dynamically selecting valuable labeled, unlabeled, and synthetic nodes to improve model performance under unbiased settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Node classification on graphs suffers from class imbalance, where majority classes dominate training, causing biased performance. Existing solutions (e.g., prioritizing nodes by class size or synthesizing minority nodes) often fail to effectively address this imbalance.

Method: The paper proposes NodeImport, which (1) uses a balanced meta-set to measure node importance—a node is important if it boosts model performance in an unbiased setting; (2) derives a formula for direct node importance assessment with low overhead and intuitive threshold; (3) filters valuable labeled, unlabeled, and synthetic nodes; (4) separates synthetic node generation from filtering for compatibility with various generation methods; (5) constructs a high-quality meta-set approximating the overall feature distribution.

Result: NodeImport evaluated across multiple datasets and GNN architectures outperforms existing baselines, demonstrating flexibility and effectiveness in mitigating class imbalance.

Conclusion: NodeImport provides a fine-grained, dynamic node selection framework that effectively addresses class imbalance in graph node classification by separating generation from filtering and using a balanced meta-set for importance measurement.

Abstract: In real-world applications, node classification on graphs often faces the challenge of class imbalance, where majority classes dominate training, resulting in biased model performance. Traditional GNNs often struggle in such scenarios, as they tend to overfit to majority classes while underrepresenting minority classes. Existing solutions, which either prioritize nodes based on class size or synthesize new nodes for minority classes, often fall short of effectively addressing this imbalance issue. This paper introduces an approach to class-imbalanced node classification by utilizing a balanced meta-set for importance measurement, where a training node is considered significant if it enhances model performance under an unbiased setting. Our method identifies important nodes that can counteract class imbalance and utilizes them for model training, allowing for fine-grained and dynamic node selection throughout the training process. We theoretically derive a formula to directly assess node importance, reducing computational overhead and providing an intuitive threshold for node selection. Guided by this metric, we develop a novel framework that filters valuable labeled, unlabeled, and synthetic nodes that enhance model performance in an unbiased context. A key advantage of this framework is its separation of the synthetic node generation process from the filtering process, ensuring compatibility with various node generation methods. Furthermore, we introduce a strategy to construct a high-quality meta-set that closely approximates the overall feature distribution, ensuring robust representation of each class. We evaluate our framework, NodeImport, across multiple datasets using popular GNN architectures, demonstrating its superiority over existing baselines. Our results highlight the flexibility and effectiveness of the framework in mitigating class imbalance, leading to improved outcomes.

</details>


### [184] [Heavy-Tailed Flow Matching via Random Clocks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13841)
*Zhouhao Yang,Yezhen Wang,Kenji Kawaguchi,Vladimir Braverman,Haoyang Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HTFM replaces the standard Gaussian source in flow matching with a random-clock-driven Gaussian scale mixture (covering Gaussian, α-stable, and Student-t families), using truncated logsignature features to handle path-valued clocks efficiently, achieving better mode coverage and tail-statistic recovery on heavy-tailed data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard diffusion and flow-matching models use Gaussian noise/sources, which provide a poor inductive match for heavy-tailed data (imbalanced images, financial returns, weather extremes) where rare events carry disproportionate importance.

Method: Heavy-Tailed Flow Matching via Random Clocks (HTFM): heavy-tailed sources are modeled as mixtures of clock-conditioned Gaussian sources; conditioning on a clock path yields Gaussian flow, while marginalizing over the clock gives Gaussian scale mixtures (Gaussian, α-stable, Student-t). The path-valued clock is encoded via truncated logsignature features for efficient velocity field adaptation.

Result: On 2D imbalanced α-stable mixtures, CIFAR10-LT (long-tailed), and HRRR weather fields, HTFM improves mode coverage, sample quality, and tail-statistic recovery over Gaussian flow matching and heavy-tailed baselines, while retaining low-NFE sampling. Varying the clock law or tail parameter provides a practical tail-control interface.

Conclusion: HTFM offers a principled framework for heavy-tailed generation with better tail fidelity and flexible control, without sacrificing the computational efficiency of flow matching.

Abstract: Heavy-tailed data arise in many domains where rare events carry disproportionate importance, such as imbalanced image datasets, financial returns, and weather extremes. Standard diffusion and flow-matching models typically begin from Gaussian noise or Gaussian source distributions, which yield tractable training targets but provide a poor inductive match for heavy-tailed data. We propose Heavy-Tailed Flow Matching via Random Clocks (HTFM), a framework that portrays heavy-tailed sources as mixtures of clock-conditioned Gaussian sources. Conditioning on a given clock path, the source distribution and flow are Gaussian; marginalizing over the clock gives a Gaussian scale mixture covering Gaussian, $α$-stable, and Student-t families. To make the clock-conditioned vector field practical, we encode the path-valued clock using truncated logsignature features, allowing the velocity field to adapt to the realized conditional space with negligible overhead. Empirically, on 2D imbalanced $α$-stable mixtures, CIFAR10-LT, and HRRR weather fields, HTFM improves mode coverage, sample quality, and tail-statistic recovery over Gaussian flow matching and competitive heavy-tailed baselines, while retaining the low-NFE sampling advantage of flow matching. Moreover, the random-clock formulation further provides a practical tail-control interface: by varying only the clock law or tail parameter, the same architecture can calibrate the ``heaviness'' of generated tails across different distribution families.

</details>


### [185] [Relevance-Aware Rule: Structural Deletion of Irrelevant Conditions in Decision Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13874)
*Jung-Sik Hong,Jeongeon Lee,Min Kyu Sim,Sangheum Hwang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper identifies that irrelevant conditions (IRCs) in decision trees arise from the structural mechanism of binary splits (class-proportion shifts in opposite directions along sibling branches). The authors propose a structural IRC deletion framework that identifies mismatched links (C1/C0 links relative to leaf class), diagnoses their relevance via prediction reliability, and selectively deletes only those that are structurally and empirically irrelevant while preserving rule reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing IRC deletion methods either preserve the original tree too loosely (compromising reliability) or too strictly (limiting simplification), because they overlook the underlying structural mechanism of tree splitting that generates IRCs.

Method: The authors establish theoretical foundations (theorems/propositions) showing that binary splits shift class proportions in opposite directions — an increase in class-1 proportion on one branch implies an increase in class-0 proportion on the sibling, creating C1-links and C0-links. They propose a structural IRC deletion framework: (1) match links that increase the leaf-class proportion; (2) flag mismatched links (those increasing the opposite leaf-class) as structurally suspicious IRC candidates; (3) rigorously diagnose each candidate's relevance by assessing prediction reliability; (4) selectively delete structurally and empirically irrelevant conditions while strictly protecting those whose deletion would reduce reliability.

Result: Experimental results show the framework achieves substantial rule simplification without sacrificing the reliability of the original tree.

Conclusion: The structural IRC deletion framework successfully removes irrelevant conditions from decision trees by leveraging the underlying binary-split class-proportion shift mechanism, balancing simplification with reliability preservation.

Abstract: Decision trees generate interpretable if--then rules, yet they contain irrelevant conditions (IRCs). These IRCs arise from the structural mechanism of tree splitting and persist even in modern optimal sparse tree induction algorithms. Existing IRC deletion methods overlook this structural mechanism; therefore, they either preserve the original tree too loosely to remain reliable, or too strictly to achieve meaningful simplification. This study provides theoretical foundations for reliable IRC deletion by establishing theorems and propositions related to the underlying IRC mechanism. The key finding is that a binary split shifts class proportions in opposite directions relative to the parent. Specifically, an increase in the class-1 proportion along one branch necessitates an increase in the class-0 proportion along its sibling, thereby generating a C1-link and a C0-link. Based on this structural fact, we propose a structural IRC deletion framework. Relative to each leaf, links that increase the leaf-class proportion are matched, whereas links that increase the proportion of the opposite leaf-class are mismatched. These mismatched links are flagged as structurally suspicious IRC candidates. Rather than deleting them outright, the framework rigorously diagnoses their relevance by assessing prediction reliability. It selectively deletes conditions that are structurally and empirically irrelevant, while strictly protecting those whose deletion would reduce the rule's reliability. Experimental results confirm that the proposed framework achieves substantial rule simplification without sacrificing the reliability of the original tree.

</details>


### [186] [AI-Augmented Adaptive Digital Twin Modeling for Brain Tumor Evolution Prediction and Treatment Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13877)
*Wenxi Liu,Michael Trimboli,Xianqi Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An AI-augmented adaptive digital twin framework integrating reaction-diffusion models, 3D residual learning, and model predictive control for brain tumor evolution prediction and treatment scheduling achieves significant improvements in prediction accuracy (84.3% MSE reduction, 43.5% Dice improvement) and treatment efficacy (22.4% tumor burden reduction) compared to baseline methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Brain tumor progression exhibits spatially heterogeneous growth, patient-specific treatment response, and complex interactions with surrounding anatomy, making accurate long-term prediction challenging with existing methods.

Method: The framework integrates an interpretable reaction-diffusion (RD) model, a 3D residual learning module for model-form correction, patient-specific digital twin updating during recursive rollout, and model predictive control (MPC) for constrained chemotherapy and radiotherapy scheduling.

Result: Hybrid RD-residual modeling reduced masked voxel-wise MSE by 84.3% and increased Dice overlap by 43.5% relative to RD baseline. Online DT updating further reduced MSE by 45.9% and improved Dice by 9.6%. MPC-based scheduling reduced final tumor burden by 22.4% relative to fixed treatment schedule.

Conclusion: The unified framework for patient-specific initialization, mechanistic modeling, adaptive learning, and constrained treatment optimization establishes a foundation for future translation to real-world adaptive treatment planning, though validated using synthetic rather than clinical longitudinal data.

Abstract: Brain tumor progression exhibits spatially heterogeneous growth, patient-specific treatment response, and complex interactions with surrounding anatomy, making accurate long-term prediction challenging. We propose an AI-augmented adaptive digital twin (DT) framework for brain tumor evolution prediction and treatment scheduling. The framework integrates an interpretable reaction--diffusion (RD) model, a 3D residual learning module for model-form correction, patient-specific DT updating during recursive rollout, and model predictive control (MPC) for constrained chemotherapy and radiotherapy scheduling. Experiments on 387 synthetic tumor trajectories with 120-step evolution show that the baseline RD model captures tumor location and overall temporal behavior but underestimates heterogeneous tumor burden during long-horizon prediction. Hybrid RD--residual modeling reduces masked voxel-wise mean squared error by 84.3% and increases Dice overlap by 43.5% relative to the RD baseline under dense simulated observations. Online DT updating further reduces mean squared error by 45.9% and improves Dice overlap by 9.6% compared with the non-updated hybrid model. In MPC-based scheduling simulations, the updated DT controller reduces final tumor burden by 22.4% relative to a fixed treatment schedule under the terminal-burden objective. Together, these results demonstrate a unified framework for patient-specific initialization, mechanistic modeling, adaptive learning, and constrained treatment optimization. Although validated using patient-data-informed synthetic trajectories rather than clinical longitudinal data, the proposed framework establishes a foundation for future translation to real-world adaptive treatment planning.

</details>


### [187] [Task-Oriented Sensing and Covert Transmissions for Collaborative Multi-AUV Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13880)
*Xueyao Zhang,Chenyang Yan,Bo Yang,Xuelin Cao,Zhiwen Yu,Bin Guo,George C. Alexandropoulos,Merouane Debbah,Chau Yuen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes SVR-MARL, a multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that characterizes how perceptual information contributes to cooperative tasks under realistic communication and covert constraints, demonstrated through multi-AUV cooperative localization and tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In underwater covert missions, AUVs rely on passive observation with incomplete perception, and acoustic communications are constrained by delays, interference, low reliability, and covert exposure risks. Existing MARL studies model communication as ideal information flow, while communication optimization focuses on link-level performance—neither captures the actual contribution of perceptual information to cooperative tasks under realistic covert communication constraints.

Method: The SVR-MARL (Sensed Information Value Realization Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning) framework uses practical information to characterize the utility of information for cooperative tasks and learns distributed cooperative policies under realistic communication and covert constraints.

Result: Through a case study of covert multi-AUV cooperative localization and tracking, the framework demonstrates improved collaborative task efficiency while reducing unnecessary communication and exposure risks.

Conclusion: The proposed SVR-MARL framework effectively bridges the gap between communication-oriented MARL and traditional communication optimization by modeling the actual value of sensed information in cooperative tasks under realistic physical communication and covertness constraints.

Abstract: In underwater covert cooperative missions, autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs) often cannot rely on active sonar to continuously obtain complete information, since active sensing and frequent communications increase the risk of exposure. As a result, AUVs primarily rely on passive observation, an approach that yields incomplete local perception and limited task efficiency. Although underwater acoustic communications can mitigate this limitation through information sharing, they are simultaneously constrained by long delays, severe interference, low reliability, and the risk of covert exposure. Existing communications-oriented multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) studies often model communication as an ideal information flow, whereas traditional communication optimization primarily focuses on link-level performance. However, both are insufficient to characterize the actual contribution of perceptual information to cooperative tasks under realistic conditions of covert physical communications. This paper proposes a Sensed Information Value Realization Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (SVR-MARL) framework that leverages practical information to characterize the utility of information for cooperative tasks and learns distributed cooperative policies under realistic communication and covert constraints. Through a case study of covert multi-AUV cooperative localization and tracking, the potential of the proposed framework to improve collaborative task efficiency while reducing unnecessary communication and exposure risks is demonstrated.

</details>


### [188] [PiVoT: A Variational Solution for Real-time Large-scale Multi-object Detection and Tracking under Heavy Clutter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13891)
*Runze Gan,Qing Li,Simon J. Godsill,Mike E. Davies,James R. Hopgood*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PiVoT is a fast, clutter-resilient Bayesian multi-object tracker for noisy radar point clouds that achieves real-time, training-free detection and tracking of up to a thousand objects, outperforming existing trackers and matching deep-learning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Bayesian trackers based on Poisson measurement models are training-free but struggle with accuracy and efficiency under severe clutter, large object populations, and full-resolution Doppler point clouds in data-scarce radar applications.

Method: PiVoT performs end-to-end joint inference of object states, shapes, existence probabilities, data association, and measurement rates using variational inference innovations including theoretically justified birth pruning, quadratic-to-linear complexity reductions for exact updates, and a computationally efficient Doppler Poisson model.

Result: PiVoT substantially outperforms existing Bayesian trackers in challenging scenes, demonstrates exceptional scalability to a thousand objects, robustness to clutter visually inseparable from objects, and real-time operation on full-scale modern automotive radar datasets, attaining performance comparable to a deep-learning detection benchmark.

Conclusion: PiVoT provides an efficient, training-free solution that bridges the gap between classical Bayesian tracking and deep-learning-based detection in challenging radar environments.

Abstract: Multi-object detection and tracking from noisy point clouds remain challenging in many data-scarce radar applications. Current Bayesian trackers based on Poisson measurement models offer a training-free solution but struggle to achieve accuracy and efficiency under severe clutter, large object populations, and full-resolution Doppler point clouds. We address this with PiVoT, a fast, clutter-resilient multi-object tracker for both positional and Doppler measurements. PiVoT performs end-to-end detection and tracking of a large and time-varying number of objects without external clustering or detectors, through joint inference of object states, shapes, existence probabilities, data association, and measurement rates. Its efficiency is driven by several variational inference innovations, such as theoretically justified birth pruning, quadratic-to-linear complexity reductions for exact updates, and a computationally efficient Doppler Poisson model. Experiments show that PiVoT substantially outperforms existing Bayesian trackers in challenging scenes, while also demonstrating exceptional scalability to a thousand objects, robustness to clutter visually inseparable from objects, and real-time operation on full-scale modern automotive radar datasets, where it attains performance comparable to a deep-learning detection benchmark as a training-free joint detector and tracker.

</details>


### [189] [RF Spectrogram Anomaly Detection with Quantum Kitchen Sinks: Architecture, Representation, and Hardware Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13897)
*Abdallah Aaraba,Alexis Vieloszynski,Remon Polus,Ola Ahmad,Soumaya Cherkaoui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper extends Quantum Kitchen Sinks (QKS) with multi-depth data re-uploading and ring entanglement for RF spectrogram anomaly detection, demonstrating that DCT representations outperform raw/PCA inputs, moderate-depth entangled QKS configurations achieve best results (test AUROC 0.8778, F1 0.7995), and real-device validation on ibm_quebec QPU shows AUROC deviations below 0.013 from simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Anomaly detection is critical for secure spectrum management due to the broadcast nature of wireless channels exposing RF networks to anomalous and malicious transmissions; QKS offers a lightweight hybrid quantum feature map but its behavior on structured signal data is poorly understood.

Method: Extended standard QKS template with multi-depth data re-uploading and ring entanglement, evaluated via a validation-locked five-stage ablation protocol separating effects of shallow architecture, re-uploading depth, episode budget, input representation (raw, PCA, DCT), and classical readout.

Result: DCT representations dominate raw and PCA inputs; moderate-depth entangled QKS forms the strongest operating regime; QKS improves over matched classical baselines; best config achieves test AUROC 0.8778 and F1 0.7995; real-device validation on ibm_quebec QPU shows AUROC deviations below 0.013 from simulation.

Conclusion: Provides a practical, reproducible framework for deploying QKS-based anomaly detection in wireless networks, bridging real measured sub-6 GHz cellular signals and real-device QPU validation.

Abstract: The broadcast nature of wireless channels exposes radio-frequency (RF) networks to anomalous and malicious transmissions, making anomaly detection a fundamental requirement for secure spectrum management. Quantum Kitchen Sinks (QKS) offer a lightweight hybrid quantum feature map suitable for near-term quantum devices, yet their behavior on structured signal data remains poorly understood. In this paper, we extend the standard QKS template with multi-depth data re-uploading and ring entanglement, and evaluate the resulting pipeline on controlled RF spectrogram anomaly detection. We introduce a validation-locked five-stage ablation protocol that systematically separates the effects of shallow architecture, re-uploading depth, episode budget, input representation, and classical readout. Across the completed benchmark, Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) representations consistently dominate raw and Principal Component Analysis (PCA) inputs, moderate-depth entangled QKS configurations form the strongest operating regime, and QKS improves over matched classical direct-readout baselines across all evaluated representation-readout pairs on the held-out test set, with the best configuration reaching a test Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.8778 and a test F1 of 0.7995. The study bridges two levels of realism: real measured sub-6\,GHz cellular signals on the data side and real-device validation on the ibm_quebec Quantum Processing Unit (QPU) on the computing side, with AUROC deviations below 0.013 relative to simulation. These results provide a practical, reproducible framework for deploying QKS-based anomaly detection in wireless networks.

</details>


### [190] [An Efficient Newton Algorithm for Nonnegative Matrix Factorization with the Kullback-Leibler Divergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13919)
*Damien Lesens,Jérémy E. Cohen,Bora Uçar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Newton-type method for KL-divergence Nonnegative Matrix Factorization (KL-NMF) that uses second-order Taylor expansion instead of the conventional separable majorant approach, implemented via a generalized HALS algorithm that converges provably and outperforms state-of-the-art methods on diverse datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KL-NMF algorithms rely on minimizing a separable majorant of the loss, which the authors argue has reached its performance limits. They seek a more powerful optimization approach for this fundamental unsupervised learning task.

Method: The authors use the second-order Taylor expansion of the KL divergence loss (a Newton-type method) instead of the standard separable majorant. They minimize this non-separable surrogate by generalizing the Hierarchical Alternating Least Squares (HALS) algorithm, resulting in a provably convergent KL-NMF algorithm.

Result: The proposed Newton-type KL-NMF algorithm competes favorably with state-of-the-art algorithms across a large variety of datasets.

Conclusion: Moving beyond first-order separable majorants to second-order Newton-type optimization, combined with a generalized HALS scheme, yields a more efficient and provably convergent KL-NMF algorithm that outperforms existing methods.

Abstract: Nonnegative Matrix Factorization (NMF) is a fundamental tool in unsupervised learning, which approximates a nonnegative matrix by the product of two low-rank nonnegative factors. The Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence is best suited to measure the data to model discrepancy when the decomposed data sample follows a Poisson distribution, which is the case for count datasets such as term-document matrices or images. Most KL-NMF algorithms in the literature minimize a separable majorant of the loss to find their next iterate. We argue that this method has reached its limits and propose to use instead the second-order Taylor expansion of the loss, leading to a Newton-type method. We minimize this non-separable surrogate by proposing a generalization of the well-known HALS algorithm. This yields an efficient KL-NMF algorithm which provably converges and which competes favorably with state-of-the-art algorithms on a large variety of datasets.

</details>


### [191] [VAIOM: Continuous-Input, Discrete-Output Decoder-Only Financial Sequence Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13929)
*Yiming Ma,Xinyu Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VAIOM is a decoder-only Transformer that models probabilistic next-return on FX bars by combining continuous input vectors with categorical output distributions, outperforming LightGBM baselines with 0.029-0.043 bits/event gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial data is continuous, heterogeneous, and noisy, but standard decoder-only next-token models are designed for discrete symbolic inputs, creating a representation mismatch that limits modeling fidelity.

Method: VAIOM separates input representation (continuous multivariate financial-event vectors) from output likelihood (categorical distribution over volatility-normalized return buckets), using a Hybrid Continuous Input model with Mixture-of-Market-States return head, auxiliary objectives (Gap, volatility-regime, Ordinal), and full-sequence supervision.

Result: Across three seeds, all models outperform LightGBM baselines (0.029-0.043 bits/event gains). Continuous input outperforms discrete input; full-sequence supervision beats last-position training; auxiliary objectives and mixture-structured head improve likelihood. Smallest complete architecture achieves best validation likelihood.

Conclusion: Continuous input representation with categorical output likelihood is effective for financial return modeling, and careful architectural choices (mixture heads, auxiliary losses, full-sequence supervision) yield consistent gains over strong baselines.

Abstract: Financial observations are continuous, heterogeneous, and noisy, whereas decoder-only next-token models are usually built around discrete symbolic inputs. We introduce Vector-Input Autoregressive Inference for Ordinal-Return Modeling (VAIOM), a decoder-only Transformer for probabilistic next-return modeling on one-hour foreign-exchange bars. VAIOM separates input representation from output likelihood: continuous multivariate financial-event vectors preserve numerical structure at the input, while a categorical distribution over the next volatility-normalized return bucket supports cross-entropy training and likelihood evaluation. The selected 0.9M Hybrid Continuous Input model combines continuous event features with categorical asset metadata, a Mixture-of-Market-States return head, Gap, volatility-regime, and Ordinal auxiliary objectives, and full-sequence supervision. Models and preprocessing are fit using pre-2024 Train data; models are selected on 2024H2 Validation and evaluated without refitting on two 2025 Test periods. Across three independent training seeds, every model outperforms fixed single-bar LightGBM baseline in both Test halves. For the canonical checkpoint, paired gains over LightGBM are 0.029 and 0.043 bits per event. Validation experiments show that continuous input improves over discrete-token input under the same categorical return objective, full-sequence supervision improves over last-position training, and auxiliary representation shaping together with a mixture-structured return head improves return likelihood in controlled comparisons. A supporting capacity study finds that the smallest evaluated complete architecture rung achieves the strongest Validation likelihood on the present corpus.

</details>


### [192] [TRACE: Turn-level Reward Assignment via Credit Estimation for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13988)
*Leitian Tao,Baolin Peng,Wenlin Yao,Tao Ge,Hao Cheng,Mike Hang Wang,Jianfeng Gao,Sharon Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRACE (Turn-level Reward Assignment via Credit Estimation) is a dense credit-assignment method for agentic reinforcement learning that uses log-ratio state values from a frozen reference model to derive per-action rewards via Temporal-Difference, eliminating the need for additional critics or process-label training. It significantly improves multi-turn agent performance on long-horizon search tasks, raising Qwen3-4B from 7.2 to 35.6 on BrowseComp-Plus without cold-start SFT or live-web training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-turn agents face sparse and high-variance outcome rewards in long trajectories, where a single failed rollout may contain many useful actions that are unfairly penalized. Outcome-only training cannot properly assign credit across tens or hundreds of tool calls, motivating a dense credit-assignment approach.

Method: TRACE represents rollouts as state transitions at tool-call boundaries, obtains gold-answer log-probabilities from a frozen reference model, transforms them into log-ratio state values, and derives per-action rewards as Temporal-Difference changes in those values. The one-step log-ratio TD component telescopes across redundant tool calls, requiring no additional critic or process-label training.

Result: On BrowseComp-Plus, TRACE raises Qwen3-4B from 7.2 to 35.6 and Qwen3-30B-A3B from 8.4 to 42.6. It also transfers to open-web benchmarks and shows earlier improvement and faster convergence during RL training.

Conclusion: TRACE substantially improves base-model tool-use ability using pure RL, without requiring cold-start supervised fine-tuning, agentic mid-training, or training on live-web data.

Abstract: Multi-turn agents solve complex tasks through extended sequences of tool interactions before producing a final answer, making credit assignment a fundamental challenge during post-training. Outcome rewards provide reliable supervision for short-horizon reasoning, but become sparse and high-variance as trajectories grow to tens or hundreds of tool calls. They can also be misleading: a failed rollout may contain many useful actions that move the agent closer to the goal, yet outcome-only training assigns them the same negative advantage as the eventual mistake. We propose TRACE (Turn-level Reward Assignment via Credit Estimation), a dense credit-assignment method for agentic reinforcement learning. TRACE represents rollouts as state transitions at tool-call boundaries, obtains gold-answer log-probabilities from a frozen reference model, transforms them into log-ratio state values, and derives per-action rewards as Temporal-Difference changes in those values. This requires no additional critic or process-label training, and its one-step log-ratio TD component telescopes across redundant tool calls. On long-horizon complex search, TRACE substantially improves base-model tool-use ability using pure RL, without a cold-start supervised fine-tuning stage, an agentic mid-training stage, or training on live-web data. On the closed-web BrowseComp-Plus benchmark, it raises Qwen3-4B from $7.2$ to $35.6$ and Qwen3-30B-A3B from $8.4$ to $42.6$. The learned search behavior also transfers to open-web benchmarks, and the learning curves show earlier improvement and faster convergence during RL training.

</details>


### [193] [Lyapunov Exponent as Physics-Informed Dense Reward: RL Discovery of Stabilization Beyond the Kapitza Pendulum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14001)
*Slava Andrejev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes using the Lyapunov characteristic exponent (LCE) as a dense reward signal in reinforcement learning to stabilize an inverted pendulum with vertical motion, resulting in the agent discovering both the Kapitza pendulum oscillatory motion and damping the pendulum to a strictly upright position.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of stabilizing an inverted pendulum with vertical motion using reinforcement learning, by providing a more informative dense reward signal (LCE) compared to sparse rewards.

Method: Using the Lyapunov characteristic exponent (LCE) as a dense reward signal for reinforcement learning to stabilize an inverted pendulum with vertical motion.

Result: The agent successfully discovered the oscillatory motion known as the Kapitza pendulum and also damped the pendulum's pivoting to leave it in a strictly upright position.

Conclusion: LCE serves as an effective dense reward signal for reinforcement learning in pendulum stabilization problems, enabling the discovery of both oscillatory and strictly upright stabilization strategies.

Abstract: We suggest using the Lyapunov characteristic exponent (LCE) as a dense reward signal for the reinforcement learning problem of stabilizing the inverted pendulum with vertical motion. With LCE, the agent not only successfully found the oscillatory motion known as the Kapitza pendulum but also damped the pendulum's pivoting, leaving it in a strictly upright position.

</details>


### [194] [Lighthouse RL: Sample-Efficient Circuit Optimization via Strategic Reset Points](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14008)
*Mustafa Emre Gürsoy,Stefan Uhlich,Ryoga Matsuo,Yağız Gençer,Arun Venkitaraman,Chia-Yu Hsieh,Andrea Bonetti,Eisaku Ohbuchi,Lorenzo Servadei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Lighthouse RL introduces a strategic reset strategy for reinforcement learning-based analog circuit sizing, using previously discovered high-performing configurations ("lighthouses") to initialize episodes, achieving up to 1.72x faster optimization, 100% success rate, and 75% extrapolation success across benchmark and analog circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional methods for analog circuit sizing lack generalization across different performance targets, and standard RL approaches waste computational resources exploring unpromising regions, leading to sample inefficiency for expensive black-box optimization problems.

Method: The method uses a strategic reset strategy that initializes RL episodes from high-performing configurations ("lighthouses") discovered during training, which are closer to target objectives, guiding exploration toward promising regions. This reset strategy can be used as a plug-and-play enhancement for any RL-based optimization approach.

Result: The approach demonstrates significant improvements over RL and Bayesian optimization baselines: up to 1.72x faster sample efficiency, 100% optimization success rate (vs. 0-87%), 75% extrapolation success (vs. 0-50%), and enhanced objective maximization on a 2D benchmark and two analog circuits.

Conclusion: Lighthouse RL offers a sample-efficient, generalizable plug-and-play enhancement for RL-based analog circuit sizing, particularly valuable for computationally expensive black-box optimization problems.

Abstract: In this paper, we introduce Lighthouse RL, a sample-efficient reinforcement learning (RL) approach for analog circuit sizing. Traditional methods lack generalization across different performance targets, while standard RL approaches waste resources exploring unpromising regions. Our method addresses these inefficiencies through a strategic reset strategy that initializes episodes from high-performing configurations discovered during training, called "lighthouses". These states, which are closer to the target objectives, guide exploration toward promising regions. When compared to RL and Bayesian optimization methods from the literature, we demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach on a 2D benchmark problem and on two analog circuits, showing significant improvements in sample efficiency (up to 1.72x faster), optimization performance (100% vs. 0-87% success rate), generalization (75% vs. 0-50% extrapolation success), and objective maximization. This efficiency is particularly valuable for computationally expensive black-box optimization problems, and our reset strategy can be used as a plug-and-play enhancement for any RL-based optimization approach.

</details>


### [195] [Transforming Rank: How Architecture Navigates the Spectral Pathologies of Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14018)
*Katie Everett*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes how Transformer feedforward block components (skip connections, normalization, two-matrix structure) affect gradient rank preservation across depth. Skip connections and normalization trade off rank collapse against ensemble-like behavior by controlling the branch-to-skip ratio. The two-matrix feedforward design (expansion-contraction) preserves rank via decorrelation and width expansion following Marchenko–Pastur law. Rank of input–output Jacobian predicts trainability on CIFAR-10.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how individual architectural components of Transformer feedforward blocks (skip connections, normalization, two-matrix structure) contribute to preserving gradient rank across depth, and to unify normalization placement and depth scaling literature through the lens of rank dynamics.

Method: The authors reinterpret skip connections and normalization as mechanisms for preserving gradient rank across depth, controlled by the branch-to-skip ratio. They analyze the two-matrix expansion-contraction structure: the second matrix decorrelates a coherent mean spike that would otherwise grow across blocks, while the width expansion between matrices keeps the branch Jacobian full rank by applying rank-reducing activations in an expanded space (following Marchenko–Pastur law).

Result: Skip connections route gradients around the residual branch to avoid rank loss. Normalization placement (Pre-Norm vs Post-Norm) controls the branch-to-skip ratio, explaining why rank collapses for Post-Norm but plateaus for Pre-Norm. The second matrix in the feedforward block decorrelates growing mean spikes, and width expansion preserves full rank of the branch Jacobian. The initialization rank of the input–output Jacobian predicts which networks train on CIFAR-10.

Conclusion: Architecture design for deep networks involves an intrinsic tradeoff among rank collapse, ensemble-like behavior, and parameter count. The findings recast normalization placement and depth scaling literature under a unified rank-preservation framework.

Abstract: We investigate how each component of the Transformer feedforward block architecture design determines how much rank survives across depth at initialization. We reinterpret skip connections and normalization, long understood as controlling magnitude, as mechanisms for preserving gradient rank across depth, since the very matrix multiplications and nonlinear activations that make the network expressive also reduce the rank. We show that skip connections trade off rank collapse against ensemble-like behavior, controlled by the relative scales of the branch and the skip: skip connections route the gradient around the residual branch, where rank is lost, rather than along the long gradient paths that encourage the layers to compose. The placement of the normalization layer controls this same tradeoff by setting the branch-to-skip ratio across depth, unifying much of the normalization placement and depth scaling literature, in particular why rank collapses for Post-Norm but plateaus for Pre-Norm. Other aspects of the architecture, like the two-matrix structure that expands and contracts the width, use additional parameters to preserve the representation or branch Jacobian rank. The second matrix decorrelates a coherent mean spike that would grow across blocks with a single matrix and uncentered activation, preventing the residual representation from collapsing. The width expansion between the two matrices keeps the branch Jacobian full rank: applying the rank-reducing activation in this expanded space leaves enough directions to span the original, at a width that follows a Marchenko--Pastur law. The initialization rank of the input--output Jacobian predicts which networks train on CIFAR-10. Taken together, we recast architecture design for deep networks as navigating an intrinsic tradeoff among rank collapse, ensemble-like behavior, and parameter count.

</details>


### [196] [Improving Wind and Solar Power Prediction with Efficient Wrapper-based Feature Selection: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14024)
*Daniel Grillmeyer,Marius Hadry,Michael Stenger,Vanessa Borst,Veronika Lesch,Samuel Kounev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes Cluster-based Sequential Feature Selection (CSFS), a novel method for automatic feature selection in renewable energy prediction (wind turbine power and photovoltaic power), achieving comparable performance to traditional SFS with 21% less computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Renewable energy prediction is critical due to the variable nature of sources like wind and solar, yet existing feature selection methods for these tasks are limited or unsystematic despite the abundance of monitoring variables.

Method: The authors conduct structured literature reviews on wind turbine power curve modeling and PV power prediction, then propose CSFS—a model-agnostic, clustering-based wrapper method for automatic feature selection. They evaluate it against SFS, filter-based methods, and Random Forest embedded feature importance on both use cases.

Result: Wrapper-based methods (CSFS and SFS) outperform filter and embedded methods. CSFS achieves comparable predictive performance to SFS while reducing computational cost by an average of 21%.

Conclusion: CSFS offers an efficient, reliable, and reproducible feature selection approach for renewable energy prediction pipelines, addressing a key gap in current practice.

Abstract: With rising global energy demand and growing awareness of climate change and its impacts, the share of renewable energies in the global energy mix continues to grow. Unlike conventional power generation, the output of renewable energy sources cannot be controlled as consistently due to their dependence on environmental conditions. Therefore, reliable prediction of current and future energy production is essential. In this paper, we report findings from two structured literature reviews on real-world renewable energy prediction tasks: wind turbine power curve modeling and photovoltaic power prediction. For the former, we conducted a comprehensive literature review ourselves, while for the latter, we synthesize the key findings regarding frequently selected input features based on an existing survey. Across both domains, our analysis reveals that despite the large number of available monitoring and environmental variables, only limited or unsystematic methods for feature selection exist. To address this gap, we propose Cluster-based Sequential Feature Selection (CSFS), a novel, model-agnostic, clustering-based wrapper method for automatic, efficient, and reliable feature selection in renewable energy prediction pipelines. To support reproducibility and reuse, we provide an open-source implementation of CSFS on GitHub. We empirically evaluate the proposed approach on both use cases and compare it with established feature selection techniques such as wrapper-based sequential feature selection (SFS), filter-based methods, and Random Forest's embedded feature importance. The results show that the wrapper-based methods overall provide better-performing selections of features. CSFS achieves a predictive performance comparable to SFS while reducing computational cost by an average of 21%.

</details>


### [197] [Linear Independent Component Analysis via Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14081)
*Ashutosh Jha,Michel Besserve,Simon Buchholz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes OT-ICA, a new ICA algorithm that uses the squared Wasserstein distance to a Gaussian as a measure of non-Gaussianity, proving its theoretical validity and demonstrating superior empirical performance over proxy-based methods on simulated data and real-world tasks like EEG artifact removal and econometric price discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical ICA algorithms rely on proxy contrast functions (e.g., fourth-order cumulants, parametric log-likelihoods) to approximate negentropy optimization, which is intractable. The authors aim to replace these proxies with a principled, distribution-free measure of non-Gaussianity using optimal transport.

Method: The paper proves that the squared Wasserstein distance $W_2^2$ between a standard normal distribution and linear projections of the data is maximized when the projection recovers an independent component. Based on this, OT-ICA performs gradient-based optimization to find these projections.

Result: OT-ICA outperforms proxy-based ICA methods on simulated data across different source distributions. It is successfully applied to EEG artifact removal and econometric price discovery without requiring distributional assumptions.

Conclusion: The Wasserstein distance provides a theoretically sound and empirically effective alternative to proxy-based non-Gaussianity measures for ICA, enabling practical use without distributional assumptions.

Abstract: Linear Independent Component Analysis (ICA) recovers jointly independent source signals from their linear mixtures. To achieve this, classical ICA algorithms attempt to maximize non-Gaussianity, measured by negentropy, which is linked to independence by information theory. Because exact negentropy optimization is intractable, they rely on proxy contrast functions, such as fourth-order cumulants, and parametric log-likelihoods. We propose instead to measure non-Gaussianity using the squared Wasserstein distance $W_2^2$ to a standard Gaussian. We prove that the Wasserstein distance between a standard normal distribution and linear projections of the data is maximized when the projection recovers an independent component. Based on this observation, we propose the OT-ICA algorithm which finds this projection by gradient-based optimization. Empirical evaluation on simulated data shows that OT-ICA outperforms proxy-based methods for different distributions of the latent variables. Application to EEG artifact removal and econometric price discovery confirm OT-ICA can be used for applied ICA tasks without distributional assumptions.

</details>


### [198] [Leveraging unlabelled data for generalizable neural population decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14086)
*Ximeng Mao,Nanda H. Krishna,Avery Hee-Woon Ryoo,Matthew G. Perich,Guillaume Lajoie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MOJO is a training framework that combines self-supervised learning (masked autoencoding) with supervised learning for spike-tokenizing neural decoders, outperforming purely supervised models across multiple datasets (monkey motor cortex, mouse multi-region, human ECoG), especially in low-label regimes, while also improving interpretability and generalizing to other neural modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current spike-based neural decoding models are restricted to supervised learning, which limits training to datasets with paired behavioral labels. This prevents leveraging the abundant unlabeled neural data available across sessions and subjects.

Method: MOJO jointly trains spike-tokenizing models using both self-supervised learning (via masked autoencoding) and supervised learning objectives, enabling the model to learn from both labeled and unlabeled data.

Result: MOJO outperforms purely supervised models on monkey motor cortex, mouse multi-region (vision/decision making), and human ECoG (speech) datasets. Improvements are especially pronounced in few-shot finetuning with limited labeled data. SSL also yields better interpretability for brain region classification and spike-statistics prediction, and achieves performance comparable to neuro-foundation models designed for continuous signals.

Conclusion: Augmenting spike-tokenizing models with SSL improves performance in label-impoverished settings and enables flexible use of unlabeled data across tasks, species, and neural modalities, suggesting a path towards more scalable training of neuro-foundation models.

Abstract: Robust and accurate neural decoders are integral to neurotechnologies such as brain-computer interfaces and closed-loop experiments. Recent work has shown that tokenizing neural data at the spike level facilitates multi-session pretraining and delivers state-of-the-art decoding performance. However, current spike-based models are restricted to supervised learning (SL), limiting training to datasets with paired behavioural labels. To address this limitation, we introduce MOJO (Masked autOencoder-based JOint training), a training framework for spike-tokenizing models that jointly leverages self-supervised learning (SSL) via masked autoencoding and SL objectives. We evaluate MOJO on three spiking datasets spanning monkey motor cortex during reaching tasks and multi-regional mouse recordings during vision and decision making tasks, demonstrating superior performance over purely SL-trained models. This improvement is especially pronounced when training with limited labelled data, particularly in few-shot finetuning, where only a small amount of labelled data from a new session is available. Incorporating SSL also yields more interpretable neuronal representations, improving performance on brain region classification and spike-statistics prediction without explicit optimization for these tasks. We further show that MOJO generalizes beyond spiking data to human electrocorticography during speech, where it continues to outperform purely SL-trained models and achieves performance comparable to neuro-foundation models (NFMs) designed specifically for continuous signals. Overall, augmenting spike-tokenizing models with SSL improves performance in label-impoverished settings and enables the use of unlabelled data across various tasks and species, while generalizing to other neural modalities. These results suggest a path towards more flexible and scalable data usage when training NFMs.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [199] [Falsifiable Release Gates for Self-Improving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13070)
*Deepak Soni*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper introduces falsifiable release gates for self-improving AI agents, demonstrated in the Antahkarana runtime, where every capability must pass machine-verifiable acceptance tests across seven gates, with safety-critical token-based control and a positively constrained self-enhancement loop. The system's bounded model checking covers 1M reachable states, and all results are published for reproducibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety claims for self-improving agent runtimes rely on self-graded assessments (policy files, guardrails, README commitments) which are not falsifiable or machine-verifiable, lacking rigorous acceptance criteria before deploying new capabilities.

Method: Defines falsifiable release gates with pre-specified, machine-verifiable acceptance suites. Applied to Antahkarana runtime across seven gates (from basic observability to self-governing loop). Uses a control ring to mint safety-critical capability tokens, exhaustively machine-checked over 1M reachable states of a bounded model, re-checked against execution traces. Self-enhancement is positively constrained: write surface limited to policy rules; tightening changes auto-apply, loosening changes require human merge; proposals that mispredict their own effect are auto-closed.

Result: Published acceptance measurements for all seven gates. A purposely broken model yields the shortest counterexample, demonstrating the checker has teeth. The bounded model covers the coordination skeleton (not learned components). Released runtime with CLI tools and gate suite for reproducibility; reviewers can run the central non-bypass check in seconds with a single command.

Conclusion: Falsifiable release gates provide a rigorous methodology for safety in self-improving agent runtimes, enabling reproducible, machine-verifiable acceptance criteria that can be applied to other agent frameworks beyond Antahkarana.

Abstract: Safety claims on self-improving agent runtimes are almost always self-graded: a policy file, a guardrail, or a README commitment. We describe falsifiable release gates, and a methodology to build and validate such systems, such that every new capability must pass a pre-specified, machine-verifiable acceptance suite before it ships, and a fixed set of standing invariants is preserved at each gate. I think we applied the method in the Antahkarana, an open runtime, via seven gates from basic observability into a self governing loop that suggests changes to its own policy. no action goes to an effector without a safety-critical property capability token minted by a control ring is exhaustively machine-checked over the one million recorded reachable state space of a bounded model and re-checked against execution traces. A purposely broken model gives the shortest counterexample, so the checker has teeth, is apparent. The self-enhancement loop is positively Constrained: the entire write surface is policy rules, tightening changes. may auto-apply loosening changes always require a human merge and a proposal autoclosed is one that mispredicts its own effect; We publish the acceptance measured results for all the seven gates, define precisely the scope of each claim (a bounded of the coordination skeleton (not the learned components) and free the runtime, either command line tools and the gate suite, so the results reproduce, and gates can run against other agent frameworks. Reviewers may repeat the single command central non-bypass in seconds.

</details>


### [200] [Compaction as Epistemic Failure: How Agentic LLM Tools Fabricate Confirmed Results from Killed Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13071)
*Hiroki Tamba*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Claude Code's compaction summaries incorrectly treat partial standard output from timed-out commands (exit code 143) as confirmed results, propagating false positives across sessions and model versions without re-verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify and document a failure mode in agentic LLM coding tools where incomplete command output is misrepresented as ground truth in session compaction summaries.

Method: Analysis of the compaction mechanism in Claude Code, specifically examining how exit code 143 (timed-out commands) leads to partial standard output being recorded as confirmed results in summary inheritances across sessions.

Result: The compaction mechanism conflates observation (terminal appearance) with persistence (durable storage), causing false positives to propagate across sessions and model versions without re-verification.

Conclusion: This failure mode extends prior work on LLM-as-judge non-determinism by showing analogous reliability deficits in agentic tools' self-reporting, with direct implications for workflows relying on agentic session continuity for data processing, scientific computation, or multi-step automation.

Abstract: Agentic LLM coding tools compress long session histories into compaction summaries that subsequent sessions inherit as ground truth. This paper documents a failure mode in Claude Code where partial standard output from timed-out commands (exit code 143) is recorded in compaction summaries as confirmed results, propagating false positives across sessions and model versions without re-verification. The underlying mechanism is a conflation of observation and persistence, where information that appeared in the terminal is treated as equivalent to information written to durable storage. This finding extends the analysis of LLM self-evaluation failures reported in prior work on non-determinism in LLM-as-judge grading by showing that agentic tools exhibit analogous reliability deficits when reporting on their own operational outcomes. The failure has direct implications for any workflow that relies on agentic session continuity for data processing, scientific computation, or multi-step automation.

</details>


### [201] [Inference Economics of Enterprise Coding Agents: A Case Study of Cloud vs. On-Premise LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13080)
*Sheng-Wei Peng,Yi-Hsun Lin,Yi-Pei Lee*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A longitudinal case study compares API-based (Claude Opus/Claude Code) vs. on-premise quantized (GLM-5.1/5.2/Opencode on Blackwell NVFP4) coding agents over two 28-day periods. Prompt caching (99.3% hit) reduced API cost to $0.57/M tokens, below the $2.83 amortized cost of shared on-premise GPU. However, the on-premise setup had a much higher defect-repair burden (FCR 74.9% vs 45.9%; OR 3.61). Under shared allocation, on-premise saved 40.1% TCO but degraded developer experience (more debugging spirals, slower commits). Hybrid routing trades defect rate for savings along a cost-quality frontier rather than dominating the API baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Engineering organizations face a trade-off between API-based frontier models (strong reasoning, high token cost) and on-premise quantized open-weight models (low-marginal-cost scaling, data sovereignty, but weaker reasoning). The paper aims to empirically quantify this trade-off in a realistic production setting.

Method: Single-developer, non-randomized longitudinal case study over two contiguous 28-day periods on a production monorepo: API-based Claude Opus 4.7/4.8 via Claude Code vs. on-premise GLM-5.1/5.2 via Opencode quantized to NVFP4 on NVIDIA Blackwell hardware. Analysis used LLM telemetry and Git history.

Result: Prompt caching (99.3% hit) cut realized API cost by 88.6% to $0.57/M tokens, below the $2.83 amortized unit cost of the shared on-premise slice. On-premise had Fix Commit Ratio of 74.9% vs 45.9% (Mantel-Haenszel OR = 3.61 across difficulty tiers). Under Taiwan-market parameters and shared GPU allocation, on-premise saved 40.1% true TCO; dedicated reservation cost 43.8% more than cached API. On-premise also showed worse developer experience (more debugging, slower commits). Hybrid routing gateways trade off defect rate for infrastructure savings along a cost-quality frontier.

Conclusion: The cost advantage of on-premise deployment depends critically on sharing GPU utilization; the true penalty under shared allocation is not monetary but a developer-experience burden. Hybrid routing cannot dominate the pure-API baseline — it trades defect rate for infrastructure savings along a Pareto frontier. Prompt caching can make API-based solutions more cost-effective than expected.

Abstract: Autonomous coding agents force engineering organizations to choose between API-based frontier models -- strong reasoning at high token cost -- and on-premise quantized open-weights models, which promise low-marginal-cost scaling and data sovereignty at some loss of reasoning fidelity. We study this trade-off through a single-developer, non-randomized longitudinal case study over two contiguous 28-day periods on a production monorepo: an API-based Claude Opus 4.7/4.8 configuration using Claude Code versus an on-premise GLM-5.1/5.2 configuration using Opencode, quantized to NVFP4, on NVIDIA Blackwell hardware. Analyzing LLM telemetry and Git history, we find that prompt caching (99.3% hit rate) cuts realized API cost by 88.6% to an effective \$0.57 per million tokens -- below even the \$2.83 amortized unit cost of the shared on-premise slice (a utilization-dependent inversion; total realized spend and total cost of ownership (TCO) are the robust quantities). At comparable gross code churn, the local configuration was associated with a far higher defect-repair burden: a Fix Commit Ratio (FCR) of 74.9% versus 45.9%, with the odds of a commit being a repair 2.6 to 4.9 times higher within every difficulty tier (Mantel-Haenszel OR = 3.61). Under Taiwan-market parameters and a symmetric labor model, on-premise deployment nonetheless saves 40.1% of true TCO under shared GPU allocation, whereas dedicated reservation costs 43.8% more than the cached API. Under shared allocation, the genuine penalty is not monetary but a measurable developer-experience burden -- timestamp indicators show more work trapped in debugging spirals and a slower commit cadence -- and an offline replay shows hybrid routing gateways trade defect rate for infrastructure savings along a cost-quality frontier rather than dominate the pure-API baseline.
